## How to read this draft

Normal text is the protocol. Italic text is commentary: reasons, examples and plain summaries. A summary that starts *In short* repeats what the rule says; where the two seem to differ, the rule's own text decides.

Each rule shows its number in draft 10 beside its new number: "Rule 14 (was 15b)". The table at the end maps every old number to its new one. Code comments, tests and records keep citing the old numbers until the freeze-ready draft; the table is how to find them here.

Some marks recur, and each matters to the next review:

- *Client conformance:* a duty of clients (wallets, the collective's client, a split service's software). The protocol cannot check it from acts alone, so it is stated for builders.
- *Stated cost:* a weakness the rules accept knowingly, written down so nobody mistakes it for an oversight.
- *Reading, to confirm:* a reading taken where the text was silent, still to be confirmed by Nobody, allegedly.
- *Mechanic, the build's* (or the project lead's): a mechanical choice taken under Nobody, allegedly's delegation of mechanics, not a decision of principle.
- **New in draft 11 (Fxxx):** a statement this draft adds, from a decided finding. **Reading (Qn):** a reading taken on one of the questions this draft's second part raised (`docs/agreements-draft-11.md`), recorded in the findings log. *To build:* the code does not do it yet; until it does, the code does what draft 10 says.
- *Open (core v22, Qn):* a point the second pass of core v22 leaves open, put to Nobody, allegedly, as a question (`docs/core-v22.md`, "Second pass").
- *Text only, no rule changed:* a slip in the text corrected to match what was already decided.

*Working rule (F265):* where the core gives participants the means to choose, the choice is theirs; the core keeps only what no choice may break.

The MIR has five parts. Part one is agreements as they run most of the time: making and signing, works and stakes, selling, splits, judges, keepers, changing a deal, defaults and the public domain. Part two is collectives: an agreement with its own identity, its tiers, members, grants, succession and abandonment. Part three is the rare cases: forks of records, how a collective ends, a broken collective, and how "before" and "after" are read in a collective. Part four is the act formats, for reference. Part five is the tasks, the reasoning and the freeze scenarios.

## Purpose

Agreements is the language of verifiable agreements between identities. It defines:

- how an agreement is proposed, signed, recorded and changed (by cloning, never by editing);
- keepers: the notaries that record deals as they happen, so that a rotation cannot erase what someone relied on;
- stakes in works and publications: how they are bound, transferred and released;
- splits: how money that reached the owners' payee pointer is divided, exactly, including module fees and shares assigned to roles;
- offers accepted by payment, conditions, and time;
- grants and collectives: acting for others, within limits;
- abandonment, and what happens when someone disappears from a deal;
- non-performance: the core never enforces, but it makes default impossible to hide.

Money ends when money reaches a payee pointer. Agreements say who owns what, how it is shared, and who failed when something breaks.

## Dependencies

Agreements builds on Identity, Envelopes, Text and Money.

Agreements are optional per client, but all or nothing: a client implements it fully, or shows its acts as unknown (core, client conformance). A client that acts on agreements implements Agreements (F11).

No rule of a lower layer depends on Agreements. Where Agreements need a lower layer to carry something, it is a field there (the publication's `for`, Envelopes).

## Words used in this MIR

### Agreements and signing

- **Terms.** An act proposing an agreement: its parties, its text, its cMIPs, its keepers and its rules.
- **Signature.** An act by one party naming terms (or a clone, a transfer, a release) as agreed. Each act has one signer, so every agreement is its terms plus one signature act per party.
- **Agreement.** Terms together with the signatures its rules require. Its id is the id of its terms act. An agreement commits identities, not keys: a later rotation changes the pen, never the promise. A party is bound only through its own signature act.
- **Agreement chain.** The acts on one agreement: signatures, clones, records, transfers, payouts, contests. Each names, in `objects` (Envelopes), the chain and the act it follows.
- **Deal.** An agreement that founds no collective: its terms carry no key grammar. A deal exists when every party has signed it, and changes only with every party's signature (F107).
- **Position.** An agreement that confers income without ownership.
- **Standing offer.** Published terms that anyone accepts by paying.
- **Proven offer, promised offer (new in draft 11, F261).** Offers are of two classes.
  - **Proven:** its delivery leaves a proof that any verifier can check from data alone, no one's word trusted: a hash in an anchoring batch; a key that decrypts to the promised hash; a rail swap's payment proof; a deterministic computation re-run to the same hash; a bounty whose answer checks itself. A proven offer uses the access form, `[ 2, [ cMIP, params ] ]` (rule 11).
  - **Promised:** its delivery rests on someone's word, so it names a vow, and each sale is confirmed or contested by those who signed onto it (rule 13, F240). A service's signed word alone (counts, checks, quality) and anything ongoing (hosting over time) stay promised.
  - A cMIP may produce the proof; **the verifier checks the proof itself, never the cMIP's say-so.**
- **Negotiation message.** An Agreements act carrying text, by which two sides negotiate (type 18, rule 52, F118). Each message names the previous message of its thread, and acknowledges the latest one received from the other side. Like every Agreements act it may carry acknowledgements (Envelopes rule 4a, F110); a text act never can.

### Works, stakes and selling

- **Work claim.** An act binding a work's hash to its creators.
- **Stake.** A share in a work or a publication. An agreement defines it, and it is identified by that agreement and its index in the agreement's stakes list. The shares in each always add up to 100%. The current holder of a stake is whoever the transfer chain on the agreement names.
  A stake whose object is the collective whose agreement defines it is a share of all that collective's income, whoever earns it and whenever (F121, Q8). It decides what every holder is paid, member or departed (F124, N5).
  **A stake whose object is a vow is held by the vow's signer: a deal made on a vow has an owner** (new in draft 13, F284; rule 14).
- **Purchase.** A payment for a work under an Agreements claim, naming the claim it pays under: the claiming agreement, and the line at which the payer's client read it current (Money receipt and claim field 9, rule 11, F126).
  - On a request rail, the claim it names is the one the seller's request committed to.
  - On a push rail, each holder the claim names settles it on its own chain (rule 12, F128, W4).

  Where the seller is a collective, the purchase becomes a **sale** once the collective's actions chain records it (rule 12, F127, W2).
- **Vow.** An act naming in words something to come, something MOR cannot hash or cannot hash yet: its own chain, its genesis id its name for good (Envelopes type 5, F237). Offers naming it sell it, and each sale of it is pending, confirmed or contested (rule 13, F240). **New in draft 11:** a vow covers anything MOR cannot hash, existing or to come, and every sale of a physical good or of a service names one, unless its delivery is proven (F252, as F261 reads it); it holds one or more promises, which may be bundled (F253); it names what is promised to the buyer, never the event itself (F259); two vows on one event stay separate, each referencing the event where it exists on MOR, not each other (F259, revised). *"Vow" replaces "announcement" (F238, F239, F241): Envelopes draft 7 still says "announcement" for the same act, type 5, with the same format, until its own redraft.*
- **Request rail, push rail.** A request rail is one on which the payee's side commits to each payment before it is made (the payment cMIP's commitment, which an invoice carries). A push rail is one on which the payer pays an address with no such request. The rail Module's specification says which it is (payment cMIP; F128, W4).

### Money and splits

- **Split plan.** The part of an agreement that says how incoming payments are divided: fixed shares on stakes, role shares, a maximum fee per payout where it states one (F271), and what happens to unfilled roles. Fees are their own terms field, 28, beside it (F213). **Every share paying anyone but a stake is paid off the top, as overhead; what is left goes to the stakes alone** (new in draft 12, F280).
- **Split service.** An identity that receives payments on behalf of a work's owners, divides each one according to their agreement, and pays every party out. It can be a company, a collective, a friend running software, or the owners themselves; a sole creator is simply their own split service.
  Where an agreement names one, the payee pointer of the identity its publications are made for counts, for Agreements, only if both hold (rule 16, F121, F123, F124 P2):
  - every address in it also appears in that service's own signed pointer in force;
  - every entry of its vault in force also appears in the service's own signed vault.
- **Payer-side splitting.** The other split model (F124, P2). Where an agreement names no split service, the payer's wallet, reading Agreements, pays each holder's own pointer by the stakes of the work's claim: one flow, with the holders' identities as destinations (F64).
- **Split.** A split service's signed act dividing one incoming payment into payouts (type 8, rule 20, F121 Q9). It is delivered to every holder it pays, and names each fee and who received it.
- **Payout.** One receiver's part of a split, paid as a simple Money payment with its own receipt.
- **Role share.** A share assigned to a role rather than to a named party, filled when the payment happens. For example: the reposter who led to the sale, the relay that served the file, a service the payer chose, the rail Modules the payment ran through.
  Its evidence is an act signed by a party other than the split service and the payee. **That evidence counts only when the payer the payment commits to acknowledges it in its claim** (rule 22; F184, QG3, F193, F194).
  The exception is a rail Module's role share: its evidence is the receipt or claim naming that rail Module, where the payee's own pointer or vault names it (rule 22, F119).
  A helper the owners chose in advance takes a named share instead, and needs no evidence (F194).

### Judges, keepers and time

- **Keeper.** A relay, operated by an identity, that an agreement names to record the acts around a deal as they arrive, and to sign what it recorded: a notary stamping sealed envelopes. A keeper records act ids and signers, never content. A record counts only alongside the act it names.
- **Arbitrator or verifier.** A third party named by an agreement to judge content (escrow and milestones, arbitration, regulated checks). Unlike a keeper, it receives the keys of the acts it must judge, by ordinary key delivery.
- **Judge.** Either a specification named for a judicial task (9 condition evaluation, 10 time reference, 11 anchoring), or an identity named to judge or testify: a keeper's operator, an arbitrator or verifier, the abandonment authority. *The split service is not a judge (F249, F250): its backups are attempts per payment (rule 17).*
- **Chain of judgment.** For a judge the terms name: the ones that take over from it, in order, when it answers "unknown" or does not act within its period. The chain is named in the terms and signed by everyone, so that it passes with no new signature. Each link names its period, measured on the agreement's time reference (terms field 21, rule 35, F121, Q7).
- **Time reference.** A source of time an agreement names, such as a block height. For that agreement, only it decides whether an act is before or after a point on its reference, and whether something is past its terms (Q35). *Before and after among acts done in a collective's name are judged on the collective's own sequence ("Made before, made after", F109); the time reference judges deadlines and absence.*
- **Concurrency rule.** Terms field 10, **withdrawn** (step 12b, OF20 c; never reused): a fork of records is settled by rule 90's default, the parent staying in force until a clone recorded after both lines resolves it. *Text only, no rule changed.*
- **Contest.** A signed objection to another act, by a party with standing. It changes nothing about any act's validity, status or selection; it makes disagreement visible. *"Dispute" is a different word: it is reserved for the status Identity gives an act after a rotation (F69).*
- **Standing.** Who may contest an act: a party to its agreement, a holder of a stake it affects, or a keeper, arbitrator or verifier the agreement names.

### Collectives

- **Collective.** Several identities acting together as one identity. A collective is a full identity (Identity MIR), with its own genesis, homes, signing key and chain key. Its founding agreement says how its members hold and use those keys.
- **This collective.** In a collective's own terms, null written as a stake's object or holder, or as a grant's grantor, names the collective whose agreement it is. This is the same device by which Identity lets a genesis name itself as its home's operator by null (F124, S1). Founding terms can so name a collective that does not exist yet.
- **Key grammar.** The part of a collective's founding agreement that says how its keys are held and activated, and the way to rotate that needs less than every member.
- **Tier.** In a collective's founding agreement and its clones, each field of the terms belongs to one of three tiers ("Tiers"):
  - Constitutional: who decides. The parties (membership), the change rules, the key grammar, the areas, the constitution's words, the departed members entry.
  - Judicial: who judges, and by what. The protected clauses, changed only with every member's signature (rule 67, F121).
  - Operational: everything else.

  Deals have no tiers (F103, scope; F107).
- **Protected clause.** In a collective, a clause that can move a party's stake or voice, or decide who wins a dispute. The protected clauses are: the abandonment clause, the succession plan, the keepers, the split service and its backups (field 29, F250), the time reference, the arbitrators or verifiers, the condition and anchoring cMIPs (Q3), and the chain of judgment (F121).
  Together they are the judicial tier, which changes only with every member's signature, one version for everyone (rule 67, F121). The split service and its backups are operational in kind, and protected as a stated exception, since they can move money. A deal needs no protected clauses: no clone of it completes without every party (F107).
- **Change rules.** The rules a collective's clone must meet, each covering part of the terms:
  - the **constitutional change rule** (terms field 18; every party when none was agreed);
  - the **judicial tier's rule**: every party whose voice remains, fixed by this MIR and never by the terms (rule 67, F121);
  - the **clone rule** (field 5), for operational matters outside every area;
  - the **power** over each area.

  In a deal, the signing rule and the clone rule are every party.
- **Power.** The right to bring a change into force: the constitutional change rule, the judicial tier's rule, the clone rule, or an area's holders meeting its threshold.
- **Mark.** A clone's terms field 4: which of the parent's change rules it claims to meet, and the parties whose signatures meet it (F104).
- **Area.** A part of a collective's operational tier that the constitution gives to some of its members, the area's **holders**. An area covers acts of the collective, by whole layer or by act type, and operational fields of its terms.
  - Its holders alone decide there, and may grant within it (Q5).
  - Its holders, and the rule they act by, change only by a constitutional change; a holder may step down from it at once (rule 71, Q13).
  - An area with no holder whose voice remains stands frozen; a clone may list none at all (Q21).

  An area reaching a whole layer is also called that layer's **lane**: lanes and areas are one construct (Q10). A lane also decides which cMIPs the collective adopts for that layer's tasks, and the Development lane decides which extensions it adopts (F106).
- **Layer.** One of MOR's layers (core, "Structure"): Identity; Envelopes and Text; Money; Agreements; Development. Which layer an act belongs to depends on its `spec`:
  - An act whose `spec` is a MIR belongs to that MIR's layer.
  - An act whose `spec` is a cMIP belongs to the layer of each task that the collective's agreement in force (rule 72) names that cMIP for (terms field 2). A task's layer is that of the MIR defining it (Development, table of tasks).
  - An act whose `spec` is an extension the agreement names (field 15) belongs to Development (F106).
  - An act whose `spec` is a specification the agreement in force names neither for a task nor as an extension belongs to no layer. In a collective with areas it counts for nothing, unless an area reaches it by a kind naming its type (rule 60, Q16).

  Development defines no tasks and no act types of its own: specifications and releases are Envelopes publications.
- **Extension.** A cMIP an agreement names outside the listed tasks (field 15), which adds rules and never relaxes the core's (Development). Its specification declares the layers it acts on (Development, specification field 10, Q18); its own acts belong to Development.
- **Voice.** A party's say in an agreement: it counts toward the agreement's change rules and, in a collective, toward the areas it holds.
  - A party loses its voice only by its own resignation (rule 70), by an abandonment declaration (rule 89), or by a constitutional clone that removes it (rule 69).
  - It loses its voice in one area by stepping down from it (rule 71), or by a constitutional clone that replaces it there (Q13).
  - It never loses its stake that way (Q6).

  In a collective, a resignation, a stepping down and a declaration take effect at the collective's line registering them ("Made before, made after", F109).
- **Departed holder.** An identity that left a collective, by resigning or by being removed, and kept a stake in it. Its stake is in the collective's stakes (field 7), which decide what it is paid. The collective's **departed members entry** records only that the identity is departed, without voice (terms field 22, rule 68, F121, F124 N5).

### A collective's acts, in order

- **Record.** The collective's everyday line (type 17, rule 72, F106, F109). It is the act by which the collective:
  - writes a complete clone of its agreement in its own sequence, naming the signature acts that complete it, so that the collective's later acts are judged under it;
  - registers its members' departures and rotations.
- **Line.** An act of a collective, in its own sequence, at which an event that changes who may act for it takes effect for its acts. A line is a record (type 17) or a rotation of the collective, each naming the latest act of every other sequence the collective keeps. What was done in the collective's name counts as made before or after a line by its place in the collective's own sequence, that is, by whether the history the line cites holds it ("Made before, made after", F109, F127).
- **Decisions chain.** A collective's decisions, in order (F127):
  - its genesis (founding);
  - its rotations (members joining or leaving, every constitutional change);
  - its records (its other clones, the release rule among them, and the departures they register);
  - its grants and revocations (F128);
  - its fork or closing.

  Each decision cites the previous decision and the action head it saw:
  - a record, by its own sequence and the tips it names;
  - a rotation, by its kept tips (Identity);
  - a grant or revocation, by its own sequence and its citations in `objects`, as an action cites (reading, F128);
  - a fork or closing, by its chain act and tips.

  Records, rotations, forks and closings are the lines.
- **Actions chain.** Every other act in a collective's name (F127): debts, payments, publications, releases, sales, the acts signed with a grant key (F128). Each cites, in `objects`, on the collective's chain (the collective's identity as chain), the decision it acts under and the heads it joins. Its own sequence's previous act counts as cited (rule 58). Identity's own everyday acts carry no objects and are on neither chain; nor are negotiation messages, which are talk (W6, F128).
- **History.** What a decision or an action cites, transitively: the previous acts of its own sequence, the tips it names, what each act reached cites on the collective's chain, and the tips of each record reached (F127). An act is before a line when the line's history holds it.
- **Tie rule.** When a decision ending powers (a fork, a closing, a departure, a grant's revocation, a departure emptying an area, F128) and an action using them cite neither each other, the ending wins: the action is void (F127). *A counterparty protects itself by waiting until the actions chain visibly moves past its act before delivering.*
- **Done.** An act in a collective's name (signed with its key, or with a grant key of its) is done once both hold (rule 57, F126, F128):
  - it is sealed to every member of the agreement in force for it (or public);
  - it is on the chain, citing the collective's head as its signer knew it.

  Before that, even signed, it is planning, and binds no one, its signer included. Where it is held is never a condition: relays are transport (F128). This is so for records too, and for forks and closings: one rule for every act in the collective's name (W3, F127; W5, F128). *"One thing is to act, one thing is to read" (Nobody, allegedly).*

### Grants

- **Grant.** A delegation of authority, with limits, from an identity or collective to another identity. A grant never reaches beyond the power of whoever issued it. A collective's grant is a decision naming the grantee's own identity, which signs to accept it, and hands the grantee a grant key (F128). In a deal, each payee's grant to the split service is written in the deal's terms (field 14), naming "this agreement" by null (F129, H4).
- **Grant key.** A key of an identity, a person or a collective (F128, extended), scoped to one grant's area and reach. The grant names it (field 9); the grantee makes and holds it. It is like a device of the collective limited to that scope (F128). What it signs is the grantor's own act (for a collective, a strand of its actions chain), citing the grant. A revocation removes it, as a decision ending powers. Every grant key ends at a fork's line. *"In the end, it is an extra key that is tied to both the collective and the grantee's hashes" (Nobody, allegedly).* *Stated cost:* *a stolen grant key acts within its scope until revoked.*
- **This agreement.** In a payee's grant to a deal's split service, null written as the agreement concerned (grant field 2) names the deal whose terms list that grant in field 14, with the versions it clones (F129, H4). It works as null in a collective's terms names "this collective" (S1). A grant can so name a deal that does not exist yet.
- **Payee.** In a deal naming a split service, a party whose own payee pointer receives the deal's incoming money, and who grants the service a grant key for it (field 14; F129, H4).

### Ending

- **Release to the public domain.** An act ending a work's claim, at once or at a point it names on the time reference (type 5, rule 55, F121, F124 N11). After it:
  - nobody earns from the work as its owner any more;
  - its history, who made it and who owned it, stays named;
  - its content key is published, or delivered at that point by the identity the act names.
- **Fork of a collective.** The end of a collective whose members split into sides that will not agree (type 19, terms field 23, rule 91, F121, F124 N1, N4).
  - Each side first founds its successor.
  - A fork act names the successors, signed by the members on its sides with their own identities under the constitutional change rule.
  - The fork act closes the original collective in Agreements and passes its ownership to the successors.
  - Each successor names the original in "forked from", a back-link only.

  Not to be confused with a fork of records or of an agreement chain (rules 6, 72, 90), which rule 90 settles.
- **Closing.** The end of a collective that holds nothing and owes nothing: every work sold or released, every debt paid or released by its creditor. It is a closing act, signed under the constitutional change rule. After its line, the collective's keys count for nothing in Agreements (type 20, rule 91, F124 N9, F125 D5). **New in draft 11 (F262):** the selling and releasing are ordinary acts before the closing. **New in draft 11 (F265):** an absentee's money share follows the collective's own rules, and stays visible as an open obligation wherever they sent it; the closing names nothing for it.
- **A deal's end (new in draft 11, F263).** A deal has no closing act. It ends, or winds down, by a clone every party signs, stating its new conditions: where its stakes went, what is still owed, and to whom its outer agreement now pays (rules 15, 47).
- **Creditor's release.** An act by which a creditor ends an obligation owed to it without full payment, for instance against stakes or a partial payment. Only the creditor signs it (rule 92, F125). It is a Money act (Money type 4; Agreements type 21 retired) (F126). A collective creditor signs it by its Money lane alone. A release traded for future terms is also an Agreements agreement, signed by the collective's Agreements lane, which the release names. *MOR has no court and cannot force a creditor to let go: a debt ends by payment or by its creditor's word.*

## Part one: agreements as they run most of the time

### 1. Making an agreement

#### Rule 1 (was 1). Proposal and signatures

An agreement begins as terms. Each party signs with a signature act naming the terms.

Founding terms exist once every party has signed them. This holds in a deal (F107) and in a collective's founding agreement alike (Q11): their signing rule (field 4) is every party. A clone comes into force by its mark (rule 45).

**A party is bound by an agreement only through its own signature act.** A rule decides when an agreement or a clone comes into force among those who signed, never who owes. An obligation in the terms of a party who has not signed is shown as unsigned, never as open (F74).

#### Rule 2 (was 2). cMIPs

Every agreement carries the hashes of the cMIPs it uses, at most one per task. An agreement that does not is invalid.

It may also name extensions (Development). A client that does not implement all of them cannot sign it.

#### Rule 3 (was 3). Commitments of identities

An agreement names identity hashes, not keys. After a rotation, the party's new key signs for the same agreement.

#### Rule 4 (was 4a). What you sign is what you saw

*In short: before anything is signed, the client can show exactly what it says, as plain text.*

*Client conformance.* Before any terms, clone or grant is signed, the client:

- MUST be able to show its text as plain text;
- SHOULD do so by default;
- MUST show every bidirectional control visibly in that view (Text MIR, rule 5a).

#### Rule 5 (was 4). Positions

Positions are agreements that confer income without ownership.

#### Rule 6 (was 5). Agreement chains

Every act on an agreement names the chain and the act it follows.

*In short: two acts following the same act are a fork. Most forks are settled by the agreement's own rule for acting independently, or by keeping the status quo.*

Two acts naming the same predecessor are a fork. The exception: signatures naming the act they sign are parallel consents, not a fork.

A fork is settled this way:

- where the agreement lets parties act independently, rule 90 settles it (the concurrency rule, field 10, is withdrawn: text only, no rule changed);
- otherwise the status quo stands;
- save that two complete versions of one deal follow rule 47 (F186), as below.

Two complete versions of one deal:

- the version before the split is the reference;
- a new act may follow either branch;
- a tangled fork stays on the reference;
- a settlement settles it, final for everything its signers could see.

The settlement is one of:

- a version naming every tip it drops;
- or the judge of forks the reference names, or the link of its chain of judgment that took over, on a party's request (F188, F190, F192, QG4).

*The rare case, in detail.* An act on an agreement chain that is void or disputed under Identity's rotation rules confers nothing: no stake, no obligation, no consent (F57).

The one exception: a member's signature act that a collective placed before its line registering that member's rotation stays valid for the collective, as "Made before, made after" says (C5). Its status under Identity is unchanged.

### 2. Works and stakes

#### Rule 7 (was 15). Binding a work

A work is bound to its creators only by an explicit work claim:

- a sole creator, by its claim alone;
- several creators, by one creator's claim completed by signature acts from all the others, and bound only when all are present.

A publication is a neutral carrier: carrying or quoting a work, or pointing to a web resource, claims nothing.

The core records claims and their order, never legitimacy. Order is not evidence of authorship; a claim cMIP MAY carry a pre-publication commitment.

Where claims from different identities conflict, priority can only be decided where they meet: by anchoring, or by a keeper that recorded both. Otherwise they remain openly contested.

**A work claim counts only public or addressed to every creator it names** (step 12b, OF16 a). It names no roles (OF17 a).

In MOR's eyes the owners are who the recorded work claim names, with the shares its agreement gives. **Where no agreement writes shares, the default holder is the creator who opened the work claim** (F218). A client shows it "as recorded", any contest beside it, never as legitimacy.

**A manifest neither claims its parts nor is reached by their claims (new in draft 11, F254).** A manifest work (an album naming its songs) is a work of its own: its claim binds the manifest's hash, never the parts it names, and a claim on a part never reaches the manifest. The parts' owners take part in it by agreement: the manifest's agreement has the parts' agreements as parties (rule 15).

#### Rule 8 (was 13). Stake rule

Any act that changes the size of a stake requires its holder's signature.

A signature on an agreement is consent to everything it defines, including pre-authorised changes such as abandonment outcomes. Clients SHOULD make such terms prominent.

#### Rule 9 (was 15a). Stakes are written in millionths

Stakes are written in millionths, summing to 1,000,000.

*In short: when a payment does not divide exactly, the few leftover units go to the holders whose exact shares were cut the most, and ties take turns. The holders keep the chain of receipts that proves it.*

Leftover units from dividing a stake go one each to the holders whose exact shares have the largest fractional remainders.

Holders with equal remainders take turns. A tied unit goes to the tied holder that has received the fewest leftover units from this stake so far, counted along the dividing split service's own receipts for the stake. Where that count is equal, it goes to the holder whose identity hash is smallest (F165).

**The count runs along the service's receipts across every version of the deal, the same stake being the same holder's share in the same work whatever its place in the list, and a new holder starting at zero (new in draft 11, F266).**

The order in which holders are listed decides nothing (F150).

**The chain of receipts.** Each receipt of a split service carries, per stake, the running count of leftover units each holder has received, and cites the service's previous receipt for that stake. So a tie is checked from two acts.

- The count is split key 4, `[+ [ stake, [+ [ holder, count ]] ]]`: the count after this split, a holder not named counting zero.
- "Receipt" in this rule means the split act (F165, F178, F182).

A verifier that does not hold the previous receipt shows that unit as unknown, never the rest of the payment.

**The split service delivers every receipt for a stake to every holder of it, paid or not** (client conformance; F171). So each holder keeps the whole chain and checks each receipt as it arrives.

**A receipt that does not cite the latest receipt for its stake, or two receipts citing the same one, is a deviation that breaks the plan** (rule 68; F171). Then:

- the break is shown on every split involved, and each holder's client names the one that arrived second;
- after a deviation, the reference stays the last split that continued the chain, so a deviating split never becomes the baseline.

Splits made from a payer's claim are in the chain. A new holder starts keeping the chain from its first split, with a warning (F182).

*The holders build the chain's reference copy themselves: whoever has money at stake holds the record of how it was divided, and checks each new link against it, so a lie is caught at its first step and a reset or fork is shown.*

*The rare cases, in detail.*

**Where the payer divides** (a payer-side split, or a split from a payer's claim with no receipt), the payer decides each tied unit, at most one per tie (F165, F168; no hash, since the receipts do not exist when the payer divides). This is a stated cost, since a payer who is not a holder gains nothing by it.

**A fork's division** keeps ties among sides by the hash of the fork act with each side, which every member signs and any may refuse (F162). Among members counted alike, it gives leftovers to the first as listed in the agreement in force, an order every member signed (F168).

**While a deal stands forked, the turns stay per branch.** Each split is judged under its branch's latest version, and its stake's holders there take their turns along that branch's receipts.

**One numbering runs across every split the service makes under the deal, on any branch** (DQ6). Each split shows its number (split key 5), from 1, each one more than the service's split under the deal before it.

*Client conformance:* **a holder's client MUST raise the alarm when the numbers on the splits it receives skip.** Splits are then being made where it is not shown, and a gap reveals a hidden branch even to the party a bait branch was hiding from. *"One count across both branches would act as an alarm" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**A deal's split that carries no number, or a number another split of the same service under the deal carries, is a deviation that breaks the plan** (QF4; F190), as two receipts citing the same one are: shown on every split carrying it.

A gap stays the holder's alarm, not a deviation: the split missing may only not have reached that holder.

#### Rule 10 (was 14). Transfer

By default, a stake is sold or given by a transfer signed by the seller and completed by the buyer's signature. Other holders are notified, not asked.

The new holder inherits the full position. The transfers name the current holder of each stake, and a split service MUST follow them (F73).

**A sale takes effect on the seller's own line** (F217). The seller signs the transfer by a chain signature too (Identity type 16, F132), which fixes its place.

**Once the seller has signed it, a payout receipt the seller signs for that stake after it on the seller's own line is a wrong receipt, counting for nothing.** That payout is owed to the buyer, in the share sold, as the split service's open obligation (rule 30), the service settling with the seller.

*Stated cost:* between the transfer and the service's first split following it (split key 8, the service's statement, never the moment), a colluding seller and service can keep paying the seller. The buyer's protection is to anchor the transfer. Otherwise the trail shows it, and the service is liable once it is shown (F214).

*Client conformance:* a co-owner's and the buyer's client show a split paying a seller for a stake the seller has transferred, beside the transfer.

*The rare cases, in detail.*

**"After" on the seller's own line** (F223; QJ1, option b, no Identity change). Identity binds an everyday act to genesis or a rotation, never to a chain signature. So **a seller's payout receipt for a stake it transferred is after the transfer, and wrong, once it is bound to a rotation later than the transfer's chain signature**, on the seller's identity line.

**Until then, what moves the money is the service.** From its first split citing the transfer (split key 8), every payout to the seller for that stake is the service's debt to the buyer (F217, F214), receipted or not.

*Mechanic, the build's:* "first" and "from" are read on the service's split numbers under the deal (DQ6): the citing split, and every split of the service under the same lineage numbered above the lowest citing one. What is owed is what the payout gives the seller above its due once the transfer and those before it on the seller's line are followed, at most the share sold of what the split pays the stake.

*Stated cost, the window's second edge:* the window F217 accepted as legible closes at the earlier of the service's first split citing the transfer and the seller's next rotation. Within it, a colluding seller and service can keep paying the seller, the trail showing it.

**A stake sold twice is settled on the seller's own line** (F224; Fable's formats review 2.8):

- a stake's transfers are ordered on the seller's own line, by their chain signatures' places;
- each is checked against what the seller still held at that point;
- transfers that fit are all valid, so two partial sales are not a fork;
- **an over-sale confers nothing, and its payment is owed back by the seller** (money received for nothing), paid as a refund (Money rule 10a; F242, M6: stated here, not decided by Money rule 10c).

*Mechanics, the build's:* what the seller still held is its share in the version the transfer names, less each transfer of the same stake (by its object, across the lineage, FR7) placed before it on its line that fitted what was left; an over-sale before it consumes nothing. An earlier transfer counts from the seller's chain signature, whether its buyer has completed it yet or not, so a later buyer's client sees it before paying.

The payment for a transfer is a Money receipt or claim fulfilling it (field 5), or one the transfer's field 4 names. The over-sale's payment is owed back by the seller (see question QK4: a transfer cannot name by hash a payment made after it).

*Client conformance* (F224): **a buyer's client pays only once the seller's transfer is receipted by the seller's homes** (its chain signature counted on the seller's resolved line) **and checked against what the seller still holds**. Nobody polices it: it is prevented where it can be and undeniable where it cannot, a refund debt any wallet sees, with signed proof of the double sale.

### 3. Selling

#### Rule 11 (was 32). Standing offers, accepted by performance

*In short: a seller publishes a standing offer, a buyer accepts it by paying, and the deal is complete when the seller's side delivers.*

*How it runs most of the time (step 12b):* a seller publishes an offer. The offer is always public. It names:

- what it sells: publications; a vow (a promised offer); or a delivery proven under a cMIP's terms (a proven offer, the access form; new in draft 11, F261);
- its price;
- who is paid.

A buyer's client finds the latest version of the offer and pays. The seller's side delivers the content key.

*Owners publish terms; anyone accepts by paying them.*

**A lone seller's offer, with no co-owners behind it, is itself the agreement between seller and buyer** (F215). It carries its price, its words, its deadline and, if the seller sets any, its own refund terms on its own time reference. The buyer's payment accepts them and names no claim.

Co-owners' offer works differently. It:

- names their agreement (field 0);
- is signed under its rule (in a deal, every party, OF3 a);
- pays a payee its field 14 lists or, where it names no split service, the stakes (Fable's review 2.1 to 2.3).

A purchase following it names that agreement. Its refund terms are the agreement's (field 17, FR1).

An offer changes by versions on its own chain. A version selling nothing withdraws the offer. A purchase under any version counts. Two versions of one previous are the seller's fork, shown (OF4 a).

A payment the time reference places past the offer's deadline is owed back. A payment whose place is undetermined is a purchase: the seller who chose a push rail bears it (OF4).

The deal is complete when the offer's side either:

- delivers the content key; or
- for a publication whose key is public, **receipts the payment naming the offer** (step 12b, OF6 b: type 7 is retired).

That proves payment was verified and honoured.

A payment that receives neither, such as one arriving after a withdrawal, creates an obligation to refund it.

A refund owed on a payment whose payer is not named is owed to whoever signs with the key the payment committed to as its payer. They claim it by a payment claim carrying that key's signature and naming where to be paid (Money rule 10a, F113). It is never owed to whoever presents the rail proof, which others on the route may hold too. A payment that committed to no key leaves its refund unclaimable by anyone.

The obligation stays open and visible until claimed and discharged. A seller who refuses a valid claim is visibly in the wrong.

**The vault's limit protects refunds too (new in draft 11, F255).** A refund paid to the refund rail a payer's claim names counts as repaid only up to the payer's vault limit for its unit; above it, only once it reaches the payer's vault (Money draft 7, rule 17, with rule 3's limit; Money draft 6's rules 14 and 14a). A payer that declares no vault has no vault rule (Money draft 7, rule 3). *Why: a thief holding the payer's signing key during a theft window could sign a claim naming their own refund rail; "vault partly exists to take care of big IOUs" (Nobody, allegedly).* A refund owed to a bare key (Money rule 10a) has no vault, so the limit does not reach it (Money draft 7, rule 17; the reading confirmed with Money draft 7's approval, 12 October 2026). *Built (`docs/agreements-draft-11-build.md`):* the library's `repaid`. *New in draft 11 (F255), the marked pass: Money draft 7, approved, states this rule, so the pointer names it, and the line on Money draft 6 waiting for its redraft is gone.*

The offer's terms MAY say how long a refund stays claimable, on the time reference. The core sets no lapse (F80). **A refund past the terms the buyer accepted by paying is ended** (F219): the obligation closes there, the owners keep the money, and it does not block a closing. The terms are a fixed point on the time reference (terms field 17, or a lone seller's offer's own; mechanic delegated with F219). Terms written in bad faith are not refused: they stay public, for anyone to judge.

*Client conformance:* the buyer's client shows the deadline before paying.

Paid access to a live stream, which has no work hash until it ends, is sold by a standing offer, not a publication. **The class follows what is sold (new in draft 11, F264).** A seller who promises the stream sells a vow, a promised offer (rule 13; the live media cMIP): the key delivered in keeping the vow is a proof that sits in the sale as evidence (rule 13), and the buyer still confirms or contests. *A stream cut short: the key worked, the promise did not.* A seller selling only a key to what already exists, such as the recording, sells a proven offer, under the access form. *An offer selling a stream's access under the access form with no vow, as the code's tests do, sells the key only, and is read so.*

**Two classes of offers: proven and promised (new in draft 11, F261).** What an offer sells is delivered either with a proof or on someone's word.

- **A proven offer** sells a delivery that leaves a proof any verifier can check from data alone, no one's word trusted. It uses the access form, `[ 2, [ cMIP, params ] ]`, under that cMIP's terms ("A service sold under a cMIP's terms"). The form is **widened from access to bytes to any delivery proven this way**: access to bytes (a key that decrypts to the promised hash, such as a subscription's keys), anchoring (a hash in a batch), a rail swap (the payment's proof), a deterministic computation (re-run to the same hash), a bounty whose answer checks itself.
- **A promised offer** names a vow (`[ 3, vow ]`, rule 13). Each sale is confirmed or contested by those who signed onto the vow (F240). A service's signed word alone (counts, checks, quality) and anything ongoing (hosting over time) are promised.
- **A cMIP may produce the proof; the verifier checks the proof itself, never the cMIP's say-so.** A delivery the verifier cannot check from data it holds is not proven, whatever the cMIP signs.
- **The pooled anchoring service is a proven offer** and is unchanged (anchoring cMIP draft 3): the hash is in the batch or it is not.
- **Each proven offer names its checker**: the cMIP in its access form. The checker is not a task (Development draft 7, "Not a task: the proof checker of a proven offer", F268): no agreement names one by a task number, so a collective sets no checker for all its offers. A cMIP that checks proofs of delivery declares so in its specification, by Development's field 12 (new in draft 11, F268, the marked pass; to build), for discovery only: a verifier asks the checker of whatever cMIP the offer names (Development draft 7, reading confirmed, Q2).

*"Technically, it is not a work, and therefore becomes a vow. It is simply that the cMIP behind this vow can deliver its own confirmation. Core does not care how confirmation is delivered, it simply looks for one" (Nobody, allegedly); then: "I'm fine with this being a different class of offers." A proven offer needs no one's confirmation, since its proof is the confirmation; a promised one needs its signer's.*

*Built (`docs/agreements-draft-11-build.md`):* the widening changes no format. The core tells the classes apart by what an offer sells, and asks the checker of the cMIP a proven offer names, among those the verifier holds, for a proof's verdict; with none held, the delivery is not proven for it. The anchoring cMIP's existing check is the first checker (`cmips/anchoring`); no other cMIP's checker is built.

#### Rule 12 (was 32a). A purchase names the claim it pays under

*In short: a payment for a standing offer, or for a publication carrying a claimed work, is a purchase only if it names the claim it pays under (F126).*

Buying a work under an Agreements claim is not pure money, since the work's claim and offer are Agreements. So a payment is a purchase only if its receipt or claim names the claim it pays under (Money receipt and claim field 9). This holds for:

- a payment for a standing offer;
- a payment for a publication carrying a work some agreement claims.

The claim named is two things:

- the claiming agreement; and
- the line at which the payer's client read it current: a version of that agreement, or the fork, closing or release that last changed who is paid for the work.

The payer's client therefore reads the work's Agreements claim. A Money-only wallet cannot make a valid purchase.

**A payment naming no claim, or a line that carries none of that agreement's claims, is no purchase: money received for nothing, owed back to the payer as a refund** (Money rule 10a: the payer named, or whoever signs with the key the payment committed to; with neither, an open, visible debt nobody can claim).

*The claim a purchase may name is read as the current version of the claiming agreement the payment names. So every agreement holding a stake in the work is a claim a purchase may name, a sale under another shown outside the first by rule 14 (step 12b, Fable's review 6.1; Money rule 10c, F242, M2).*

*Stated here alone (F242, M2): Money carries field 9 and the payment commitment, and pays the refund (rule 10a); whether a payment is a purchase, and what is owed when it is not, is this rule's.*

**Which claim the payment buys under is settled where the payment truly takes place** (F128, W4).

*"to not make it actionable, the burden is passed to the step where the action truly takes place" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**On a request rail**, the payee's side commits to each payment before it is made (the payment cMIP's commitment, which an invoice carries). There, **the claim a purchase names is the one the seller's request committed to**, so a buyer cannot pay a version the seller has left. The payment is a purchase under it, whatever the claim became after.

**On a push rail**, the payer pays an address with no request. There, **each holder of the work's stake in the claim named settles on its own chain**:

- a receipt that holder records before its own signature on the act superseding the claim is a sale under the old claim. The superseding act is a version of the claiming agreement changing who is paid, a release, a fork or a closing;
- a receipt recorded after that signature, its own history holding it, is not.

**The payment is a purchase only if every holder's receipt is a sale; otherwise every holder refunds what it received** (paid by Money rule 10a; F242, M3), and the buyer buys again under the current claim. Until every holder has signed its receipt, the payment is **unrecorded**.

A push payment to several holders is one rail payment: their receipts carry its one rail proof (F132).

*"The clock is when each holder records, the only one MOR has, and it belongs to the one answering for it." A receipt on another of the holder's sequences than its signature is not after it (reading): each holder answers for its own devices.*

**Where the seller is a collective, a payment becomes a sale once the collective's actions chain records it** (F127, W2). It is recorded by an act that:

- is in the collective's name and counts (done, on its chain, not void at an ending);
- is signed with the collective's own key or with a grant key of its, its split service's among them (F128, reading 6, adjusted);
- is the payment's receipt, acknowledges it, or is a receipt for the same rail proof.

And the claim must match:

- on a request rail, the claim is the one the request committed to;
- on a push rail, the claim named was current as that act's agreement in force reads it, and before the collective's own release where the release is current.

Until the seller's chain records it, the payment is **unrecorded**: a sale once recorded.

*The payment is the buyer's act; the sale is the collective's, on its chain, where the fork's line can place it.* *No payment is undetermined (F128).* *"Records it" and "the claim current at an act" (readings 4 and 6, F127): the latest version, in force for it, that changed who is paid for the work.*

*The rare cases, in detail.*

*Wrong receipts.* **The payment decides** (F131, IT3). **The claim is the one the payment's commitment names** (the payment cMIP: a purchase names its claim, F126). **A receipt naming another claim is a wrong receipt, shown as such, counting for nothing**: neither a sale nor a refund. Its commitment, recomputed from it, is not the one the rail proof carries, so the rail shows it. W4 then applies to the holders' receipts naming the payment's claim, each settled on its own holder's chain.

Until a verifier has the rail's answer, receipts of one payment naming different claims are judged none of them a sale or a refund: the payment is **unrecorded** (F132).

*One payment shown as both a purchase and a refund (IT3) cannot arise: of two receipts naming different claims, at most one carries the payment's commitment.*

*Payments the chain did not record in time.* **A payment the original's chain never recorded before the fork or closing that superseded its claim is no purchase, and is refunded** (paid by Money rule 10a; F242, M4). A sale in the fork's history stays a purchase. A payment recorded concurrently with the fork falls under the tie rule: the fork wins, and it is refunded.

*Client conformance (F126):* a split service whose grant has ended issues no payment request. So on rails where the payee's side issues each request (invoices), a payment to a superseded claim cannot happen.

*Stated cost: a wallet that reads only Money can pay a claimed work's publication, but cannot tell whether it bought it: its payment is no purchase, and the money is owed back to it; it can still receive the refund (Money rule 10a; F242, M5).*

*Client conformance, a strong SHOULD (F188):* **before paying, a buyer's client finds the offer and verifies that it is truly the latest in its chain**, fetching the seller's latest version from its homes or relays, **and warns where what it shows is outdated.**

*What is already in place stands: a purchase names the version and the point at which the client read it current; on a request rail the seller commits to the version sold; on a push rail each holder settles on its own chain. Guidance, not rule: sellers prefer request rails for sales; an offer may carry its own expiry on a named time reference.*

*"The work carries a law act, why can the finance only wallet act?" (Nobody, allegedly).*

#### Rule 13 (was 32b). Vows: the state of each sale

*In short: for each sale of a vow, the core records only whether it is pending, confirmed or contested.*

*"the core only wants to know whether it is confirmed, pending, or already contested" (Nobody, allegedly; F237).*

The core keeps only the grammar:

- a vow (Envelopes type 5);
- offers naming it with their words (the offer format);
- **the state of each sale of it**: was what the offered deal promised respected.

**Each sale is pending, confirmed or contested.**

- **Confirmed:** the buyer acknowledges an act about the vow (F193's shape). For a payment, this is done in a payer's claim of that payment by the payer its commitment names (rule 22: never the payer a receipt names), its `acks` naming an act about the vow, held and valid (Envelopes rule 4).
- **Contested:** the buyer signs a contest naming the sale (type 14; new in draft 11, F253: draft 10's contest named the vow, and marked every sale of its signer contested).
- Otherwise, **pending**.

**Only someone who signed onto the vow may confirm or contest it** (F240), **at any price, including none**. They sign on:

- for a payment that is a purchase under an offer naming the vow (rule 12), by their own payer's claim;
- for an offer at no price, by a signature act naming the offer.

**A vow nobody signed onto carries no state.**

**What a vow covers (new in draft 11, F252).** A vow covers anything MOR cannot hash, existing or to come: a good in stock or to be made, a service, an hourly rate, storage, a seat. **Every sale of a physical good or of a service names a vow** (an offer's `[ 3, vow ]`), **unless its delivery is proven** (new in draft 11, F261: a proven offer, under the access form, rule 11), so each such sale is pending, confirmed or contested. One form for one task. *"MOR truly needed a structured way of selling something that has no bytes, or no bytes yet" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**A proof may sit inside a vow as evidence (new in draft 11, F261).** A promised sale may carry a proof of delivery beside the buyer's confirmation or contest: it is evidence, shown, and replaces neither. The sale's state is still the signer's confirmation or contest alone. How a proof is carried and shown is the cMIP's (F237).

**Promises, and bundles (new in draft 11, F253).** A vow holds one or more promises. A seller bundles them as it chooses, in public: a season, a series, a single match. What each promise is, and how promises are listed, is the vow's words or a cMIP's (F237).

- **A contest names the sale it contests**, never the whole vow ("Contest (type 14)"). **Naming the promise that failed is optional** (reading (Q3), as F253 says a contest "may say" which promise failed: a buyer may know only that something went wrong; field 1). One failed match marks one buyer's sale contested, not the season's every sale.
- **A confirmation likewise names the sale** it confirms. *Reading, to confirm:* a buyer's claim of a payment is that sale, so a confirmation made there names it; for a free sale, the buyer's act acknowledges both the act about the vow and its own signature act on the offer.
- **What a buyer is owed back when part of a bundle fails is set by the bundle's terms**: the offer's words, its agreement's, or a cMIP's. The core fixes no partial refund.

*Built (`docs/agreements-draft-11-build.md`):* the contest naming the sale, and the per-sale reading, replacing draft 10's V-R9.

**A vow names what is promised to the buyer, never the event itself (new in draft 11, F259).** A place in the stand to watch a match is one vow; access to a stream of the match is another; neither is "the match". **Two vows on one event stay separate** (new in draft 11, F259, revised): neither references the other. This is how they are made, not a check a verifier runs: a vow naming another stays valid (below). **Each may reference the event, where the event exists on MOR as an act of its own** (in its `refs`, Envelopes; mechanic, this draft's, to confirm), never as a vow, since a vow names what is promised. A reference carries no claim, no stake and no marking under rule 14. Each vow sits under its own agreement; whether an agreement claims a vow, and how its rights are managed, is its signer's business. *"None are the football game, both need their own agreement"; "Both the seat and the stream reference the football game, if that was to ever exist on MOR" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**A vow's reference to another vow is not an act about it** (reading (Q2)). Any vow may still name another in its `refs`; that reference moves none of the other vow's sales' states: acknowledging the referring vow confirms no sale of the vow it names. *Built (`docs/agreements-draft-11-build.md`):* another vow naming the vow in `refs` is no act about it.

*"Only a person who signed to use the vow should be able to contest it" (Nobody, allegedly).*

*Mechanics, the vow grammar build, each to confirm:*

- "an act about the vow" is one of its versions, or a valid act naming its name in `objects` or `refs`, except another vow naming it in `refs` (reading (Q2), above);
- a contest prevails over a confirmation and voids nothing;
- a free sale is confirmed by any valid act of the buyer's acknowledging such an act;
- a sign-on by signature is by anyone other than the offer's own side (its signer, its payee, the parties of its agreement);
- a payment whose buyer signed nothing the verifier holds, or committed only a bare key, is a purchase nobody signed onto, listed, with no state (F193's stated cost).

**Everything about the substance is the cMIPs'** (F237):

- what is claimed or delivered and how it is confirmed;
- what a vow became and how claims reach it (an ordinary work claim, dated by itself under rule 7, so nothing is backdated);
- quantities and seats;
- delivery points;
- kinds;
- and streams (the live media cMIP draft 1).

*"By keeping the grammar lean, the same tools can be used for concerts, sports events, live streams, gigs,… the cMIPs used by the sub fork will decide" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**Rule 7 is unchanged and speaks of works only:** a claim on a vow's name covers nothing it became.

#### Rule 14 (was 15b). A sale is tied to the seller's agreement, not to the work's stakes

The core cannot tie a sale of a work to that work's stakes. It ties a sale to the agreement of whoever sells, and makes a sale outside the claiming agreement visible to those it concerns.

A publication or standing offer for a work with an existing claim, not signed under that claim's agreement, is a valid act that visibly contradicts a signed agreement:

- the claim's holders contest it;
- a keeper has the claim on record;
- the seller's own split shows what they took.

*The bits and bolts are all in the core: work claims with order, contests with standing, keeper records, the published split, the publication's `for` field, and the payer's claim. A client that refuses to sell a claimed work under a plan that does not pay the claim's holders, or a reputation service that reads contests, can be built on them, and adoption decides (F72).*

*Client conformance.* A client SHOULD show such a publication or offer as outside the claiming agreement.

**A vow's name is read as a work's** (F247, QV1, option a). A standing offer naming a vow (`[ 3, vow ]`, rule 13) in which an agreement writes a stake, not signed under that agreement, is the same valid act visibly contradicting it, shown as outside it. One rule for anything sold under a claim, bytes or not: a buyer sees that the seller is not the agreement claiming the vow.

*Mechanic, the build's:* "signed under that claim's agreement" is read as made under a version of that agreement's line (the offer's field 0). A lone seller's offer is under none. Whether every party of a deal signed the offer stays OF3 (a)'s.

**A deal made on a vow has an owner: the vow's signer, who holds the stake** (new in draft 13, F284). A stake an agreement writes in a vow, its object the vow's name, is held by the vow's signer; in a collective's terms, the collective's own vow is held by the collective, written null (S1). The overhead (a platform's fee, a helper's share, a fee) comes off the top (rule 22, F280), and what is left is the owner's. A version that pays the owner less needs the owner's signature where the owner has no voice in the power making it (rule 48, F270): in a deal the owner who is a party signs every version anyway (rule 47). Terms writing a stake in a vow held otherwise are invalid, signed by every party or not.

*"The deal in question is based on a vow. Somebody owns the vow" (Nobody, allegedly).* *A worker's vow, a job booked through a platform: the job's agreement writes the stake in her vow, held by her; the platform's fee comes off the top; what is left is hers, and the platform raising its fee needs her signature.*

- *Reading, to confirm (`docs/f282-f284-build.md`):* the vow's signer holds the whole stake in the version that first writes it (the founding terms, or a clone whose parent writes no stake in that vow). From then on the stake moves as any stake moves: by its holder's transfer (rule 10), by succession (rule 89), or by a version its holder signs (rule 48), so the owner may sell or share what she owns.
- *Reading, to confirm (`docs/f282-f284-build.md`, Q1): a deal silent on the vow it sells.* **An offer made under an agreement and naming a vow counts only where the version it is made under writes a stake in that vow**; a collective's offer of its own vow needs none, its income going to the collective's own stakes (rule 68). A lone seller's offer is under no agreement: selling her own vow, the seller is its payee (F215).
- *Mechanic, the build's:* the vow's signer is read from the vow the verifier holds, by its name (its genesis). A verifier that does not hold the vow cannot tell its name from a work's hash, and judges neither check, as for an offer's `[ 3, vow ]` ("Standing offer (type 6)").

*Budgets are not vows (F284): the vote that decides where a common budget goes is a cMIP's; the projects answering the need make vows, each a deal with its owner, which the budget pays. No core text.*

**After the fork of a collective, the original's stake passes to the successors in the shares the fork act gives them (rule 91, F121): a successor's sale is under the claiming agreement, as a holder of that stake, never outside it.**

*Stated cost (new in draft 11, F258):* a resale under a fresh vow escapes this marking. A holder of a claimed piece who signs a fresh vow for the same object and sells under it is marked by nothing here: this rule reads only offers naming the claimed vow, and the core cannot tell that two vows describe one object, since the object is what MOR cannot hash (F237, F252). Catching it is the provenance cMIP's, with registries and reputation. The point of cheating stays easy to find (F256): it is the fresh vow, signed by the reseller. *"We cannot police this" (Nobody, allegedly).*

#### Rule 15 (was 16). A publication carries two agreements

A publication carries two agreements (F216): the publication's own, and the work's.

*How it runs most of the time:* the owners sell through their own publication, and only the work's agreement divides.

Where a publisher carries the work:

- **the publication's agreement** sets the terms of use and divides the publication's income among its participants, a publisher's share among them;
- **the work's agreement** is one participant of it, consenting by its own rule (in a deal, every party signing the publication's agreement), and divides whatever reaches it.

A sale through the publication passes outside in. The publication's split gives the work's agreement its line, which nobody on the publication's side changes alone (rule 27 at each envelope). Then the work's split divides it.

A publisher holds a stake in the publication's agreement, never in the work, by construction. No stake is marked as a publisher's. Recoupment and every other shape are the publication's split cMIP's.

*Mechanics, the build's (replaced by F254 below, now built):* the work's agreement is named by its version's hash, as a party and as a holder of the publication's stake. Its line passes on to its stake in the work the publication carries (payer-side splitting as built).

**Money passes layer by layer** (F235, QK3, option c generalised): a pool (a subscription service's, splitting by its stated rules; freeze scenario 7), then each publication's agreement, then the work's agreement, as many layers as the agreements chain. *"Money is split envelope by envelope, deal by deal, and can involve more participants than simply the work and the publisher, to reward the pipe" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**The receiver of a layer's share is the one pointer the next layer's agreement names for money arriving from above** (F244, QL1). The layer above never divides the next layer's share. Behind that pointer is the next agreement's own business:

- a lone owner's payee pointer (nothing to split);
- a split service;
- or an owner acting as treasury for the joint venture.

*"Either use a split service or have someone act as a treasury for the joint venture. I'd pick the first." (Nobody, allegedly).*

**The work's agreement designates the one pointer at which it accepts payments, the same whoever pays** (F248, QV2): a buyer directly, or a layer above. The publisher points there and divides nothing. Its obligation for the work's share is discharged once that share reaches the designated pointer (Money's receipt shows it). What follows is the work's agreement's alone. *"Work Agreement states pointer, publishing agreement states cut. Publisher delivers cut and its responsibility ends there." (Nobody, allegedly).*

*The rare mechanics, in detail.*

*Mechanic, the build's, reusing what the terms already write:* **the designation is field 14's one grantee**, read from the holding agreement's version in force:

- in a deal, every grant field 14 lists names the same service, or the deal carries none of them ("A payee's grant in a deal", F129 reading 3);
- in a collective, its one grant;
- an owner acting as treasury being an owner so named.

**Where it names no split service, a deal of one party holding every stake it writes designates that party's own payee pointer**, the one pointer its terms can mean (F244's lone owner).

**A backup of the split cMIP's fields (field 29) receives a payment only once the attempt before it failed for that payment, as the cMIP defines failure. The next payment starts again from the designated pointer** (F249, QW1; F250; rule 17). *"This is not a line of judgement. It is truly a back up if users wish to. It acts if the previous one fails" (Nobody, allegedly).* So the designated pointer is always the first. A layer's obligation for the share ends when it reaches the first service in order whose attempt succeeds, Money's receipt the proof.

**An agreement cannot be paid through a layer until it designates one pointer.** This covers:

- an agreement designating none (several owners and no split service: payer-side splitting, rule 16);
- an agreement naming several split services without designating one (field 14's grants naming different services, which the deal then does not carry).

A verifier reads a payout to the designated pointer as paid to that agreement's holding. It reads a payout to anyone else on that holding as a mismatch (rule 27): an owner it does not designate, or a backup whose earlier attempts did not fail for that payment.

Who receipts the incoming share depends on the rail:

- **where the pointer is a split service and the rail pays the service itself** (Lightning to its own node), **that service receipts the share in its own name**, never with a grant key, its receipt naming the split as Money's routes chain receipts (rule 23);
- **where the rail pays an account an owner holds** (a bank transfer to the owner), **the owner may grant the service to sign for that incoming share on its behalf, the rail's proof required on that receipt** (F235; "What a split service's grant key may sign").

Either way the service **splits it under its own agreement** (rules 20, 30: until a split of its names that receipt, it is the service's open obligation). A lone owner receipts its share as any payee does, with nothing to split.

Each layer may pay more participants than the work and the publisher (relays and other pipes, by role shares, rule 22, F193, F194), as its agreement says.

**H5 and H7 stand** everywhere else.

*Mechanic, the layers-and-judges build:* a deal that is a party of another deal signs it where every party of its version in force signs it, a party that is itself a deal signing the same way, at most eight layers deep.

**Any agreement may be a party to another (new in draft 11, F254).** The two agreements of a publication are one case of a general rule. *"The way publishing is structured a work agreement needs it" (Nobody, allegedly).*

- **Any agreement may be a party of any agreement**, consenting by its own rule. A collective signs with its own keys. A deal, having none, consents when its own parties sign under its own rule: every party (rule 47).
- **As a party it holds stakes**, paid at the one pointer it designates (F248).
- **It is named by its line**: its founding version, never a single later version. Every later version of the inner agreement carries what it signed outward, and cannot escape it by re-versioning. Money goes to the pointer the inner agreement's version in force designates. *"What happens in that envelope is separated from the publishing deal" (Nobody, allegedly).*
- **No loops.** An agreement is never a party of itself, directly or through others: terms making one so are refused, failing closed.
- **Nesting is bounded at 8 layers**, a freeze parameter ("Open technical parameters").
- **When an inner agreement ends, its stakes move by ordinary acts, and what is still owed stays visible** (new in draft 11, F254 as F262 and F263 read it). F254's "liquidated per the closing" is read so:
  - **A collective is liquidated by ordinary sales and transfers before its closing** (F262): each stake sold or moved by a stake transfer (rule 10), or released (rule 55). The closing (type 20) still counts only once the collective holds nothing (rule 91). **An absentee's money share follows the collective's own rules** (new in draft 11, F265): its split plan, its absence clause, its terms, written before anyone left. What those rules could not deliver stays visible as an open obligation wherever they sent it, until a valid claim appears: never lost, never taken ("Closing (type 20)"). The closing needs no entry for it; its field 4 stays for money owed back. No new field. *Batching those acts together is a client's or a cMIP's convenience, never a second way in the core.*
  - **A deal ends, or winds down, by a clone every party signs** (F263; F107, rule 47), **stating its new conditions**: where its stakes went, what is still owed, as open obligations, and to whom its outer agreement now pays (its designated pointer, F248). What those conditions are depends on the deal. **A deal has no closing act.** A party who is absent is handled by the absence rules: in a deal, one identity judges absence (rules 85 to 89, B19).
  - Either way, the outer agreement keeps paying the one pointer the inner agreement's version in force designates, its own terms unchanged.
  *"I think B is cleaner, A is the act of batching these decisions which also works, but not always the cleanest option"; "closing the deal is simply creating a clone that states the new conditions. It really depends on the kind of agreement" (Nobody, allegedly).*
- **A manifest neither claims its parts nor is reached by their claims** (rule 7).

*Built (`docs/agreements-draft-11-build.md`):* naming by line, the loop refusal and the bound as a checked parameter. A deal's last clone needs nothing new: it is an ordinary clone of a deal.

**Responsibility ends at the designated pointer, in every chain of agreements (new in draft 11, F256).** Any agreement paying another agreement pays the pointer that agreement designates, and its responsibility for that payment ends there, Money's receipt the proof: a publisher paying a work, an agency paying a production company paying its crew, a compilation paying its parts. What follows is the receiving agreement's alone. *"The idea of the network is to segment everything so that the point of failure or cheating is easily found" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**A compilation is a layer with its parts' consent, a derivative without it (new in draft 11, F257).** With the parts' agreements as its parties, a compilation (a course of lessons by different teachers, an anthology) is a layer: it pays each part's designated pointer, and its responsibility ends there. Without their consent it is a derivative: its own work, paying its sources by its own agreement, its published split showing whether it honours them.

### 4. Splits

How a split service runs is defined by the split cMIP its owners' agreement names. Agreements keep only the vocabulary and the guarantees below.

*In short: who names the split service, what it may sign, and what it is handed.*

#### Rule 16 (was 18). Named by grant key

The owners' agreement names the split service through a grant, with any limits the owners choose (for example how long it may hold money, or which rails it may use), handing the service a grant key (F128).

The grant:

- **in a collective, one grant of the collective** (field 14, one hash). A collective's field 14 stays one hash, its one payee being the collective (reading 5, F130);
- **in a deal, one grant per payee**, each naming "this agreement" by null and signed by the payee, the service holding one grant key per payee (field 14, a list; "Grant (type 9)", F129, H4).

**Every split service's grant key, in a deal or a collective, signs only receipts for money coming in under the grantor's own claims and offers.** It never signs one whose payer is the service, nor a payout the grantor is owed ("What a split service's grant key may sign", F129 H5, F130 H7). The one exception: a layer's incoming share on the owner's behalf, with the rail's proof behind it (F235, rule 15).

The grant is revocable at any time under the agreement's rule. In a deal, each payee revokes its own.

**Two models, chosen per collective** (F124, P2):

- a split service: two flows (payer to owners' pointer, service to holders);
- or payer-side splitting: one flow, the payer's wallet paying each holder's own pointer by the stakes of the work's claim, with no intermediary to bypass, each holder's own vault protecting its share.

The model is chosen by naming a split service (field 14) or not (F125, D3); no field says which.

The pointer check, in detail. **Where an agreement names a split service, the payee pointer of the identity its publications are made for counts, for Agreements, only if:**

- every address in it also appears in the split service's own signed pointer in force (F121, F123);
- and every entry of its vault in force appears in the service's own signed vault (F124, P2);
- or both appear in those of a backup the split cMIP's fields name (field 29; reading 4, F250).

In each case one service carries every address and every vault entry. The identity its publications are made for is the owners' collective, the sole owner, or, in a deal, each payee whose own grant field 14 lists (F129).

An address is a rail, a rail Module and the address bytes it reads (Money, payee pointer). That is how money reaches the service (Money, F46, F68).

*Client conformance:* an Agreements client, and any wallet reading Agreements, checks the pointer before paying, and shows a pointer that bypasses the split service as such, never as an ordinary pointer.

*Stated cost (F121, as F68):* a wallet that reads only Money cannot check it, and pays the pointer as it stands; payers who want the protection use wallets that read Agreements.

*"Make it legible" (Nobody, allegedly, F123): a bypass needs the service to sign, publicly, for an address that is not its own: collusion in plain view. No format changes. Set aside: a "receiver" field (anyone can write any name next to their own address); a pointer delegating to another identity's pointer (cleanest, but a change to Money and every wallet); making every pointer change need every stake holder (every new rail would need everyone); sending every buyer's claim to every owner (it reveals buyers).*

*Stated cost, for payer-side splitting (P2): several payments, partial payment if one fails, owners' pointers visible to the buyer, and a wallet that reads Agreements.*

#### Rule 17 (was 18a). The split task, handed to the split cMIP the agreement names

The split task is handed to the split cMIP the agreement names (F250, F249).

*How it runs most of the time:* one split service, field 14's, receives every payment.

**The split service, its backups in order and what counts as a failed attempt are fields of the split cMIP**, signed with the terms (field 14, then field 29).

**Attempts run per payment, not by stages:**

- each payment is tried at the first service (the designated pointer, F248);
- only if that attempt fails, as the cMIP defines failure, is the next tried, and so on;
- **the next payment starts again from the first.**

*"A judge chain has stages. A back up chain has attempts if previous one fails. Everything reverts back if attempts 1 succeeds." (Nobody, allegedly).*
