A payer's obligation ends when the payment reaches the first service in order whose attempt succeeds, Money's receipt the proof.

**Every payer tries the services in order (new in draft 11, F260).** A buyer paying the owners directly follows the same rule as a publisher or a layer above: it tries the services the agreement names, field 14's then field 29's, in order, attempt by attempt, each payment starting again from the first. **A backup paid directly receipts in the owners' name, with the grants it already holds** (field 29's, one per payee in a deal; "What a split service's grant key may sign"). Money's pointer format is unchanged: the owners' payee pointer leads to the first service, and a backup's address is the one its own signed pointer carries, which the pointer check accepts (rule 16). *Built (`docs/agreements-draft-11-build.md`):* rules 15 and 17 applied to a direct buyer's payment, and the participant's check (rule 18) following it.

**The task:** the cMIP accepts the agreement and the attempts made for a payment, and produces the receiver for this payment.

**The guarantee, the core's: money goes only to an address the owners signed.**

- A cMIP chooses among the services the signed terms name (field 14's and field 29's), never invents one.
- A payout to anyone else on the agreement's holding is a mismatch (rule 27), whatever any cMIP answers.
- Where every attempt fails, the money stays where it is, an open obligation of whoever holds it (rules 24, 30).

*What counts as failure depends on rails and services, so it is the cMIP's arena (F249). A verifier that does not hold the cMIP, or its answer for a payment, reads only the designated pointer as paid on the holding, as a client not holding a judge's specification shows its question as unknown (rule 35): it fails closed.*

*"Core stays simple. We've defined the 'assets', the rules are mostly the same for all of them, how the messages travel varies" (Nobody, allegedly).*

#### Rule 18 (was 18b). Each participant's check

Once a purchase triggers the agreement, each participant checks: **is my cut at my flow address, or at my vault where needed?** (F250)

*"Core says, agreement condition triggered (purchase) where is my cut at my flow address (or vault if needed)." (Nobody, allegedly).*

Its cut is what the splits that follow the purchase assign it, layer by layer (rule 15):

- the payment itself, where it is the payee (payer-side splitting, or a lone owner);
- otherwise, its payouts in each split naming the payment, signed by a service the agreement names, field 14's or a backup's, whoever paid, a direct buyer included (new in draft 11, F260; built).

It has arrived when its own receipts, valid, each with the rail's answer, place that much at its payee pointer or at an entry of its vault (Money rules 12, 14a).

Where the trail stops short (no split yet, a split that breaks the plan, a payout with no receipt of the participant), the check says where, and the shortfall is the service's open obligation (rule 30).

#### Rule 19 (was 19). What it receives

The split service receives:

- the incoming payment (an ordinary Money payment, for which it signs the receipt);
- any payer's claim for it;
- the split plan from the agreement;
- the evidence for role shares: the referral the payer signed in its claim, and any act offered as a helper's evidence (a relay's delivery record, a service's use record) once the claim of the payer the payment commits to acknowledges it (F184; QG3, F193, F194);
- and, for a rail Module, the receipt or claim naming it (F116, F119).

*A Module is a specification: it signs nothing (F112, F116).*

*In short: what the split service must do with every payment it receives.*

#### Rule 20 (was 20). Publish a split

For every incoming receipt, and for every payer's claim that shows money arrived without one, the service publishes a split. The split is signed with its own key, names that receipt or claim, and lists every payout.

**It delivers the split to every holder it pays, departed or not, and names each fee and who received it** (F121, Q9; "Split plan and split"). A split not delivered to a holder it pays is shown so.

#### Rule 21 (was 21). Conservation

A split's payouts MUST sum exactly to the amount received, in the unit's smallest part. **A split that does not sum exactly is invalid.**

Under the default rule, every leftover unit goes exactly where rule 9 sends it, so a unit sent elsewhere is a deviation (F175).

The split cMIP declares its remainder rule; where it declares none, leftovers follow rule 9. A declared remainder rule MUST NOT depend on the order in which holders are listed (F150).

#### Rule 22 (was 22). Role shares with evidence

**Every share paying anyone but a stake, a role share, a named receiver or a metric share, is paid off the top only, as overhead; what is left goes to the stakes alone, and within each stake by its holders' shares. A plan putting a non-owner in the rest is invalid** (new in draft 12, F280). *"I think subtle ways to dilute should be stopped. Shared ownership means shared fruits" (Nobody, allegedly). Weights between several stakes stay a business choice.*

*In short: a role share pays a helper known only at payment time, and, save a rail Module's, it counts only when the payer's own claim vouches for the helper.*

*How it runs most of the time:* the owners name in advance the helpers they pay (a host, a mastering service), by a named share in the plan, and no evidence is needed. A role share pays for help known only at payment time, and **its evidence counts only when the payer the payment commits to acknowledges it**, in its claim for the payment: the payer chose the helper, and the payer's own signature says so.

Each role payout names who filled the role and the proof. The proof MUST be an act signed by someone other than the split service and the payee.

The evidence:

- **a referral**: the referral the payer signs in its claim (Money, the claim's referral field);
- **any other act offered as a helper's evidence**, a relay's delivery record (relay transport cMIP, "Delivery records") or a service's use record (Development rule 17), whatever cMIP defines it, **counts only when the payer's claim acknowledges it** (Envelopes rule 4a: a Money act may carry acknowledgements; F184; QG3). So no verifier needs telling which specification defines it, and every verifier gives the same answer.

For a relay's delivery record, **the object it names is the one the payment was for** (F184), where the verifier reads the record's format (QH3, "Open in this draft").

One no claim acknowledges is evidence of nothing. A claim may acknowledge several, so several helpers can share one payment.

**The payer is the one the payment's own commitment names** (F193; payment cMIP: an identity, an anonymous payer's bare key, or nobody): a claim of the same rail payment whose rail proof carries the commitment recomputed from it, its signer, or the bare key it carries, as payer. **It is never the payer a receipt names**, which the split service writes. Where the commitment names nobody, or the rail binds no payer, nothing can be acknowledged, and the share is one nobody fills.

*Stated cost (F193): a payer who stays wholly anonymous, committing no key, cannot reward a relay or a service.*

**No service is paid on its own record alone** (F194):

- a service the owners chose in advance is paid by a named share;
- a service the payer chose at payment time is paid by a role share only when the payer acknowledges its use record, as for a relay;
- a service evidenced only by its own record is not paid.

A service that worked unnamed is paid by whoever chose it: the owners (by naming it in a new version), the payer (through a client that carries the choice into the payment), or the relay or service that used it (by its own agreement with it).

*Whether a client carries a user's choices into the payment is good practice for client developers, the builders' choice, legible to those who look: "pay the pipe if you wish to" (F184).*

*A helper's own signature alone would let it claim help it never gave ("paying for usage invites faking usage"); the confirming signature comes from the other side of the deal, as for a referral (F75).* *Stated cost: a payer and a helper together can acknowledge help that never happened, which the owners bound by the size of the share they offer, as for a self-referral (F184).*

A referral the service names on its own earns nothing.

*The exception: a rail Module.* **A rail Module's role share is the one exception** (F119):

- its proof is the receipt or claim naming that rail Module in its field 0 (Money rule 10b, F116);
- it counts even when the split service signs it, provided that rail Module is one the payee's own payee pointer or vault names, where the payment was paid (Money rule 12a, F115);
- the payee is the identity the payment was made to, whose pointer leads to the split service (rule 16).

A receipt or claim naming a rail Module the payee's pointer and vault never named is evidence of nothing, and counts for nothing as a receipt either (Money rule 12a).

*The service cannot invent a Module: what it may name is fixed by the payee, publicly, and the Module's own rule checks the rail's proof. Requiring a third party's signature here would leave only the payer's claim, which payers publish by choice, so most uses would go unrecorded.*

*Stated cost (F119): where the payee's pointer or vault names two rail Modules for one rail, whoever issues the invoice, here the split service, chooses which of them earns; the pointer shows it publicly, and the payee's agreement with the service can constrain it.*

Anyone can check the evidence afterwards.

A role share nobody fills follows the option the plan chose: to the owners pro rata, held open as an obligation, or to a named party (F75). **Absent a choice, it goes to the stakes, in proportion to their parts** (F218), shown before signing (Development rule 15).

**A role share is named by its role, never by a cMIP's hash** (step 12b, Fable's review 7.2): a new transport cMIP needs no clone of every plan.

**Evidence is acknowledged in the claim written at payment** (F210). *Client conformance:* a buyer's client fetches, asks the relay for its delivery record, and acknowledges it in the claim it writes at payment.

*Stated costs:* a record acknowledged after the split fills nothing. One record acknowledged by the same payer's claims for several payments counts for the first on the payer's sequence and is spent for the others (mechanic, the build's: two at one position, on two devices, leave it spent for both).

Several fillers of one role divide it equally (OF11 a).

#### Rule 23 (was 23). Pay out

Each payout is paid as a simple Money payment to the current holder of the stake, or the named receiver, with its own receipt naming the split.

**A stake held by another agreement is paid to the one pointer that agreement designates for payments** (rule 15, F244, F248). That pointer is:

- its split service, field 14's one grantee, which receipts it in its own name and splits it under that agreement (F235), an owner acting as treasury being named so;
- or a lone owner, with nothing to split;
- or, for one payment, a backup of that agreement's field 29 once the attempts before it failed (rule 17; F249, F250).

An agreement designating none cannot be paid through a layer until it designates one.

#### Rule 24 (was 24). Keep what it cannot deliver

The service keeps what it cannot deliver as open obligations, visible until paid. Amounts below a rail's minimum are held until they add up to enough (F50).

#### Rule 25 (was 24a). Payout fees

The split plan says who bears each payout's rail fee: each receiver by default, or the split service as part of its cut. **It may state a maximum fee per payout** (plan key 6); **where it states none, there is no cap** (new in draft 11, F271).

A payout is discharged when its receipt shows the amount minus a fee no greater than that maximum, where the plan states one (Money rule 18a, Money draft 7 rule 21). A shortfall beyond it is an open obligation of the service (F64; stated here, not in Money: F242, M8).

*Client conformance* (F271): **before a party signs terms whose split plan states no maximum fee per payout, its client MUST flag the absence**: where a receiver bears its payout's rail fee, any fee may then come off its payout, and no shortfall is the service's obligation.

*"Flag the risk" (Nobody, allegedly). Whether to cap the fee is the parties' choice; the core keeps only that the choice is seen before signing (F265).*

#### Rule 26 (was 25). Batching

The grant may allow the service to accumulate and pay out periodically or above a threshold, measured on the agreement's time reference.

Every incoming receipt must still be matched by a published split, so accumulation is visible. Payouts paid with one rail payment name the same batch in their receipts (Money).

*In short: what the split service may never do.*

#### Rule 27 (was 26). Only the plan

A split service MUST NOT pay anyone outside the plan's stakes as currently held, roles as evidenced, and named receivers.

It MUST NOT change the plan: only the owners can, by cloning their agreement.

#### Rule 28 (was 27). Fees

*In short: a Module states its fee, but only the agreement's own fee entry makes a fee owed.*

*Step 12b (F211, F213, F214):* a Module states its fee in its own specification (Development field 7), machine-readable and informative. **Only a fee entry of the agreement (terms field 28) makes a fee owed**, and nothing is forced (F42).

Fees are their own field (F213), so a constitution can place them in an area apart from the split plan, or leave them to the clone rule. The bearers (every stake, fees coming off the top) consent by the power the field falls under, save a holder with no voice in it, a deal's holder who is no party or a collective's departed holder: a version adding or raising a fee its stake bears needs that holder's signature (rule 48; new in draft 11, F270).

A fee entry says when it applies: every payment, payments for a named publication, or payments by a named rail. **A split leaving out a fee its entry makes owed is a wrong split, and the fee is the service's open obligation** (F214). The split's list of Modules (key 6) is the service's statement, never what decides.

*The text before step 12b:* A fee is one total with an agreed split among the modules that earn it. The service's own fee appears in the plan like any other cut. No split can include a fee without the signature of whoever bears its cost; on a creator-side fee, that is the creator. Every split names the modules it ran under (the field's format open, settled with the split plan's format before freeze; F140), so an omitted declared fee is visible, and each fee it takes as a payout naming its module and receiver (Q9).

#### Rule 29 (was 28). Attention

A split may follow plays, views or any metric a module reports.

**A metric share is paid off the top only, as overhead, like every share paying anyone but a stake** (rule 22; new in draft 12, F280).

The metric, its module and the resulting payouts are named (the metric and its share in the plan, the records in split key 7: "The open formats", step 12b), so it is legible like any other.

The metric's evidence is a record signed by the identity running the service that measured it: never by the split service, and never by a Module, which signs nothing (F116).

*In short: what the service owes when something is not paid, and how owners change services.*

#### Rule 30 (was 29). Open obligations

Each of these is an open obligation of the service, naming exactly one receiver and one agreement:

- every incoming receipt, and every payer's claim showing money arrived, without a matching split;
- every payout without a receipt;
- **every fee a split left out though its entry made it owed** (F214);
- **every payout a seller receipted after transferring the stake, owed to the buyer** (F217).

**A metric's measurer is none of the identities the metric divides among, nor the split service** (rule 29; Fable's review 2.7, F194). A metric pays only identities the plan already pays (OF13 a).

#### Rule 31 (was 30). Switching services

The owners switch services by revoking the grant, naming a new one, and having the identity publications are made for publish a new version of its payee pointer.

In a deal, each payee revokes its own grant, and the parties clone the deal, every party signing, its field 14 listing the payees' grants to the new service (F129, H4).

**A backup the split cMIP's fields already name (field 29), with one grant per payee in a deal, needs no clone.** It receives a payment whenever the attempts before it fail, payment by payment, and the next payment is tried at field 14's service again (rule 17; F249, F250; H6's grants per payee).

Switching for good, so that field 14 names another service, is this rule's revocation and clone.

The old service still owes payouts on everything it received before.

#### Rule 32 (was 31). The trust that remains

*Between receiving and paying out, the service holds other people's money. MOR cannot prevent theft, but makes it provable from the receipts and the payers' claims: hiding income requires the payer's silence or collusion. Owners who want less exposure choose services that pay out immediately, or payer-side splitting cMIPs, where capable clients pay each owner's pointer directly and no service's honesty is needed.*

### 5. Time, conditions and judges

#### Rule 33 (was 33). Time

An agreement that needs deadlines names its time reference. "Past its terms" is judged against that reference only.

Showing that something did *not* happen before a deadline needs evidence: an anchoring cMIP or a named keeper. Without either, the answer is undetermined.

#### Rule 34 (was 34). Conditions

An agreement may make an obligation or a payout depend on a condition, evaluated by the condition cMIP it names.

#### Rule 35 (was 34a). The chain of judgment

*In short: an agreement may name, for each judge, who takes over, in order, when that judge answers "unknown" or does not act in time; so a dead judge traps nobody.*

For each judge it names, an agreement may name, in its terms (field 21), the ones that take over, in order, when it answers "unknown" or does not act within its period (F121). The ones that take over are:

- for a judicial task, other specifications;
- for a keeper's operator, an arbitrator or verifier, or the abandonment authority, other identities.

*The split service is no judge (F249, F250): its backups are attempts per payment, not stages (rule 17, field 29), and this rule's periods and deadlines do not apply to them.*

Everyone signs the chain with the terms: every party in a deal, every member in a collective, as for the whole judicial tier (rule 67).

*How it runs most of the time.*

**When a judge answers "unknown", the next in its chain is asked. The first answer that is not "unknown" is the chain's answer. No new signature is needed when it passes.**

- A condition cMIP's "unknown" and a time reference's "undetermined" are such answers.
- "pending" is not (Tasks).
- Where every one answers "unknown", the chain's answer is "unknown".

*A client that does not hold a judge's specification shows the question as unknown, as for any act it cannot judge. That is the client's ignorance, not the judge's answer, and it never passes the question down the chain: otherwise two clients holding different specifications would reach different answers.*

**A judge that does not act passes the question on too** (Q7). Each link names a period. Once the judge has been asked and has not acted within it, as the agreement's time reference judges it, the next may act. Where the time reference answers "undetermined", so is the chain's answer.

Within one link, a judge contradicting itself has answered "unknown" (F206), and the next link is active with no period to wait.

An arbitrator's answer is an act of the condition cMIP the agreement names (task 9).

*The rare cases, in detail:* periods and stages.

**The chain runs on the agreement's deadlines, stage by stage, per question, never per party** (F246, N6, F245).

*"There is no 'one party deadline'. There is an agreed deadline." (Nobody, allegedly).*

A question is a disputed fork (rule 47) or any question put to the chain.

- **The first valid request with standing** (rule 54) **starts the active link's period**, measured on the agreement's time reference (rule 33).
- **Later requests on the same question join that stage** and start no clock of their own.
- **When the period passes with no answer, the next link is active, and every earlier link is void on that question** (F124, N6, F245).
- **Only the active link can settle.** **A settlement signed outside its link's active window counts for nothing** (mechanic, the project lead's, under the delegation): before the window opened, or after it closed.

*"if the next judge is triggered, the previous judge is void" (N6); "the currently active judge settles it" (F245).*

So two links never both settle one question. Where an earlier link settles after a later one took over, having seen its settlement or not, the later link's stands (QL2).

*Mechanics, the build's:*

- the question of a fork is its reference, the version the settlement requests name (type 22);
- the question of a request to a judge is its field 2 (type 25);
- each next link's period runs from the end of the one before.

*Read the same way, the project lead's (QL3):* the rule is the chain's, so it holds for every judge the chain serves, one active link per question.

**A request counts, and the period runs, only where it is public or sealed to the judge it names** (step 12b, Fable's review 2.6, in F189 (8)'s form). This holds for the request to a judge (type 25), and for the settlement request (type 22) likewise. A link of the chain the request never reached was never asked.

*"It's a chain of judgment" (Nobody, allegedly): a judge that fails is replaced by the one everyone already signed, so a dead judge traps nobody.*

### 6. Keepers

#### Rule 36 (was 6). Named by the agreement

Keepers are chosen by the parties when they sign, so neither side can later substitute a friendly one.

- In a collective, the keepers change only with every member's signature (rule 67, F121).
- A deal's keepers change only with every party (rule 47).

#### Rule 37 (was 7). Records

A keeper records the deal and the acts around it as they arrive, in its operator's sequence, and signs each record.

#### Rule 38 (was 8). Several keepers

An agreement may name several keepers and a rule for what counts as recorded: any one, a threshold, or all. Only the named keepers' records count as testimony.

#### Rule 39 (was 9). Copies

Records are signed and hash-linked, so they can be copied anywhere and verified wherever they sit. If a keeper disappears, its records survive in every copy.

#### Rule 40 (was 10). Keepers check homes

Before recording an act, a keeper checks the signer's identity chain at its homes, and records any rotation it sees.

#### Rule 41 (was 11). What a rotation cannot erase

A record counts against a rotation only if it sits before the keeper's own record of that rotation, in the keeper's sequence.

Such an act, even outside the kept ancestry or disowned, becomes a visible dispute rather than void (Identity).

#### Rule 42 (was 11a). A record counts only alongside the act it names

A keeper record counts, for that purpose, only if:

- the verifier holds the act it names;
- that act is valid as an act;
- and the keeper is one named by an agreement the act's `objects` names, or, for an act of a collective placed against the collective's own line, one named by the collective's agreement, as "Made before, made after" says (C4).

A record of an act nobody can produce changes no verdict (F58).

*A keeper can delay recording a rotation while recording a thief's acts first. That is trust in keepers, accepted and stated: choose keepers as carefully as homes.*

#### Rule 43 (was 12). Keepers are always sealed

*In short: a keeper never needs an act's content, and is never given it for being a keeper.*

*A keeper only has to prove that an act existed before a rotation. For that it needs the act id and the signer, both on the act's outside, and a check of the signer's home, never the content.*

A client MUST NOT deliver an act's content key to a party because it is a named keeper. A keeper that must also judge content is named as an arbitrator or verifier as well.

*This is a rule for clients: key deliveries are private, so no one else could check it.*

#### Rule 44 (was 12a). Judging content is someone else's job

Where a third party must judge content (escrow and milestones, arbitration, regulated verification), the agreement names an arbitrator or verifier, and the parties deliver it the keys by ordinary key delivery.

### 7. Changing an agreement

#### Rule 45 (was 45). Clone, never modify

*In short: an agreement is never edited. A change is a new version, a clone, that names the version before it and comes into force only when everyone it needs has signed.*

A clone is new terms naming the parent and the parent's latest act. Its mark (field 4) names each power its changes require (rule 64, or rule 47 for a deal). For each power, it names the parties whose signatures meet it (rule 65, or rule 47 for a deal).

A clone is a draft until all of these hold:
- every party its mark names has a valid signature act naming it;
- every party it adds, and every party it makes a holder of an area it did not hold, has one too;
- every holder whose share of a stake it drops or lowers has one too, member or not (rule 48, F124 N5);
- every holder it pays less, by lowering the stake's part of the split plan or adding or raising a fee the stake bears, who has no voice in the power making that change (a deal's holder who is no party, a collective's departed holder), has one too (rule 48; new in draft 11, F270);
- every departed holder whose own stake plan it changes has one too (M1, F125);
- where it changes the release rule, every holder of every stake the parent defines has one too (N8);
- the parent exists.

**A draft changes nothing.** *Nobody is added to a collective, or given an area, without their approval (Q11, Q13): a party added is bound only by its own signature (rule 1), and until it signs, the clone that adds it changes nothing.*

A complete clone closes the parent. Obligations before that point settle under the parent.

Where two complete clones of one deal share a parent, rule 47 says which version is in force (F186). For a collective's agreement, rules 72 and 90 settle it.

#### Rule 46 (was 45a). A false mark makes a clone invalid

A false mark makes a clone invalid (F104).

A clone is invalid, whatever signatures it gathers, if its mark:
- names other powers than those its changes require; or
- names, for a power, a signer who is not among the parties that power is counted among; or
- names too few of them to meet it (as rule 65 counts them).

A mark naming a succession plan, `[3, party]`, is checked as rule 83 says.

Signature acts by parties the mark does not name bind those parties (rule 1), but do not bring the clone into force.

*The mark names exactly the required powers, never "one at least as strong": powers are not ranked against each other (an area held by one member is neither stronger nor weaker than a clone rule of two of three), so "stronger" could not be computed. It names identities, not signature acts: the signature acts name the clone, so the clone cannot name them. Every version thus says on its face who brought it into force and under which power, and any verifier checks it from the parent and the clone alone. Founding terms' field 4 already said the same thing: which signatures make them exist.*

#### Rule 47 (was 45b). A deal changes only with everyone's signature

A deal changes only with everyone's signature (F107).

In a deal, the signing rule and the clone rule are every party (terms fields 4 and 5). A deal exists when all its parties have signed it. A clone of it completes only when all the parent's parties whose voice remains have signed it. Its mark names the clone rule, `[1]`, and those parties.

Majorities, areas and tiers belong to collectives. Rules 62 to 66, 67, 68, 90 and 91 apply to collectives only.

**A deal ends, or winds down, by such a clone (new in draft 11, F263).** A deal has no closing act. Its parties end it, or wind it down, by a clone every party signs, stating its new conditions: where its stakes went, what is still owed (open obligations), and, where the deal is a party of another agreement, to whom that outer agreement now pays (its designated pointer, rule 15, F248). What those conditions are depends on the deal. A party who is absent is handled by the absence rules (rules 85 to 89; in a deal one identity judges absence, B19): where the deal provides it, the declaration removes the absent party's voice (rule 89), and the clone completes with the parties whose voice remains; the absent party's stake is never reduced without it (rule 48). *"It really depends on the kind of agreement" (Nobody, allegedly).*

*Stated cost: one silent party blocks every change to a deal, however large: "one person can force everything until all agree. That's deal making in everyday life" (Nobody, allegedly). The ways out: the abandonment clause, whose declaration is not a clone, can remove an absent party's voice where the deal provides it (rule 89); succession plans; and, for many parties or a long relationship, a collective.*

*How it runs most of the time:* a deal has one line of versions, each a complete clone of the one before, and the latest is in force. A deal cannot break: it has no keys of its own, and a clone that is not complete, or whose mark is false, is a draft or invalid and changes nothing (rules 45, 46).

**Rarely: two complete versions of one deal** (F186, F188, F189, F190, F192, F196, QG4).

**The fork.** Rarely, every party whose voice remains signs two clones of the same version, neither descending from the other (two devices out of step, a careless client, one party in bad faith and another mistaken). Both are complete, and that version has two branches: a fork of the deal.

Then, in short: the reference holds while the deal has two branches; a new act may follow either branch, and counts; the parties settle the fork by one version that names every tip it drops; a settlement is final for everything its signers could see; and where the parties cannot agree, the judge of forks the reference names may settle it on a party's request. Each part follows.

**The reference.** While the deal has two branches, the version before the split is the reference. It is the last version every party agrees on, what a verifier reports in force, and the point the settlement names.

**Acts on either branch.** A new act may follow either branch, and counts (A1 beside A4). A payment, a split, a sale or a release naming a version of a branch is judged under that branch's latest version. One naming the reference is judged under the reference. Every party signed both branches, so what is done under either counts, in Agreements and in Money alike, which cannot be separated here.

*The buyer is protected from the parties' human error: "I'd like the before split to always count, but it cannot due to the decentralized nature of the network. So, this scenario has to be accepted, the buyer should be protected from the human errors" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**The settling version.** The parties settle the fork by one version that names every tip it drops, signed by the parties of every branch. From it on, its line is in force and the dropped branches are not. A branch that grows settles nothing.

Only complete versions of the deal count here (F189, 2). A version is read as settling only once it is complete and is a version of this deal. A draft, or an act whose parent is no version of the deal, is nobody's version, and changes nothing.

The settling version (A2; F188; F189, 6; QF3) is a complete clone:
- whose parent (field 11) is the latest version of one branch; and
- which names in field 26 every tip it drops: the latest version of each other branch, ascending, each cited in `objects` in that order after its parent.

It is signed by the parties of all the branches involved (DQ5, QF3). Besides its mark, which names the clone rule and the parent's parties as for any clone of a deal (this rule), every party of each tip it drops whose voice remains signs it too. So no one on a dropped side is dropped without consent: everyone sees every option, everyone signs the choice.

**What a settlement could see** (F192). MOR has no clock, so a settlement could see exactly what its history holds:
- its own line (its parent and the versions before it);
- each tip it names, with that tip's history;
- what it cites, each with its own history;
- for a judge's settlement, the histories of the version it keeps and of the tips it drops.

Nothing else counts as seen.

A settlement counts only where it names every tip it saw (F188). Where its history holds a version of the fork that is neither on the line it keeps nor at or below a tip it names (a newer version of a branch whose older version it names, or a branch it cites and does not name), it settles nothing, and is a plain version.

**Finality.** A settlement that counts is final for everything its signers could see (A3), and nothing they could not see reopens it. These reopen nothing:
- a version on a dropped branch the settlement does not hold (published late, kept back, or grown there by a party left alone on it);
- a whole branch the settlement never saw;
- a second settlement whose history holds it (made after it), whoever signs it, the judge of forks included.

Only a new version on the line the settlement put in force, complete as this rule asks (signed by every party whose voice remains in the version in force), changes the deal. A client may show such a late version as a leftover of a dropped branch; it changes nothing.

*Stated cost: whoever signed a version on a dropped branch that the settlement's signers could not see loses it, honestly forgotten on an out-of-step device or kept hidden on purpose; nobody who settled in good faith loses anything.*

Finality stands (F221): a settlement is final against a complete branch its signers never saw (A3, F192). The exploration "a forked deal is broken, like a collective" is closed, not adopted (Fable's review, `docs/reviews/forked-deal-broken-review.md`, whose tests pin this). *"Yes, if that is the version that shifts the weight and the responsible party the best" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**Client conformance against hidden branches.** Every hidden branch passes through one point: a party signing two different successors of one version.

*Client conformance* (F221): **before signing a successor of a version, a client checks, across all its owner's devices, whether its owner has already signed another successor of the same version; if so, it shows the first and does not sign silently; and a settling version the client signs names every successor of the reference its owner signed** (IT2b's rule for endings, carried to deals).

*Mechanic, the build's:* proposing a version is signing it; the reference is the latest version both the settling version's line and every tip it names descend from; a successor is named where it is on the settling version's line or at or above a tip it names.

Accidental double signing disappears; what remains is deliberate, its trail public for good.

*Stated cost:* (QH2, F222) a newcomer on a branch the settlers hid is dropped by a settlement that does not name it (F192's stated cost). This is now reached only by settlers who knowingly double-signed past their clients' warnings, their signatures public for good.

*Client conformance* (F222): **before a newcomer signs onto a deal, her client looks for another successor of the version she joins, at the parties' relays and keepers, and warns her if it finds one**.

*Mechanic, the build's:* every version on the line she joins is looked at; a successor a settling version on that line names (at or above a tip it names) was dropped for good and is not warned of.

*For deals that want more:* a keeper (Q28); nothing new in the core.

**Two settlements of one fork, neither holding the other.**
- Where both are the parties' settling versions: every party signed both, and the deal is tangled, on its reference, until a settlement holding both, and naming every tip, settles it (DQ3, QF3).
- Where both are one judge's (F206, QH1): a judge's two settlements of one fork, neither holding the other, both count for nothing. They are read as the judge answering "unknown" (rule 35): the deal stays on its last agreed version (DQ1 to DQ4), and the next link of the chain of judgment settles, with no period to wait. The double signature stays visible, and a client shows it beside the fork.
- Where one is signed by every party and the other is a judge's: the parties' holds, and the judge's counts for nothing. The judge is a fallback a request activates, and a version every party signed is the deal changing by its own rule.
- Two links' settlements of one fork never both count: only the link active on the fork settles it (F246; rule 35). A link whose stage on the fork passed with no answer is void on it. So where an earlier link settles after a later link took over, the later link's stands, whether or not the earlier one saw it (F245, QL2). A settlement signed before its link's stage opened counts for nothing too.

F206 still applies within one link. F192 (a settlement holding another is after it, and the first is final) still decides where one holds the other.

*Mechanic, the build's:* a judge has contradicted itself where its first settlements of the fork, read by history as F192 reads any two, keep different versions; one that settles the same way twice has not; a judge that contradicted itself speaks for nothing on that fork afterwards.

**A settling version naming a version this verifier does not hold** is read as a plain version until the verifier holds it. From then on it counts as a settlement (F196). The deal stays readable meanwhile, forked or on its reference as before, and the parties may sign a correct settlement.

**A tangled fork stays on its reference** (DQ1 to DQ4). A fork is tangled where, with no settlement that counts:
- a branch splits again; or
- three or more complete versions share one version; or
- two settlements of the parties compete (DQ3); or
- a settling version is made from below its own branch's latest version, which it holds (DQ4; one that does not hold it could not see it, and that later version loses, F192).

The deal then stays on its last agreed version, the reference, until one settlement naming every tip it drops (QF3). The parties lose what the tangled versions changed, and must settle cleanly. Any buyer who paid under a version every party signed stays protected, as for a simple fork, the payment judged under the version it names. *A stated cost, borne by those who kept signing two versions.*

**The judge of forks.** Where the parties cannot agree, the judge of forks the reference names may settle the fork (DQ8, QF2, F190). The reference names which of its judges settles forks: terms field 27, one identity among its arbitrators or verifiers (field 13). Where it names none, none does, and the deal waits on its reference. *"A grant to act under specific conditions, here the condition is the existence of a fork."* Every party consented in advance by signing the reference, or a branch that inherits it.

The judge of forks acts only once activated by one of the signing parties: a settlement request (type 22), signed by a party who signed the reference, naming it.

Its settlement (type 23) names that request, the version of the fork it keeps, and every tip it drops (QF3). From it on, the kept version's line is in force, what grows on it included, as after a settling version. It is final as a settling version is, for what it could see, and counts only where it names every tip it saw.

It follows the chain of judgment (QG4):
- Where the judge of forks does not act within its period, the next link of its chain (field 21) takes over, with no new signature, as everyone signed the chain (F121). The period is counted on the deal's time reference from the first request on the fork, later requests joining that stage (rule 35, F246).
- An answer a link gives after its period counts for nothing in that question (F124).
- Settling a fork is signing a fork settlement, so only an identity in the chain can settle. A link that is a specification signs nothing and passes the question on once its period passes.

*"Judging relies on a chain of judges every time it is needed. If a fork ends up being disputed it needs to follow the chain described in the agreement" (Nobody, allegedly).*

Any judge speaks once per fork (F192): a second settlement, whose history holds the first, counts for nothing.

What the request shows (DQ8, restated by the project lead under the delegation): that a party who signed the reference knew of the fork when it signed the request; never on the judge's own initiative. It does not show that every party knew, the party losing a branch included.

*Stated cost (Fable's review of the F190 build, 6d): on a bait branch, the party who made the bait knows of the fork and may request at once, and a judge chosen badly may settle before the other party has seen it; every party chose the judge, by signing the reference.*

**The keepers' record of who signed what, and in what order, is the evidence the judge weighs**, never an automatic "first wins". With no judge of forks named, the deal stays on its reference. The amicable path comes first: a version naming every tip it drops, signed by the parties of all the branches.

*A keeper or arbitrator settling a fork no party has asked about would reopen the bait branch through a colluding keeper; the request is what MOR can check, having no clock.*

*Settled only by a version naming the other branch's tip, so every signer saw the choice: under "the next complete version on one branch settles it", a party in bad faith could steer an unaware partner into settling the fork with a harmless change, and a verifier holding one branch only would see no fork at all; one act naming the tips makes the choice knowing and the settlement checkable. Its cost: field 26 ("The bad faith cases push me towards b", Nobody, allegedly). "Tips are what matter on the forks" (Nobody, allegedly).*

*DQ5's reasoning: by accident the case almost never arises; on purpose it is a bait branch, a fork only one side knows of, used to take a newcomer's money, work, rights or name, then settle on the other side and drop them. Stated cost: a party of either branch can refuse every settlement and keep the deal on its reference; that is all it can do.*

*F192's reasoning: treating a version nobody could see as possibly made before the settlement would reopen a settled deal; that would narrow A3 ("Settle is final… Only one survives… Matter closed") and break "only what a party can read can be used against them".*

**The alarms.** *Client conformance:*
- **a seller's client, and a split service, MUST raise the alarm when a payment names a version of the deal that does not descend from the version they hold, showing both branches and pointing to settling the fork;**
- a version they do not hold raises it too (F189, 3): an unknown version is the hidden fork the alarm exists for, to be fetched from the payer's relays;
- a payment naming an older version of the same line, with no fork, raises a plain notice, not the alarm (DQ7).

*"It is not the same issue. One is a fork, one is an outdated offer somehow still interpreted as valid" (Nobody, allegedly); alarm fatigue would teach sellers to ignore the alarm the day a bait branch shows up.*

*A payment under an agreement names the version it followed (Money, the claim's field 9, and the receipt's), so a purchase under the other branch reaches the sellers as signed evidence of the fork: "They are the participants who can look for the full picture if made aware of the need to look" (Nobody, allegedly).*

#### Rule 48 (was 46). No stake reduced without its holder

A clone can never reduce a stake without its holder's signature, unless the agreement pre-authorised it.

**A stake moved by succession is not a reduction, and the only advance permissions that move a stake without its holder are rule 89's** (new in draft 12, F279): a declaration of absence passes the stake to the stake successors the holder's succession plan names, or, where it names none, outcome 1 or 2 redistributes or transfers it.

**Nor is what a stake is paid lowered without its holder, where that holder has no voice in the power making the change** (new in draft 11, F270). A version that lowers a stake's part of the split plan (field 8), or adds or raises a fee the stake bears (field 28), needs the signature of every holder of that stake it pays less who has no voice in the power making that change:
- in a deal, a holder who is no party (every party signs every version of a deal, rule 47, F107);
- in a collective, **every holder who is no member: a departed holder (rule 68), a stake successor who received a stake by succession (rule 83), and a holder who bought part of a stake (rule 10)** (new in draft 13, F282: draft 12's reading of Q2, decided). *"Q2 also answers itself. A new agreement is born. All signature are needed, yes it is born from a clone" (Nobody, allegedly): a change paying such a holder less is a new version, born from a clone, and every holder it pays less who has no voice in the power making it signs it.*

A member, who holds the power or chose it by signing the constitution, is left to the collective's own rules (rules 64, 65).

**No advance permission reaches what a stake is paid** (new in draft 12, F279; F273's mechanism superseded by F279). Abandonment never lowers what a stake is paid while the stake stays with its holder. A departed holder who vanishes does not block the collective's fees, because its stake moves: declared absent, to its stake successor, or, with none named, by outcome 1 or 2 (rule 89); whoever then holds it signs, or already holds the say. F273's "never" for a deal's holder who is no party stands (new in draft 12, F273).

*"Abandonment is meant to protect operational flow. It is not meant to change stakes or even pay. What abandonment can trigger in that regard is succession. Where the stake and associated pay go to the successor" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**A stake is paid less by any change that lowers what it receives from a payment or can expect from the plan as written** (new in draft 12, F274): another share added or raised, a role share added, unfilled roles sent elsewhere, the rail fee moved onto receivers, the maximum fee raised or removed, the split cMIP or its parameters changed, as well as a fee added or raised. **What a cMIP pays cannot be read from the plan alone, so changing the split cMIP or its parameters counts.**

*"What money sees and money expects is what matters" (Nobody, allegedly). Judged by effect: what reaches the stake from a payment, and what it can expect from the plan as written.*

**Since a plan pays non-owners off the top only, every share in the rest is a stake's** (new in draft 12, F280): a stake is paid less by anything taken off the top added or raised (a role share, a named receiver, a metric share), by a role nobody fills sent away from the stakes, or by its part lowered against the other stakes' parts; never by a non-owner's share in the rest, which no valid plan has.

*"If this can happen in a simple joint venture agreement it is a problem"; "One stone two birds, agreed" (Nobody, allegedly). The voices chose the power over fields 8 and 28; those without one did not, and what a stake is paid is what no choice of theirs may break.*

#### Rule 49 (was 48). Exit

Agreements are always in core format, so activity on a fading cMIP moves by cloning the agreement onto another.

### 8. When something goes wrong

*The core never enforces; it makes default impossible to hide.*

#### Rule 50 (was 54). Atomic where possible

Payment is acceptance. Delivery released by payment cannot be defaulted on.

#### Rule 51 (was 55). Failure attribution

*In short: when a payment or a payout fails, the record names who and what failed.*

Money's receipts and payment claims name the hop and agreement that failed. A split's missing payout names the receiver and agreement.

Agreements define what follows: refund, redirection, contest.

#### Rule 52 (was 56). Negotiation record

Two sides negotiate by negotiation messages (type 18) (F118). Each message is locked and signed, names the previous message in the thread, and acknowledges the latest one received from the other side.

The record is provably complete up to the last acknowledged message. A message acknowledged by the other side fixes every message of the thread before it, by the previous messages each names. It also shows the other side received them all.

Only negotiation messages make the record:

- a text act, a reaction or any other act naming the thread is no part of it, carries no acknowledgement (Envelopes rule 4a, F110), and proves nothing about its completeness;
- a negotiation message whose previous is not a message of the same thread is no part of the record either.

Two messages naming the same previous are a fork, visible and provable (Envelopes).

#### Rule 53 (was 57). Disclosure by choice

Either party may publish the record, by publishing the keys of its messages (Envelopes: going public later).

#### Rule 54 (was 57a). Standing

A contest counts only when signed by someone with standing:

- a party to the agreement;
- a holder of a stake the contested act affects;
- a keeper, arbitrator or verifier the agreement names.

A contest never changes any act's validity, status or selection. It is shown alongside what it names (F69).

*Reputation, arbitration and courts build on this record from outside the core.*

### 9. Public domain

#### Rule 55 (was 17). Public domain

The owners may release a work by a release act (type 5, F121). It takes effect at once, or at a future point on the time reference, its content key delivered then by an identity the act names (F124, N11).

A release does three things:

- **the claim ends**: nobody earns from the work as its owner any more, and no sale of it is outside a claim;
- **its history stays named**: the stakes it ends and the claims naming its creators;
- **its content key is published**: anyone may carry or sell copies.

It needs every direct owner's signature, departed holders of a stake in the work included, a collective owner signing by its own rules across the lanes of every layer a release touches (F124, N7). This holds unless the release rule says otherwise (field 24, set at founding, changed only by a clone every owner signs, N8).

**A release is permanent.**

**A claim the release does not name in its history is shown beside it**: made after it, or a competing claim, openly contested (rule 7, N12), which the release cannot end.

*"A work that belongs to no one but traces its history" (Nobody, allegedly).*

Its legal effect lies outside the core.

## Part two: collectives

### 10. A collective

#### Rule 56 (was 35). A collective is a full identity

A collective is a full identity, with its own genesis, homes, signing key and chain key.

It uses the Identity MIR unchanged: it signs its own receipts, sets its own payee pointers, and rotates. Its genesis declares its founding agreement (an Agreements declaration in the declarations slot), and the agreement carries its key grammar.

#### Rule 57 (was 35a). An act is done once sealed to every member and on the chain

A collective's act is done only once it is sealed to every member and on its chain (F126, F128).

*In short: what a collective does, every member can see, and it is written on the collective's own chain. Until both are true, the act is only a plan.*

An act in a collective's name, signed with its key or with a grant key of its (F128), is done, and binds the collective, only once both of these hold:

- it is sealed to every member: every party of the agreement in force for it is among its recipients (Envelopes, `to`), or the act is public; and
- it is on the collective's chain, citing its head as its signer knew it (rule 58).

Before that, even signed, it is planning. It binds no one, its signer included. It counts for nothing in Agreements: an area's signatures on it complete nothing, and an obligation it carries binds no one.

*A counterparty is safe once a later act of the collective cites its act, against everything but the collusion of an ending's signers, which stays visible* (F131, IT2a, IT2b; rules 76, 79).

*Client conformance:* the collective's client drafts the act, signs it, seals it to every member, and publishes it where the collective's clients look.

*"Powers can be segmented, but information should run free; it's a continuous collective assembly where matters are exposed. It only bothers cheaters" (Nobody, allegedly).*

*Stated cost: a collective keeps no secret from its own members. What a collective plans can stay with some members for a while; once done, it is a fact that touches everyone in it.*

**Where an act is held is never a condition** (F128). An act is valid by its signatures, seals and citations wherever a verifier holds it. Relays are transport: they are where a collective's clients publish and look first, a matter of client conformance or of a cMIP (the relay transport cMIP), never of the core. A verifier states where it found each act as information only; no validity rests on it (reading 9, adjusted, F128).

One rule covers every act in the collective's name, with no exception by type. That includes records (W3, F127), and forks and closings (W5, F128):

- a record not done is no line ("Record (type 17)");
- a fork or closing not done takes no effect (rule 91).

*The rare exceptions, in detail:*

- **Negotiation messages are the one kind not held to this rule** (W6, F128). They are talk, binding nothing, and on neither chain. They need not be sealed to every member, since planning is allowed to stay secret (F126). The deal they lead to is signed, and that signature is an action, which must be done.
- **The collective's identity-chain acts (genesis, rotations) are done by Identity's own rules**: public and at its homes (reading 8, adjusted, F128: no relays).

#### Rule 58 (was 35b). Two chains

A collective keeps two chains (F127): a decisions chain and an actions chain.

**The decisions chain** holds its genesis, rotations and records (which write its clones and register who joins and leaves), its grants and revocations (F128), and its fork or closing. Each decision cites the previous decision and the action head it saw, by what it already names:

- a record, by its own sequence and kept tips;
- a rotation, by its kept tips (Identity);
- a grant or revocation, by its own sequence and its citations in `objects`, as an action cites (reading, F128);
- a fork or closing, by its chain act and tips.

Decisions keep their formats (reading 1, confirmed).

**The actions chain** holds every other act signed with the collective's key or a grant key of its (reading 2, adjusted, F128): debts, payments, publications, releases, sales, and the acts of its grantees' strands.

On neither chain are:

- negotiation messages (W6);
- Identity's own everyday acts, which count for nothing in Agreements. A witness act of the collective is one of them: it adopts nothing (F142) and places nothing (F156).

**How an action cites.** An action names, in its inside's `objects`, the collective's identity as chain, once for each act it cites:

- the decision it acts under: a record of the collective, or a link of its identity chain, under the action's own key or an earlier one; and
- each head of another device it knows, so that the next act joins the heads where several devices signed at once.

Its own sequence's previous act counts as cited. These entries come after those the act's type defines, all on that one chain. Agreements' formats allow them there: signature, release, resignation, declaration, terms.

**An act within its signer's powers counts for the collective as soon as it is done and cites the collective's head as the signer knew it.** It is judged under what its decisions leave in force: the chain's declarations, records carried forward under earlier keys, and, under its own key, the records its history holds and those before them (rule 72). A record it never cites decides nothing for it, save by the tie rule ("Made before, made after", 7). An action is judged under the decisions its history holds (reading 3, confirmed).

**An action citing no decision, or naming on the collective's chain an act that is not on it, is on no chain of the collective, and counts for nothing.**

**An act signed with a grant key cites, among its decisions, its grant** (F128). The grantee's strand is like a device of the collective, limited to the grant's scope.

*"If I act within my powers in a collective I act for the collective" (Nobody, allegedly). The chain's role is to make acts unmissable, not to confirm them.*

*Client conformance: the collective's client cites its latest decision and every head it holds.*

*Identity's own everyday acts (a witness act, routes, an encryption key) carry no objects (Identity) and are on neither chain (reading 2, confirmed).*

#### Rule 59 (was 36). Key grammar

The founding agreement says how the collective's keys are held and activated, according to the members' taste.

**The signing key** is held in one of these ways:

- one holder;
- a threshold: any k of n members jointly produce one ordinary signature, for example with FROST threshold schnorr;
- a custodian holding the key under the collective's grant.

**The chain key** is held by one holder, by a custodian, or as shares: split so that any k of n members can rebuild it.

**Rotating with a shared chain key.** At rotation, k members bring their shares to one offline device, which rebuilds the key, signs once and forgets it.

*For that moment one device holds the whole key; that is the price, and it holds the next key too, before dealing its shares. Nothing can prove a device forgot a key.*

The Module that does it MUST deal the shares of the next key verifiably, and have them checked against the committed key before that key is relied on, so that the members' shares rebuild it. Otherwise the device's holder could end up the only one able to rotate the collective (F97).

*Verifiable dealing stops sole control, not a copy; a copy kept is the price above, stated.*

**A key grammar MUST leave a way to rotate that survives the loss of any one key holder** (F96). That way is either:

- a threshold below the member count; or
- a named recovery path: a custodian holding a share under grant, or an escrowed share released by the abandonment authority, which the abandonment clause names.

A key holder is anyone holding the whole key or a share of it: a member, a custodian, the holder of an escrowed share.

**Where one person holds the chain key, the agreement MUST name a successor (a seat successor in that holder's succession plan) and an escrowed share released to them under its succession or abandonment clause.** A single custodian likewise needs a recovery path held by another.

A grammar without such a way is invalid.

Two partners may choose "both", provided they name where a further share sits and who releases it. Whether the path works is the members' risk, stated (F77).

*For a single holder, one construction: the key is split so that any two of four shares rebuild it; the holder keeps two and signs alone, the successor and a keeper hold one each, useless alone. When the named authority declares the holder absent, the keeper releases its share and the successor rotates the collective to a new key; the succession clone passes the seat.*

**That succession clone also carries a succession plan for the successor, naming their own seat successor, signed by them in the same clone** (Flaw B17, rules 64, 83). So the key always has a named successor, and the clone meets this rule.

*The successor signs the clone to join anyway (Q11), so the plan they add is signed by them; the escrowed share stays where the key grammar's recovery path puts it, released by the same clause.*

Where members want individual consent to show, the constitution gives acts of the collective to areas (rule 60).

#### Rule 60 (was 36a). Areas: rules by layer, powers of members

A collective's founding agreement may divide its acts and its operational fields into areas, each held by some of its members (F103).

*In short: an area is a part of the collective's business that some members decide on. An act in that area counts only when enough of them sign it.*

An area is drawn either:

- by whole layer (Identity, Envelopes and Text, Money, Agreements, Development); or
- by act type within a specification.

An area over the Identity layer governs the collective's rotations and key events, Identity acts on its decisions chain. The collective's everyday Identity acts count for nothing in Agreements (F156, F162).

**When an act counts.** An act of the collective that an area reaches counts only with valid signature acts naming it (rule 6), meeting the area's threshold as rule 65 counts it, by holders of that area who:

- are holders of that area in the agreement in force;
- each signed it, or a version it descends from by complete clones; and
- still have a voice there for that act ("Made before, made after", rules 70, 71).

**The agreement in force for that act** is the one the collective's identity chain declared at the chain act its signing key is bound to (its binding). Where the act comes after a record made since that declaration, it is instead the clone such a record put in force, as rule 72 picks it (F100, F106, F109).

An act of an area that stands frozen counts for nothing (rule 71).

An act no area reaches counts on the collective's own signature, as any identity's act does, unless its specification is unadopted (below).

*So when members change, the rotation that declares the clone also fences off the old rules: whatever the old key signs afterwards is void under Identity, whoever signs it. Members' own identities do not rotate when they leave a collective; without this, former members could sign under the old rules at any later time, and no one could tell.*

*An area says whose consent counts; the key grammar says who can physically produce the collective's signature. A holder who should act alone without holding the key acts under a grant issued within the area (rule 74), with the grant key it hands them (F128).*

*Layers and areas are one construct (Q10): a rule for a whole layer, a lane, is an area that reaches that layer, held by the members who decide on it; an area may also reach single act types, and operational fields, with one set of rules for holding it, granting within it and changing it.*

**What a lane reaches.** An area reaching a layer reaches every act of that layer ("Layer"):

- the MIR's own acts;
- the acts of the cMIPs the agreement in force names for that layer's tasks;
- for Development, the acts of the extensions it names (F106).

An act that belongs to two layers or more, of a specification named for tasks of each, is reached by the lane of each. It counts only when each lane's holders meet its threshold, as a clone across two areas does (Q7, R4).

**A lane decides what the collective adopts for its layer** (F106):

- which cMIP fills each of that layer's tasks. So the holders of the Money lane adopt a new payment cMIP alone; the rails beneath it are named by the collective's payee pointer, an act of that lane, or its vault (F115);
- for the Development lane, which extensions the collective adopts.

**Specifications the collective has not adopted.** In a collective whose agreement in force has any area, an act whose `spec` is a specification that agreement names neither for a task (field 2) nor as an extension (field 15) counts for nothing (Q16). The one exception: an area reaches it by a kind naming its type, `[1, spec, type]`; then it counts as that area decides.

To count, the specification must first be adopted, under the powers its adoption needs (rule 64):

- for an operational task, that task's lane;
- as an extension, the Development lane with the lane of each layer it declares.

An act of a MIR is never such an act.

*Otherwise an unlisted specification would be a way around every lane: whoever holds the collective's key could act under a specification nobody adopted, around the lane's holders. A rail is not adopted this way: it is named by the collective's own pointer or vault (Money rule 12a, F115). With adoption in the lane's own hands, waiting costs little.*

**The judicial tasks.** The tasks of the judicial tier (9 condition evaluation, 10 time reference, 11 anchoring) stay judicial, outside the Agreements lane (Q3, Q15). Neither the Agreements lane's holders nor any other area changes them. They change only with every member's signature (rule 67, F121). That includes a version that also changes the constitution, which needs the constitutional change rule too (rule 64.1, F122).

The Agreements lane adopts the cMIPs for Agreements' other tasks (8 split, 12 grant limits, 13 work claims).

**A specification adopted for a judicial task is adopted for no other task, and is no extension** (Q20, Q25, "Areas"). So R4 never joins a judge to a lane.

*Each judicial task can decide who wins a dispute, and one lane holder picking a friendlier judge after the fact is worse than a majority doing it.*

**Extensions.** An extension acting on another layer needs that layer's lane too, in the same clone, all at once (Q7). Its specification declares the layers it acts on (Development, specification field 10), so a client knows whose approval to ask (rule 63).

**Dropping an extension needs the same approvals as adopting it**: the Development lane and the lane of each layer it declares (Q18).

*"Modules and cMIPs should be able to be governed on the layer they sit."*

The declaration is not trusted for enforcement. In a collective, a verifier applies an extension's rules only to acts of Development and of the layers whose power its adoption met. So an extension that under-declares binds nothing beyond the lanes whose holders approved it.

Nothing an extension does relaxes the core (Development 8c): the MIRs' own acts stay in their own layers, whatever an extension adds around them.

#### Rule 61 (was 36b). Every constitutional voice is covered

Every party whose signature a collective's constitutional change rule is counted among MUST be covered by the agreement's abandonment clause (F105).

Those parties are: every party under the default or a threshold; the named parties under a rule naming parties.

A party is covered when the clause allows outcome 0, removing a voice, and its authority can declare that party absent. So a named authority does not cover itself.

Coverage is judged under the clause in force, one version for everyone (rule 67, F121).

A founding agreement or clone of a collective that leaves such a party uncovered is invalid, as a key grammar without a way to rotate is.

*The constitution is the tier that can repair every other (reassign an area, revoke a grant, refit the key grammar), so it alone must never freeze; it freezes only through the loss of someone whose signature it needs. A member without constitutional power needs no clause: their loss is repaired from above. This is F96's promise, a dead member never freezes the collective, carried from the keys to the rules.*

### 11. Tiers and clones

*Nobody loses their say by default (F103). Each part of a collective's agreement is changed by the power that owns it. Which power that is follows mechanically from the fields a clone changes, so every verifier reaches the same answer and the mark (rule 46) can be checked.*

**Tiers and areas apply to collectives only** (F103, scope): to a founding agreement carrying a key grammar, and its clones. A deal has none: every clone of it needs every party (rule 47).

*"Deals should be as simple as possible while remaining rich. Collectives is for when things get complicated." Two people who end up doing many deals together can found a collective.*

#### Rule 62 (was 44a). Every field belongs to one tier

Every field belongs to one tier.

| Field | Content | Tier |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Parties | Constitutional (membership) |
| 1 | The words: the constitution's and general words | Constitutional (Q2) |
| 2 | cMIPs, per task | Tasks 9 (condition evaluation), 10 (time reference), 11 (anchoring) and 14 (absence proof, F178; its cMIP named in the abandonment clause's key 3): judicial, outside the Agreements lane (Q3, Q15). Every other task: operational, in the lane of the task's layer where an area holds it (F106), and of each other layer the same specification serves (R4). A specification named for a judicial task, or in field 6, is named for no other task and as no extension (Q20, Q25) |
| 3 | Keepers | Judicial |
| 4 | Signing rule, or a clone's mark | None: each version states its own (Q9) |
| 5 | Clone rule | Constitutional |
| 6 | Time reference | Judicial |
| 7 | Stakes | Operational |
| 8 | Split plan | Operational |
| 9 | Abandonment clause | Judicial |
| 10 | *Withdrawn* (step 12b, OF20 c): once the concurrency rule; rule 90's default stands; never reused | — |
| 11 | Parent | None: every clone names its own |
| 12 | Key grammar | Constitutional |
| 13 | Arbitrators or verifiers | Judicial (Q3) |
| 14 | The split service's grant: the designated pointer (F248), the first of the split cMIP's addresses (F250) | Judicial (the stated exception: operational in kind, able to move money) |
| 15 | Extensions | Operational, per extension, added or dropped: in the Development lane, and in the lane of each layer the extension declares in its specification (F106; Development field 10, Q18) |
| 16 | Succession plans | Judicial |
| 17 | Refund terms | Operational |
| 18 | Constitutional change rule | Constitutional |
| 19 | Areas | Constitutional |
| 20 | Area words, per area | Operational, in the area whose id it carries (Q2, Q32) |
| 21 | Chain of judgment | Judicial (F121) |
| 22 | Departed members entry | Constitutional (F121); it records only who is departed, the stake being in field 7 (F124 N5) |
| 23 | Forked from | None: founding terms only (F121) |
| 24 | Release rule | Operational, in no area: the clone rule, and every owner's signature (F124 N8) |
| 25 | *Withdrawn* (F128): once the relays (F126); never reused | — |
| 28 | Fees (F213) | Operational: an area may reach it apart from field 8 (`field-ref` `[0, 28]`); otherwise the clone rule |
| 29 | The split service's backups, and what counts as a failed attempt (F249, F250) | Judicial, as field 14: able to move money (reading, the build's: they sat in field 21, judicial, before F250) |

An area's field references (area key 4) name only operational fields and tasks: fields 7, 8, 17 and 28, and tasks of field 2 other than 9, 10, 11 and 14 (fields 10 and 25 are withdrawn).

*Text only, no rule changed: field 28 (F213), field 10's withdrawal (step 12b) and task 14's judicial standing (F178) were decided before this draft; the table now says so.*

*Field 4 belongs to no tier (Q9): in a clone it is a mark of fact, written fresh each time; founding terms are signed by everyone (Q11), and the tiers govern only later changes. Who signs what for the collective is its areas, which are constitutional. Each area carries its own words, changed by its holders with it, so the words never go stale and no area's holder rewrites the constitution's (Q2).*

#### Rule 63 (was 44b). What a clone changes

*In short: a clone's changes are found by comparing it with its parent, field by field; each change belongs to its field's tier and, when operational, to an area or to none.*

A clone changes a field when the field's deterministic encoding in the clone differs from the parent's, or the field is present in one and absent in the other.

Fields 2, 15 and 20 are compared entry by entry: by task number, by extension and by area id. Each differing entry (added, removed or replaced) is one change.

Fields 4 and 11 are never compared, nor field 23: a clone never carries it ("Terms").

**A change of field 16 that only takes, for each party the clone takes out of the parties, the seat part of its succession plan (keys 2 and 3), is no change of its own** (F124, M1). This holds where the plan stays for its stake part, or goes where it has none, and every other plan stays as it was. It goes with the removal, as the party's areas do (Q21).

Each change is in the tier of its field or entry (rule 62). An operational change lies:
- for an entry of field 2: in the parent's area reaching that task's layer as a whole, its lane (F106); where no area reaches that layer, in the parent's area whose field references name that task, `[1, task]`; where none does, in no area, under the clone rule (B4). Where the specification the clone names for that task is also named in the clone for a task of another layer, it lies in that layer's lane too (R4). A clone naming one specification for a judicial task and for another task is invalid, whatever its mark (Q20);
- for an entry of field 15: in the parent's area reaching Development, and in the area reaching each layer that the extension added or dropped declares in its specification (Development, field 10). Dropping an extension needs the same powers as adopting it (Q18). A clone naming as an extension a specification it names as a judge is invalid, whatever its mark (Q25);
- for an entry of field 20: in the area whose id it carries;
- otherwise: in the parent's area whose field references name that field.

The part of a change lying in a layer that no area of the parent reaches, and any other operational change, lies in no area.

#### Rule 64 (was 44c). Which powers a clone needs

*In short: the powers a clone needs follow from its changes alone.*

Which powers a clone needs, from its changes alone:

1. If any change is constitutional: the constitutional change rule, `[0]`. Where some change is also judicial, the judicial tier's rule, `[4]`, is needed too (F122): the version stays a draft until both are met, its mark naming both. *Without this, two members of three could change a judge by adding a one-word change to the constitution. The constitutional change rule still changes every other tier alone; a judge needs every member as well.*
2. Otherwise:
   - the power of each area in which some change lies, `[2, area]`, one for each such area (Q7);
   - the clone rule, `[1]`, if some operational change, or part of one, lies in no area, or if the clone changes nothing;
   - and the judicial tier's rule, `[4]`, if some change is judicial (rule 67, F121).

   So a clone that changes an area and something outside every area needs both, its mark naming both (Q12).
3. A clone that executes a party's succession plan may instead name that plan, `[3, party]`, alone, where rule 83 allows a seat to pass automatically (Q14). It may do so provided it changes nothing but what the plan gives (Flaw B14):
   - the party out of the parties (field 0), its seat successors in;
   - the party out of the holders of every area it held (Q21), nothing else in the areas changed;
   - **the seat successors in the party's place in every holding of the key grammar (field 12), every threshold unchanged**;
   - the executed plan dropped from field 16, every other plan as it was;
   - where the party held the chain key alone (`[0, party]`), a succession plan for the seat successor in the executed plan's place, naming at least one seat successor of theirs, as rule 59 requires (Flaw B17). The seat successor signs the clone as the plan's signer, so that plan is signed by them in the same clone.

   *A key grammar's holdings name only parties, and in most collectives every member holds a share of the chain key, so a seat could never pass without the keys; a plan left in field 16 for someone no longer a party would make the clone invalid. The thresholds stay as the members chose them (F96); the rotation declaring the clone re-deals the keys (rule 83). What this leaves open, a verifier refuses rather than guesses ("Open in this draft").*

A clone needing several powers comes into force only when each is met, all at once or not at all (Q7).

*The clone rule does not reach into an area, and one area's holders do not reach into another: an area's power is exclusive, held by its holders or by the narrower rule that governs it. Its holders, and the rule they act by, change only by a constitutional change (Q5).*

#### Rule 65 (was 44d). Counting a power

*In short: each power is counted among the parties whose voice remains; a party that has left still counts for what it signed before it left; and when fewer voices remain than a number asks for, all of them together meet it.*

**Who each power is counted among:**
- the constitutional change rule: among the parent's parties, under the parent's field 18, or every party when the parent has none;
- the judicial tier's rule: among the parent's parties, every one of them (F121);
- the clone rule: among the parent's parties, under its field 5;
- an area's power: among that area's holders in the parent, at least its threshold of them.

In each, only parties whose voice remains are counted. This is judged on the collective's own sequence at the act, or, for a clone, at the record or rotation that puts it in force ("Made before, made after", F109). Left out are:
- a party whose resignation (rule 70) or declaration with outcome 0 (rule 89) the collective registered on a line before it;
- for one area, a holder whose stepping down from it (rule 71) the collective registered on such a line.

They stay left out under every parent descending from the agreement in which they lost that voice.

**A party is named again only by a constitutional clone** made after the version in which it lost that voice, naming it as a party or as that area's holder, which it signed by a signature act the collective did not place before the line registering its departure. It counts again under that clone and its descendants (B10). *An ordinary clone that only copies the list of parties or of an area's holders restores nobody: otherwise a holder who stepped down would count again by signing a change of the area's words.*

**A party that lost its voice still counts, for a clone or an act, through a valid signature act on it placed before the collective's line registering its departure** ("Made before, made after", F109, Q23, Q28):
- for that clone or act alone, it counts as a voice that remains, so the rule is met or not as written, its number counted with that voice among the others, never lowered by the departure;
- it counts so even where the record or rotation putting the clone in force does not name that signature among those completing it, and is then a voice that did not sign there (B3);
- a declaration never undoes a clone that a record or rotation put in force before the line registering it, whatever version of the clause or of the agreement it names.
