*"Her position at time of signing is valid. She was still acting. Whether it gets approved now depends on collective rules." Under three of three, a member who signed a clone and then resigned still leaves it needing both others: three voices count for it, and three signatures are needed; a rotation naming only the two others' signatures does not meet it (B3). Under every party, it needs everyone who has not left, the early signature standing among them. "Before she left" reads "placed before the collective's line" (F109): the record came first, or the collective acknowledged her signature as it arrived (C2).*

**When fewer voices remain than a rule's number asks for, all the remaining voices together meet it; where enough remain, the number stands as written** (F105, flaw C). This holds for every number an agreement states, and for the judicial tier's "every party":
- the constitutional change rule;
- the clone rule;
- an area's threshold, one a clone left above the area's holders included (Q26);
- an abandonment authority that is a threshold of the other parties (rule 85).

When no voice remains, nothing meets the rule. An area left without a holder, or listing none (Q21), stands frozen, as in rule 71, until a constitutional clone gives it a holder.

*A number can outlast the voices it counts: "three of three" would still ask for three after one voice is removed, and F105 would unfreeze nothing. Read this way, a number never freezes a rule, it matches what "every party" already does, and it keeps the founders' number wherever enough voices remain. A lone survivor still holds the keys and the constitution only under F96 and F105: the key grammar's thresholds are cryptographic, and this rule never lowers them.*

#### Rule 66 (was 44e). Protections still hold

Protections still hold. Whatever power brings a clone into force, it cannot:
- reduce a stake without its holder's signature (rule 48), a departed holder's included (rule 68), nor lower what it pays a departed holder without that holder's signature (rule 48; new in draft 11, F270);
- nor change the judicial tier without every member, a version changing the constitution included (rules 64.1, 67; F122).

*Stated cost (F103): by default a member who is present but unwanted cannot be removed; the others can leave, each alone (rule 70), or use the abandonment clause where it applies. A group that wants expulsion writes a constitutional change rule below everyone at founding, where every founder signs it; even then, removal takes the voice, never the stake (Q6). A member who dies or vanishes never freezes the constitution: the abandonment clause every collective carries can remove their voice (rule 61, F105).*

#### Rule 67 (was 46a). Protected clauses: the judicial tier

*In short: the clauses that decide disputes change only with every member, and there is one version of them for everyone.*

Collectives only (F121). **The judicial tier changes only with the signature of every member whose voice remains, and there is one version of it for everyone.** The judicial tier is:
- the abandonment clause;
- the succession plans;
- the keepers;
- the split service and its backups (fields 14 and 29, F250);
- the time reference;
- the arbitrators or verifiers (field 13);
- the condition, time reference and anchoring cMIPs (field 2, tasks 9, 10 and 11) (Q3, Q15), and the absence-proof cMIP (task 14, named in the abandonment clause's key 3; F172, F178);
- the chain of judgment (field 21).

*The concurrency rule (field 10) is withdrawn (step 12b, OF20 c): rule 90's default stands. Task 14 is judicial in its own entry ("Tasks", F178). Text only, no rule changed.*

A clone changing any of them names the judicial tier's rule, `[4]`, in its mark, with every party of the parent whose voice remains, counted as rule 65 says. A version that also changes the constitution names the constitutional change rule beside it, and needs both (rule 64.1, F122).

A clause naming only the member a version removes goes with the removal (F124, M1). This is only the seat part of their succession plan, which needs no judicial power (rule 63). The plan for their stake stays with the stake, as a departed holder's plan (field 16 may carry it; it names stake successors only). No other judicial clause changes that way.

A departed holder's stake plan changes only with that holder's signature too (F125).

Every member, and every departed holder (rule 68), is judged by the version in force.

*"It's the joint signature that matters" (Nobody, allegedly). If a member who did not sign a change kept the version they signed, two members could hold different judges, and where one joined after a change the other never signed, no judge would be signed by both: nothing would say who decides between them. With one version for everyone, every dispute has one judge both signed. Arbitrators, the condition cMIP and the anchoring cMIP can each decide who wins a dispute, so a majority still cannot pick a friendlier judge after the fact (Q3).*

*Stated cost (F121): one member can hold a change back ("leverage is part of life"); a judge that dies cannot trap the collective, the chain of judgment taking over (rule 35); and a holdout makes a standoff: "if a group within the collective wants change A but another group refuses it, it creates a standoff, which is good. Communication becomes the only channel to solve it, with a collective fork as an option to settle it" (rule 91).*

In a deal no clone completes without everyone, so the question never arises (F107).

#### Rule 68 (was 46b). Departed holders

*In short: a member who leaves keeps their stake but loses all control; their stake is treated exactly like everyone else's.*

Collectives only (F121). A collective's departed members entry (terms field 22) records only who left, without voice. Their stake is in the collective's stakes (field 7), which decide what every holder is paid (F124, N5).

**A departed holder has no control and no veto:** it is counted in no change rule and no area, and the collective's current rules and judges apply to it. Its one signature is on what lowers its own stake or its pay (rule 48), never on anything else.

Its stake never shrinks without its signature (rule 48). A clone that lowers its share in field 7, or drops it, is a draft until that holder signs it (rule 45), as for every holder. **Nor is what its stake is paid lowered without it:** a clone that lowers its stake's part of the split plan, or adds or raises a fee its stake bears, is a draft until it signs (rule 48; new in draft 11, F270). *This protects what it is paid; it gives it no say in anything else.*

The stake is a share of all the collective's income, not only of what was made while it was a member.

Every term applies equally to every stake, member or departed:
- a term may not treat a departed holder's stake worse than a member's of the same size;
- a fee raised for the departed is raised for the members;
- a fee paid to a service the members own is public. *Client conformance* (F140): a verifier cannot see who owns a service, so the collective's client MUST disclose it to the members.

The stakes decide every holder's money (field 7, Q8, F124 N5), and every payout matches its stake exactly, every fee alike for every stake ("Split plan and split", rule 27, N10). Equal treatment is what following the stakes with uniform fees produces, not a separate test, and any deviation, either way, breaks the plan. *A member who wants to give income to another transfers part of a stake (N10).*

Every split is delivered to every holder it pays and names each fee and who received it (rule 20, Q9), so a fee to a service the members own is seen by everyone it is taken from.

Unpaid shares stay open debts (Money rule 16) until the holder can receive, for instance after setting up on a new rail the collective chose.

*"A person who keeps stake in a collective accepts that they have zero control over decisions. If I think the collective won't survive, the incentive to sell the stake is there. It's a stake in a collective as a whole; it simply defines his share of income" (Nobody, allegedly). A rail swap is the collective's decision: "to get paid, past members have to accept the decision and set up accordingly if they wish to get paid". Equal treatment, from company law, makes any creative accounting "legible and poetically public".*

*The core checks the split, not the split plan, whose format (field 8) is still open.*

### 12. Members joining and leaving

#### Rule 69 (was 37). Changing members

*In short: changing who is in a collective takes a rotation of its keys plus a clone of its agreement that changes the constitutional tier. A rotation that gets this wrong breaks the collective until a rollback brings it back.*

Changing members means a rotation to new keys plus a clone of the founding agreement. The rotation declares the complete clone as the agreement the collective lives under. It names the signature acts that complete the clone, and places each at its own position. The clone counts as complete there only with those ("Collectives in the identity chain", "Made before, made after", Flaw M).

Membership is constitutional, so the clone needs the constitutional change rule (rule 64): by default, every party whose voice remains at the rotation. A member whose resignation (rule 70) or declaration with outcome 0 (rule 89) the collective registered on a line before the rotation is no longer counted.

The rotation is a line by its kept tips: a change of members, or of an area's holders, that it declares takes effect for the collective's acts there.

A clone that takes a party out of the parties takes it out of every area's holders too. An area it leaves with no holder is written with an empty list of holders, and stands frozen until a constitutional clone gives it a holder (rule 71, Q21).

A constitutional clone that removes a party who did not sign it, where the constitutional change rule allows that, takes that party's voice only. The party keeps every stake it holds (rule 48), as a departed holder, under the collective's current rules and judges (rule 68, F121) (Q6).

Any other change to the constitutional tier (the change rules, the key grammar, the areas) is likewise declared by a rotation before it applies to the collective's acts.

Each agreement the chain declares after the founding one MUST:

- be a clone of the agreement in force at the rotation (the one declared before it, or recorded since, rule 72), its parent (field 11) naming it;
- be complete with the signature acts the declaration names;
- change the constitutional tier.

Otherwise the collective's acts that need member signatures count for nothing: **the collective is broken** from that rotation on, until a rollback brings it back (rule 93, F185). So it is where a rotation removes the collective's Agreements declaration or carries one that cannot be read (RB4).

A rollback is the one exception to this rule: it is a clone of the agreement in force just before the broken act, and need not change the constitutional tier (rule 93).

*A rotation declares only a constitutional clone (B5): a clone changing only the judicial or operational tier is written by a record. And it declares only a direct clone of the agreement in force (B6): every other clone is recorded at once (rule 72), so there is never a clone in between, and one that nothing recorded would have no line at which its completeness was judged (A2).*

A rotation declaring nothing new carries the agreement in force forward (Flaw B1, "Collectives in the identity chain").

*Clones that change only the judicial or operational tier need no rotation (Q8): they change neither who holds the collective's keys nor who decides, so there is no old key to fence off. They are written on the collective's record by a record act instead (rule 72). The collective's key resembles a chain key more than a signing key in that regard.*

A departing member hands over nothing: the remaining members rotate to keys the departing member never held, and re-split the chain key among the members who stay or join.

#### Rule 70 (was 37a). A member can always leave alone

A member can always leave alone (Q6). A party to a collective's agreement gives up its voice by a resignation act (type 16), without anyone else's signature.

**The resignation takes effect for the collective at the collective's line registering it** (record field 3, F109). The collective's client draws that line in its next act.

From that line on, the party's signature acts count toward no change rule and no area of that agreement, or of any agreement descending from it. A signature of the party placed before the line stays valid, and still counts toward what it signed. For that clone or act alone, its signer still counts among the voices that remain, so the departure never lowers the number the rule asks for ("Made before, made after", rule 65, Q23, C2).

The party keeps its stake, and stays bound by what it signed; it is then a departed holder (rule 68, F121).

The remaining members then rotate the collective's keys (rule 69) and clone it without the departed party, which takes the party out of every area it held too (Q21). They record the departed party, with its stake in the collective itself (field 7), in the departed members entry (field 22).

Meanwhile, from the line on, each rule, and each area the party held, is counted among the voices that remain (rule 65, flaw C). An area left with no holder stands frozen from that line until a constitutional clone gives it a holder, as in rule 71. Every grant in it ends at that line (G2, F128), to be reinstated, where wanted, by cloning it at the refit.

A resignation counts like a loss (F96, F105): where the key grammar's recovery path is an escrowed share released by the abandonment authority, the authority may release it on a resignation as on a declaration of absence.

*Leaving needs no permission, and lets nobody remove anybody else; the collective that delays drawing the line only delays itself. Absence remains the abandonment clause's business, under the clause the member signed.*

**Leaving a broken collective** (F185). A member can leave a broken collective too (rule 93). During the broken stretch, a resignation, or a stepping down (rule 71):

- names **the agreement in force just before the broken act**;
- takes effect at the collective's next valid line: normally the rollback, which registers it ("Collectives in the identity chain"), or else the first record after the rollback that registers it (record field 3).

A record drawn in the broken stretch is no line and registers nothing (rule 93). A resignation naming the clone the broken act declared names an agreement never in force, and no line can register it.

**Stepping down during the broken stretch is treated as resigning** (F187, 8): a client allows it, and the rollback registers it. *A rollback whose clone names that member again as a holder of the area, and which they sign, gives the area back to them, as any constitutional clone they sign naming them does (rule 71, B10), so a client leaves them out of it.*

*A resignation cannot make the constitutional change rule impossible: a number is counted among the voices that remain, and all of them meet it where fewer remain than it names (rule 65, flaw C). Where a departure takes away a share of the collective's key that the rollback's rotation needs, the key grammar's own way through applies: a resignation counts like a loss (above). Neither case is new to a broken collective.*

**The last voice** (F185). *Client conformance:* where the member resigning is **the last voice that remains** in the collective, the client MUST say so in plain words before the resignation is signed. The member is the last voice that remains where no other party of the agreement in force (during a broken stretch, of the agreement in force just before the broken act) keeps a voice there as rule 65 counts it. The client says that the collective's works will be frozen as they stand, and nobody will be able to change, release or move them. Leaving stays the member's alone: the client says it, and never blocks it. *"It acts as an: entering danger zone" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**The last constitutional voice** (RB5). Where the constitutional change rule names some parties only, and the member resigning is the last of them whose voice remains while other parties keep voices in their areas, the client MUST likewise say so before signing, in these words (BQ5):

> **"You are about to break the collective's constitutional layer. Once you leave, nobody will be able to change its rules again. The other members keep acting in their areas."**

Its constitution freezes as it stands, and nobody can add or remove a member, redraw an area or change a rule of the constitution again (rule 65). It is not a broken collective (rule 93), and has no rollback. *"The monarch has no partner or no heir" (Nobody, allegedly). "This could become a deliberate act under rare scenarios" (Nobody, allegedly): a collective may choose to freeze its own rules for good, and the warning makes sure it is chosen.*

**Counting the voices that remain for these warnings, the client reads too the resignations the other parties have published that no line has registered yet** (F187, 3). Agreements count a resignation only once a line registers it (rule 65), and during a broken stretch nothing registers, so voices leaving from separate devices would otherwise each be told the others remain.

*A client names in the resignation the agreement Agreements find in force. Where it cannot read Agreements (its relays and homes away), it says that the resignation names its own copy, which may not be the one in force, and that no last-voice warning can be given (F187, 4).*

*Stated cost: when the last voice has left, the collective's works freeze as they stand (F185).* Its works stay published and claimed, and every former member keeps its stake as a departed holder (rule 68). Nothing about them can be decided any more, a release to the public domain included, since a collective owner releases only by its own rules (N7) and no voice remains to meet them (rule 65). A broken collective can no longer be rolled back. No rule prevents it: *"Someone with stakes in works worth keeping does not resign" (Nobody, allegedly).*

*Whether money under offers already published keeps flowing through a split service the founding terms name by a grant tied to no area is still to be checked ("Open in this draft").*

#### Rule 71 (was 37b). Leaving an area

Leaving an area (Q13): giving up an area changes the constitution at that level. It is a constitutional clone (rule 64), declared by a rotation (rule 69). It comes about in one of three ways:

1. the holder leaving nominates a replacement, and the constitutional change rule approves it or not;
2. the constitutional change rule proposes a replacement, and the holder leaving approves it;
3. the constitutional change rule redraws that area's rules entirely.

The replacement signs the clone too (rule 45, Q11). Until one of them completes, the holder keeps the area.

*Mechanically the first two are one clone, signed by the leaving holder and meeting the constitutional change rule, started from either side; the third needs no signature of the holder, since an area's holders and rules change by a constitutional change (Q5).*

**Stepping down at once.** A holder may instead give up an area at once, alone, by a resignation naming that area (type 16, field 1): *"I'm done, won't do anything anymore."*

**The resignation names the drafts its signer had signed and leaves behind** (field 2; F207). A version it names never gives the signer the area back, whichever line puts it in force. A draft it fails to name does: the signer's stated cost (F195). *Client conformance:* the holder's client names them, as for a resignation.

The stepping down takes effect for the collective at the collective's line registering it (record field 3, F109). From that line on, the holder's signature acts count toward nothing in that area, in that agreement or any descending from it. A signature of the holder placed before the line stays valid, and counts as rule 65 says (Q23, C2). The holder keeps the rest of its voice, and its stake. The collective then makes the constitutional change for that area by one of the three ways.

**Meanwhile, where other holders of the area remain, they carry on** (Q17), counted as rule 65 says. The area's threshold stands where enough of them remain, and all of them together meet it where fewer remain than it asks for. Grants issued within the area keep backing acts. A departure that leaves an area held leaves its grants in force; only a revocation ends them (G2, F128).

*Rarely, no holder of the area remains.*

**An area with no holder remaining is frozen** until that change. Its acts made after the line that froze it count for nothing (Q17), and every grant issued within it ends for good (Q22). The line that freezes the area is:

- the collective's line registering the departure of its last holder whose voice there remained (a resignation, a stepping down, or a declaration removing that holder's voice, rule 89); or
- the rotation declaring a clone that leaves the area with no holder (A1).

**A departure that empties an area ends every grant in it: a decision ending powers** (G2, F128).

- An act signed with such a grant's key that the line's history holds was done within the grant's powers, and binds.
- One racing it (neither citing the other) is void, the ending winning (the tie rule).
- Every act after it is void.

No suspension, and no undetermined act. *"An empty area should be treated as an emergency. End it. The copy still exists, cloning it takes two seconds if need of reinstating" (Nobody, allegedly).*

A void act binds the collective only where the collective itself, by an act of its own key that counts, acknowledges, pays on or imports it, adopting it (rule 76, A6; reading).

*Client conformance:* a member's client shows an empty area as an emergency.

The change ends the freeze once a constitutional clone is declared that changes that area's entry of field 19, the entry with its id (Q32), so that it has a holder whose voice remains (Q21).

**At the refit, the new holders may reinstate any ended grant by cloning it** (Flaw N, G2). A reinstatement is a grant (type 9):

- whose field 7 names the ended grant, and whose fields 0 to 6 are that grant's;
- with a grant key of its own (field 9);
- signed by the collective and completed by signature acts of the refitted area's holders, meeting the area's own rule, its threshold as for any grant within it (rule 74, Q27);
- accepted by the grantee.

It is a new decision, judged at its own place like any act the area reaches (rule 60). It counts only when made after the line at which the new holders took the area, since before it the area has no holder to complete it. One reinstatement for each grant, never one for several, and nothing is reinstated automatically (Q22).

A signature act naming the ended grant itself reinstates nothing: it is placed at that grant (C1), before the freeze, where the new holders held nothing.

A reinstated grant backs acts as a grant within the refitted area, and only within that area's reach (rule 80), from its own place on. **It takes nothing on**: the acts under the ended grant stay as the ending left them (G2).

*With grant keys, the grantee's acts are on the collective's own chain, so the line that emptied the area places them as it places every act, and nothing is left to decide later.*

*Stepping down thus works as resigning does (rule 70): neither can be played against the other, and one holder cannot stop the co-holders' work at will, nor keep an area frozen by withholding the signature its refit needs. A grant is the area's, not one holder's: a grant made within the area before the stepping down, even one to the holder who stepped down, keeps backing acts while the area runs, until its remaining holders revoke it (rule 74).*

*"If her departure freezes a layer, the collective will organize quickly to fix it." Payments to the collective's payee pointers still arrive on their rails; only the collective's own acts in that area wait.*

#### Rule 72 (was 37c). Every complete clone is written on the record at once

**Every complete clone is written on the collective's record at once** (F106, flaw D). A clone that changes the constitutional tier is declared by a rotation (rule 69). Every other complete clone of a collective's agreement is written, at once, by a record act (type 17) that the collective signs in its own sequence.

For an act the collective's key signs, the agreement in force is the one the chain declares at the act's binding. Where a valid record made since that declaration counts for the act, the agreement in force is instead the clone named by the latest such record, the one furthest along the agreement chain. Such a record may be under the same binding, or under an earlier one that a rotation declaring nothing new carried forward (Flaw B1). Which records count:

- for a record itself, only the records before it (B2);
- for an action, the records under an earlier binding carried forward, and, under its own, the records its history holds and those before them (F127, rule 58). A record it never cites decides nothing for it, a concurrent one included, save by the tie rule ("Made before, made after", 7).

**Records on concurrent lines are a fork of the agreement chain, settled as rule 90 says** (A4, Q38). Where two valid records of different clones of the same parent, neither before the other, both count for an act (a record after both, or an action whose history holds both), it is judged under the parent itself, the earlier version staying in force, until a clone of either branch, recorded after both lines, resolves it, as rule 90 says (B11). *The concurrency rule (field 10), which once selected a clone here, is withdrawn (step 12b, OF20 c): text only, no rule changed.*

*Where the collective's records were drawn concurrently, which of them its key holders drew first does not decide. Records drawn one after the other are no fork: the second names a parent no longer in force, and puts nothing in force.*

A record of a clone counts only if:

- that clone is complete, judged with the signature acts it names (A2); and
- its parent is the agreement in force for the record itself, or one of the clones a fork leaves for it (B11).

So records follow the agreement chain one clone at a time. A rotation's declaration of a new agreement supersedes every record before it, and a rotation declaring nothing new supersedes none (Flaw B1).

*MOR has no clock, so an operational clone that nothing writes on the collective's record could not tell which of the collective's acts it governs: a payment cMIP the Money lane adopted would not govern the collective's acts until the next rotation, and acts of a cMIP adopted for a task would fall in no lane, counting on the collective's own signature, without the lane's holders (flaw D). The record fixes the moment in the collective's own sequence, with the signing key; only clones that change who decides need the chain key's ceremony (Q8).*

*Until the record exists, the collective's acts are judged under the agreement before the clone: a delay, visible, which the key grammar's holders could already cause by withholding the collective's signature from any act.*

### 13. Grants

#### Rule 73 (was 38). Grants

Grants delegate authority with limits: signing new deals, managing specific existing ones, or acting for an identity or collective (posting, publishing, spending up to a limit).

A grant is authority to act in the grantor's name without a stake, the employer-and-employee kind. It confers no voice in the agreement.

**A collective's grant hands its grantee a grant key** (F128). An agent acting for a collective works under a grant, holding a key of the collective scoped to it, never the collective's own keys. What it signs with the grant key is the collective's own act, within the grant's reach, on the grantee's strand of the collective's actions chain, citing the grant (grant field 9, rule 58).

The grant is a decision naming the grantee's own identity, which signs to accept it.

Grants are revocable at any time, by a revocation: a decision ending powers, which removes the key (type 10).

During a collective's broken stretch its grants are quarantined: a grantee's act of the stretch counts for nothing. The grants work again after the rollback (rule 93, RB1), for an act citing the rollback or a later decision (BQ1).

*Stated cost (F128): a stolen grant key acts within its scope until revoked; the collective's identity gains a scoped kind of key (Identity, "Scoped keys").*

**Every identity grants keys the same way** (F128, extended; H2). A person's grant hands its grantee a key of the person. What the grantee signs with it is the person's own act, citing the grant on the person's chain (the person's identity as chain).

**A person's grant and its revocation are everyday acts, signed with the person's own signing key: adding or removing a grant key needs no rotation and no chain key.**

They are public (reading: "scoped and visible"; a person has no members to seal them to, and everyone who checks the grantee's acts must read them). A private one backs nothing.

A rotation of the grantor judges its grant like any act signed with the key it replaces (Identity rules 15 to 17). A grant the rotation does not keep is void, and its key with it. So a thief who stole the signing key and granted itself a key loses it at the owner's next rotation.

A person's grant reaches the kinds it names (a MIR's layer, or a type of a specification). Naming none, it reaches every act but a decision (reading).

*Stated cost: as with every act of the signing key, a thief holding it can also revoke the owner's grants until the owner rotates and grants again.*

**Grants to a split service.** In a deal, each payee grants the split service its own grant key, scoped to that deal (F128, extended; F129):

- the grant names "this agreement" by null;
- the deal lists it in field 14, one per payee;
- it counts once the deal exists;
- its key signs only receipts for money coming into the deal, never one whose payer is the service, nor a split's payout ("Grant (type 9)", H4, H5).

The same holds for every grant to a service the deal's chain names to take over, one per payee (F130, H6), and, under the same limit, for a collective's grant to its split service (F130, H7).

#### Rule 74 (was 38a). A grant never reaches beyond its issuer's power

A grant never reaches beyond the power of whoever issued it (F103).

The holders of an area may grant within it. The collective signs a grant naming the area (field 5) and the acts it reaches (field 6), within the area's reach. It counts only with valid signature acts naming it by the area's holders, meeting its threshold as rule 65 counts it, as for any act the area reaches (rule 60). It is judged by the area it names alone, whichever area reaches grants as acts.

**So is a revocation of such a grant** (Q29): it counts only with valid signature acts naming it by the holders of the area the grant names, meeting its threshold as rule 65 counts it, whichever area reaches revocations as acts. So is a reinstatement (rule 71, Flaw N).

A collective's grant naming no area is judged as any other act of the collective, and reaches only acts that no area of the agreement in force reaches.

A grant whose field 6 reaches beyond its area is invalid, a reinstatement aside (rule 71).

*So a member holding an area can act alone there, under a grant to their own identity, and can hire others for it; and neither they nor the one who holds the collective's signing key can give anyone more than that area.*

#### Rule 75 (was 39). Strands

A collective's actions chain has a strand for each of its devices and for each grant key (F128).

A grantee's acts are its strand, each citing its previous act there by `prev` and its grant on the collective's chain.

*A grant key's strand is the collective's own, which every member's chain reading follows.*

#### Rule 76 (was 40). Revocation

Revoking a grant removes its key (F128, G1). The revocation names the grant, never a place on its strand (C6).

**An act signed with the grant key that the revocation's history holds was done within the grant's powers, and binds. One racing it, neither citing the other, is void, the ending winning (the tie rule). Every act after it is void.**

A revocation of a grant within an area is judged by that area alone (rule 74, Q29).

**The grantor may adopt a void act of a grant key as its own.** An act of the grantor's own key that counts (for a collective, an act of the collective's own key that counts) makes it bind when it does any of these:

- acknowledges it;
- cites it (F131, IT2a: for a collective, its history holding the act, reading U3);
- pays on it;
- imports it

(F76, A6, reading, F128; F129, H3 and reading 6).

**Once adopted, no ending racing the adoption voids it** (F131, IT2a). A revocation, or a departure emptying the area, that does not hold the act still ends the grant for every act after it, never for one the collective already took on.

**Another grantee then manages it under its own grant** (scope 1, naming it), with its own grant key (F129, H3).

*A collective that took the money cannot later say the deal never happened. The adoption is the grantor's own act, never the grantee's: no act signed with a grant key adopts anything (reading 4, confirmed).*

#### Rule 77 (was 41). Import

The grantor's import act lists acts of an ended grant it adopts into its own chain. Its format is still open: a verifier refuses it.

There is no handover (F129, H3). A grant key's acts are the grantor's own, on its chain, so there is no branch to move them from. A void one counts only if the grantor adopts it (rule 76), and another grantee then manages it under its own grant.

*Adoption already does the job, visibly; a second mechanism would have no purpose of its own.*

Type 11 is the import alone.

#### Rule 78 (was 42). Acknowledgements

An acknowledgement of a grant key's act counts as an adoption only when it is an act of the collective's own key that counts (rule 76), wherever it is stored (C6).

**That means done: sealed to every member and on the collective's chain (rule 57). An Identity witness act (type 15), on neither chain, adopts nothing. The collective adopts by an action of its own key citing the act. No act of a grant key adopts anything (rule 76, F142).**

#### Rule 79 (was 43). Fate of acts

An act of a grant key that a decision ending its grant does not hold does not bind the collective: it is void, unless the collective adopted it (rule 76).

Decisions ending a grant are: a revocation, a departure emptying its area, a fork, a closing.

**Counterparties: wait until a later act of the collective cites the act before performing** (F128). From then on the act is adopted (rule 76, F131 IT2a), and no ending racing that citation voids it.

**The promise, restated** (F131, IT2b): safe once cited, against everything but the collusion of an ending's signers.

*Stated cost:* a fork or closing whose line is drawn early on purpose, before the citing act, leaves both out of its history, and both are void (the tie rule). Without a clock, a line drawn early on purpose cannot be told from one drawn before the later acts existed, and every cure would let something published after a fork reopen it, against IT1. It needs every signer of the ending to collude against the counterparty: each conforming client refuses to sign it ("Made before, made after", client conformance). And it stays fully legible: the signed act, the collective's citation of it and the ending leaving them out stand as evidence for any outside court.

#### Rule 80 (was 44). Evaluation

A client evaluating an act signed with a grant key follows its binding to the grant (F128).

The act binds when all of these hold:

- the grant counts (its area's holders met, or the founding terms carry it), and its grantee signed to accept it;
- the act is signed with the grant's key, cites the grant on the collective's chain, is done (rule 57), and lies within the grant's reach (rule 74);
- no decision ending the grant fails to hold it (rules 71, 76, 91), or, where one does, the collective adopted it (rule 76).

An act beyond the grant's reach is not backed by it, whatever its strand.

*Every act of a grant key binds or does not.*

### 14. Succession

#### Rule 81 (was 48a). The core gives the language, not the answers

The core gives the language, not the answers.

A party may attach a succession plan to an agreement, as part of its signed terms. A plan names:
- stake successors, each with a share of the party's stakes (summing exactly);
- seat successors, each with a voting weight, entering automatically or with the members' approval.

Stake and seat successors may be the same identity or different ones.

Changing a plan takes a clone, which needs every member's signature, the party's included (rule 67, F121).

#### Rule 82 (was 48b). Triggers

A plan executes when the party triggers it while alive, or when the authority named by the abandonment clause declares the party absent.

*Death is a complex issue; the core leaves it to the parties, their named authorities and verifying modules.*

#### Rule 83 (was 48c). Execution

Succession is a clone: the new terms carry the stakes and seats the plan gives, and any position attached to a seat is defined at that moment. In a collective, a change of seats changes the parties, which is constitutional.

**A seat passes automatically** (plan key 3, value 0) **only if every party whose voice remains signed that version of the succession plan** (Q14). That is: each of them signed a version of the agreement, in the chain up to the parent, whose field 16 carries that party's plan exactly as the clone executes it.

The clone's mark then names the plan, `[3, party]`, with the seat successors entering as its signers. It comes into force on the trigger (rule 82) and their signatures, with no other power, provided it changes nothing but what the plan gives, as rule 64 says:
- the party out and its seat successors in, among the parties and in its place in every holding of the key grammar, every threshold unchanged;
- the party out of every area it held (Q21);
- the executed plan dropped (Flaw B14);
- and, where the party held the chain key alone, a plan for the successor in its place, naming their own successor, signed by the successor in that clone (Flaw B17, rule 59).

*Consent given in advance, by everyone still counted (rule 8); a plan that some members cloned without the others is not that version, so it cannot slip a stranger into the constitution through a judicial clause. A plan changes only with every member (F121), so a plan some members clone without the others never comes into force at all.*

Otherwise the seat successor is a nomination, approved under the constitutional change rule (rule 64). That approval is counted among the voices that remain once the absent party's voice is removed (rule 89, F105), as in rule 71. The successor signs the clone too (rule 45).

A stake passes by succession as planned: it is property, not a seat.

**A seat passed by succession carries membership, never the areas its holder held** (Q19). The seat carries the party's voice in the constitution and the change rules. The clone that completes the succession, automatic or by nomination, takes the party out of the holders of every area it held (Q21). Each such area is refitted as rule 71 says:
- its remaining holders carry on meanwhile (Q17);
- an area left with no holder is written with an empty list of holders and stands frozen, its grants ending (Q22, G2) and reinstated, where wanted, by cloning at the refit, until a constitutional clone gives it a holder, which may be the same clone where the successor is a nomination;
- an area left with fewer holders than its number keeps that number, met by all who remain (Q26).

*An area is a job the collective gave a person; a plan is the party's own, and letting it hand out that job would reach into the constitution. The successor is free to be nominated for it.*

In a deal, the clone needs every party whose voice remains (rule 47).

For a collective, the members then rotate and re-split the chain key to include the new key holders, using the grammar's recovery path where a share is gone for good. The succession clone changes the parties, so a rotation declares it (rule 69), and that rotation re-deals the collective's keys to include the successor (F96, F105, B14).

#### Rule 84 (was 48d). Fallback

Where no plan exists, the identity-level succession declared in a rotation (Identity) applies, and otherwise the abandonment clause.

### 15. Abandonment

#### Rule 85 (was 49). Clause

An abandonment clause, signed into the agreement, names who decides absence and which outcomes may follow.

Every collective carries one that covers each party with constitutional power (rule 61, F105). A deal may carry one or not. *In a deal, a party's death leaves the agreement running as written, with succession plans to step in, and locks nobody out of an identity.*

The authority MUST be an identity:
- the keeper's operator;
- a threshold of the other parties, counted as rule 65 says, so it never asks for more of them than remain. In a collective, they are counted among the voices that remain at the collective's line registering the declaration, or at the recovery rotation where the declaration takes effect there (rule 89, C7): the same place every other departure is judged (Q37);
- or a named third party.

**In a deal, the authority is one identity: a party, a keeper's operator, or a collective, never a threshold of the other parties** (Flaw B19). Parties who need to decide together form a collective, and name it.

Where a threshold of the other parties needs more than one of them, one signs the declaration and the others add signature acts naming it, as for terms. It counts once the required number have signed (B15, "Abandonment declaration"), placed at the collective's line, or named by the recovery rotation where the declaration takes effect there (Flaw B18).

An anchoring cMIP is named as the time reference the authority judges absence against, never as the authority.

Absence means absence from duty: no act by the party on the agreement, for a period measured on the agreement's time reference.

*Client conformance* (as Text rule 5a; F178): **before a party signs terms carrying an abandonment clause, its client MUST show in plain words who may declare it absent, with which outcomes, and whether an absence-proof cMIP stands between that authority's word and its stake.**

#### Rule 86 (was 50). Liveness

A party with nothing else to sign shows presence with a liveness act.

*Presence is shown, never proven by time in the core: whether a party is gone is the judgment of the authority the members chose, or, where their clause names one, of an absence-proof cMIP (rule 87, F172).*

#### Rule 87 (was 51). Declaration

A declaration is a signed act by the authority the clause names, stating the agreement, the clause, the party and the outcome (type 13, B12).

The core checks:
- that it is signed by that authority (for a threshold of the other parties, by one of them, completed by the others' signature acts until the number is met, B15);
- that the outcome is one the clause allows;
- that field 1 names the version the party declared absent signed last ("Abandonment declaration");
- that, where the party's succession plan names a stake successor, it states neither outcome 1 nor outcome 2 (rule 89; new in draft 12, F279).

**Otherwise the declaration is the authority's judgment, a stated cost the party accepted by signing the clause, and a contest shows it** (F172).

Where the clause names an absence-proof cMIP (key 3), the declaration counts only if that cMIP accepts it (task "Absence proof"; F172).

A declaration moves nothing by itself. Its outcomes take effect only through what is put in force under it: a record or rotation in a collective, a clone in a deal (rule 89). The checks are judged at the point where that act uses it. A later act of the party, or a later contest, never undoes what was put in force ("Made before, made after", Q28; F172).

The clause applied is the one in force, of the agreement it names, for every party (rule 67, F121): a party whose voice was removed before a judicial change is judged by the clause in force, as every departed holder is.

#### Rule 88 (was 52). Contest

A party who believes they were wrongly declared absent contests it with a contest act.

*Client conformance* (RB3): **a declaration naming a party MUST always be shown to that party, broken collective or not, with the way to contest it.**

*A contest shows a declaration, it does not void it (F172): a wrongly declared member regains their voice only by a later version of the agreement naming them. "The absentee still has a possibility to void their statement of absence. But, the absentee has to be informed somehow" (Nobody, allegedly); under rule 88 a contest shows, it does not void. The contest act's format (type 14) is written (BQ4): it names the declaration it answers and is signed by the party declared absent, which shows presence; the declared member's client lets them sign it ("Contest (type 14)"). A declaration counts only if public or addressed to the member it names (F189, 8), so it reaches them through the very act that uses it.*

#### Rule 89 (was 53). Outcomes

**A declaration moves a stake only by succession** (new in draft 12, F279): the stake, and its pay with it, goes to the stake successors the holder's succession plan names, in the plan's shares (rules 81 to 83). A clone carrying exactly that, put in force under the declaration, needs no signature of the absent holder. Abandonment never lowers what a stake is paid while the stake stays with its holder (rule 48).

*"Abandonment is meant to protect operational flow. It is not meant to change stakes or even pay. What abandonment can trigger in that regard is succession. Where the stake and associated pay go to the successor"; "It feels right this way, it matches how things run out here" (Nobody, allegedly).*

The outcomes of a declaration may be combined:
- **voice removed** (F105), described below;
- stake redistributed among remaining holders in proportion, **only where the holder's succession plan names no stake successor** (new in draft 12, F279);
- stake transferred to parties named or defined by role, **only where the holder's succession plan names no stake successor** (new in draft 12, F279);
- obligations redirected or held;
- agreement closed.

Outcomes 0, 3 and 4 are unchanged. A declaration stating outcome 1 or 2 against a holder whose succession plan names a stake successor is refused (rule 87; new in draft 12, F279).

Voice removed: the party is no longer counted in any change rule or area of the agreement, or of any agreement descending from it (rule 65). This holds:
- in a collective, from the collective's line registering the declaration, or from the recovery rotation where the declared party is the one who would draw that line, the rotation that removes them (C7, B16);
- in a deal, from the declaration on ("Made before, made after").

A clone put in force before then is never undone, whatever version of the clause or of the agreement the declaration names (Q28). "Before then" means by a record or rotation of a collective before its line, or, in a deal, by signatures a keeper recorded before recording the declaration.

When the voice is removed in a collective, the remaining members rotate its keys and clone it without the party (rule 69), the recovery path standing in for a share that is gone.

*A declaration is not a clone: it needs no change rule, only the authority the party signed. Removing a voice never takes the stake; that takes the party's succession plan or, where it names no stake successor, outcome 1 or 2, which the party also signed (new in draft 12, F279).*

## Part three: rare cases

### 16. Forks of records

#### Rule 90 (was 47). Forks of records

*In short: where a collective's rules let parties act separately, the agreement must say how a split record is settled; if it does not, the version before the split stays in force until a later change, recorded after both branches, chooses one.*

Collectives only. Where a change rule of a collective (a clone rule, or an area's power) lets parties act independently, the agreement must define how forks are settled.

If it does not, the parent stays in force until a clone of either branch resolves it (B11). Only a clone written on the collective's record after both lines (a record that each line of the fork is before) resolves it. It comes into force there, and the other branch is not in force.

A clone of a branch is a clone of that branch's latest clone, as records follow the agreement chain one clone at a time (rule 72):
- where a branch moved on, a clone of its first clone resolves nothing;
- where a branch forked again, no clone resolves the fork through it.

Two clones recorded on concurrent lines are such a fork, settled the same way (rule 72, Q38).

*A clone names one parent (field 11), so "a clone naming both branches" could not be written; recording a clone of one branch after both lines shows the collective saw the fork and chose, with no new format. "Its latest clone" was found by the ordering simulation: read as a clone of the branch's first clone, a resolution would silently drop a clone already recorded on that branch. A clone of one branch recorded after only one of the lines resolves nothing, and two clones of the branches recorded on concurrent lines leave the fork as it was.*

### 17. How a collective ends

#### Rule 91 (was 47a). How a collective ends: four shapes

*In short: a collective ends in one of four ways: a group leaves and the collective carries on (A), it forks into successors (B), it is abandoned but keeps earning (C), or it is dissolved and closed (D).*

Collectives only (F121). *"A fork forces a whole lot of decisions to be taken again" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**A broken collective cannot fork or close before it is rolled back** (rule 93, RB6): while it is broken, no fork or closing counts, whatever line it names (F189, 7). "If they want to push an official act, they have to fix the issue first" (Nobody, allegedly).

- **A. A group splits off, with the powers to do so.** This is an ordinary membership change (rule 69). The collective re-signs, by a constitutional clone and a rotation, and keeps its identity. The members who leave become departed holders, recorded with their stake in the departed members entry (field 22, rule 68), keeping it under equal treatment, the pointer check and no veto. They may found a collective of their own. Members without the powers can still leave one by one (rule 70) and found something new together.

- **B. The fork proper,** where both groups want changes and disagree.

  Each side first founds its successor: a new collective (its own genesis and keys) whose founding terms have that side's members as parties and name the original in "forked from" (field 23), a back-link only, never a parent (F124, N4).

  **A fork act (type 19) names the successors by their identities, and, signed by the members on its sides with their own identities under the constitutional change rule, closes the original in Agreements** (N1). By default that is every member, a silent member being the abandonment clause's business. Where the founders set a lower rule, the fork may complete without a member, who is "forced to either fork or leave": a member who signs no side has no seat in any successor, and is a departed holder of each at their percentage.

  From the fork's line (N2), anything the original's keys sign counts for nothing in Agreements, whatever its key grammar's holders do. A rotation of the original is optional cleanup.

  *Two fork acts signed concurrently on the same agreement, neither naming the other, are concurrent acts on its chain (rule 6): the status quo stands, and neither closes it (N4), until one names the other. **A complete fork or closing is final** (F131, IT1): a later ending of the same collective, naming it, counts for nothing. A later fork of a successor is a new fork, of the successor, in sequence.*

  Once the fork is complete:
   - **Ownership.** The fork act transfers the original's stake in each work, as a stake transfer, to the successors, in the shares it names (field 5). By default the shares are in proportion to the stakes of the members on each side in the original itself (field 7, Q8), divided in millionths, leftovers by largest remainder as rule 9, ties ordered by the hash of the fork act with each side (F162). Where the original carries no stakes in itself, each member counts alike (N3: "no agreement means equal weight"). Each successor then owns its share outright and runs it: pointer, rails, split service. The work's fingerprint never changes.
   - **Departed holders keep their percentage in every successor,** and their stake follows into the successors' future works, a stake in each collective as a whole. Each successor's founding terms keep every departed holder of the original, and every member who signed no side, at the same share of all its income, in its stake in itself (field 7) and its departed members entry (field 22); the members divide the rest. *Example: four at 25%, one of them departed; sides taking two thirds and one third: the departed holds 25% of each, the others 37.5% in one or 75% in the other.* *"If too many departed members still own shares, the incentive is to create an entirely new collective instead of forking."*
   - **Debts.** The fork cites the original's action head, and hands out every obligation in that history, each to one successor or to several jointly, or it does not take effect (F124 N13, F127; "Fork (type 19)").
     - The successor collective signs for each debt assigned to it, by its own signature act on the fork, so members are never made personal debtors of what the collective owed.
     - **A complete fork is never undone over a debt**, nor by a later ending (F131, IT1): a debt published after it was in its history, and handed out, or outside it, and void (the tie rule).
     - No successor owes a debt the fork did not hand it (F127).
     - A later fork of a successor hands out, in turn, what an earlier fork handed it, where its own signature on that fork lies in its history (F125, F126).
     - An obligation signed by a collective binds it once done, like any act (rule 57, F126, F128): sealed to every member, and on its chain. A debt sealed to the creditor alone binds no one, its signer included, so hiding gains nothing.
     - Publishing the debt is the creditor's choice and protection (client conformance), and no validity rests on where an act is stored (F128).
   - **Grants.** Every grant of the original ends at the fork, the old split service's included (F124, N14). **Every grant key ends at the fork's line** (F128), as when a departure empties an area (rule 71). Each successor grants, with a grant key of its own, what it wants. An act of a grant key in the history the fork cites, or that the original adopted before the fork, binds, as a debt. Any other is missing from the ending's history, and void (the tie rule, F127): its counterparty deals again with a successor.
   - **Money after it.** Payment follows the work's current claim (N14): the work carries nothing and never changes; its claim names the successors as owners, so routing follows to their own pointers and split services.
     - The fork act withdraws the original's open offers: a wallet reading Agreements sees them withdrawn, and pays a successor's offer instead.
     - **A payment that reaches the old split service after the fork** (a wallet not reading Agreements paying a withdrawn offer, a push rail that cannot refuse) **names no claim, or the original's superseded claim, and the original's actions chain never recorded it before the fork: it is no purchase, and is owed back to the payer as a refund** (rule 12, F126, F127 W2; the old service's grant key ended at the fork's line, F128).
     - A sale the original's chain recorded in the fork's history stays a purchase.

     *Client conformance:* the old split service, its grant ended, issues no payment request. *Stated cost: a wallet reading only Money does not see the withdrawal, and could still pay an on-chain address of a dead service; that money is owed back to it.* *"The work is the point of payment… why does it need the old one?" (Nobody, allegedly).*
   - **Defaults.** Whatever the fork act does not settle falls to these defaults.

- **C. Actively abandoned,** where everyone agrees the collective has ended but its works still earn: a plain collective, nothing new. It keeps its rules, changes rails, renews offers and pays every holder until winding it down makes sense. *"Plain collective enough, it's only how it is used by stakeholders that changes."*

- **D. Dissolution,** where the collective dies. Its works are sold (stake transfers, rule 10) or released to the public domain (rule 55), then it closes. **A closing act (type 20), signed under the constitutional change rule like the fork act, ends a collective that holds nothing and owes nothing.** After its line, the collective's keys count for nothing in Agreements (F124, N9). A collective cannot close while it owes anything (F125, D5): one that cannot pay stays open, abandoned, its debts visible, until each is paid or its creditor releases it (rule 92). **New in draft 11 (F262):** this is the whole of liquidation: ordinary sales, transfers and releases before the closing, the closing naming no stake. **New in draft 11 (F265):** an absentee's money share follows the collective's own rules (its split plan, its absence clause, its terms); what they could not deliver stays visible as an open obligation wherever they sent it, and it does not keep the collective from closing. The closing names nothing for it; field 4 stays for money owed back (rule 93, "Closing (type 20)"). Batching them is a client's or a cMIP's convenience, never a second way in the core.

*"A fork closes the original" holds for shape B only. "Then maybe it is best no group keeps the original hash": a lone holdout keeping the identity, its history and its pointers while everyone else leaves was the cost that closing the original removes. Leaving costs the vote and the right to act, never a stake unless it is sold.*

#### Rule 92 (was 47b). The creditor's release

*In short: only a creditor can forgive a debt, by signing a release.*

The creditor's release is a Money act (F125, F126). **A creditor may end an obligation owed to it without full payment** by a creditor's release (Money type 4), for instance against stakes or a partial payment.

Only the creditor signs it. No rule, majority or court can discharge a debt for it, and a release signed by anyone else ends nothing.

A collective creditor signs it by its Money lane alone (F126, E2): forgiving a sum only gives up money, and the release is a Money act, which that lane reaches (rule 60).

A release given against future terms (a share of future income, stakes) is also a deal: an Agreements agreement of its own, signed by the collective's Agreements lane, which the release names among what it was given against (Money release field 1). The agreement binds on its own, and the release counts whatever it names, its field 1 a record only, never checked (F126). *Plain forgiveness needs the Money lane; forgiveness traded for future terms needs both lanes, each for its own act.*

Once it counts:
- the obligation is owed by nobody, the debtor or any successor owing it (rule 91);
- it ends the whole debt, whatever was paid toward it: to forgive part, the debtor signs a new obligation for the rest and the creditor releases the old one;
- it counts wherever a verifier holds it, published or not, even before the debt it ends binds (E1).

**A closing checks that every debt is paid or released** (rule 91, D).

*"That 10% is what changed everything, that's a complex deal that continues in the future. That is law" (Nobody, allegedly).*

*Bankruptcy, walked through (Nobody, allegedly): a collective that cannot pay keeps its debts open and visible and cannot close; its assets can go to its creditors by sale or by stake transfers, debt turned into ownership; its members are not personally liable (N13); what was missing was discharge, and MOR has no court: the creditor's release is the creditor's own word.*

### 18. A broken collective

#### Rule 93 (was 37d). A broken collective, and its way back

*In short: a collective is broken when a rotation moves its keys without putting a valid agreement in force, so Identity and Agreements no longer agree on who is in (F185). While it is broken, everything done in its name counts for nothing, and it keeps only what its rules before the break allowed. The way back is a rollback: a later rotation returning to the agreement in force just before the break.*

**What breaks a collective.**

A collective is broken when its keys moved by a rotation whose declared agreement fails rule 69, or whose Agreements declaration is missing or cannot be read (RB4). Identity counts the rotation, so the keys have moved, but:

- the clone it declares is not a clone of the agreement in force; or
- it does not change the constitutional tier; or
- it is not complete with the signature acts the declaration names (its mark false, rule 46, included); or
- the rotation declares founding terms; or
- it removes the Agreements declaration; or
- it carries one in neither form.

Agreements then finds no agreement in force, and Identity and Agreements no longer agree on who is in. That rotation is **the broken act**: the first such rotation after the last agreement in force.

*Breaking the collective is what makes such a move worthless. If Agreements ignored the failed declaration and carried the earlier agreement forward, whoever holds the chain key could rotate a member out of the keys at no cost.*

**The broken stretch and quarantine.**

The broken stretch runs from the broken act to the rollback. Every act of the collective bound to a link of its identity chain in that stretch **stays shown and counts for nothing in Agreements**, during the stretch and after it. This covers the broken act, the rotations after it other than the rollback, records and the clones and departures they write, agreements, releases, debts, grants, and every other act in its name.

Such an act places no signature at all, a member's signature act on a declaration of absence included ("Made before, made after", 2; F187, 7). Its records register nothing (record field 3): only the rollback's registration counts.

The broken collective is quarantined (RB1, RB2). *"A broken collective is broken until fixed. Quarantined as much as possible" (Nobody, allegedly).*

**A grantee's act of the broken stretch counts for nothing**, like the collective's own. The grants issued before the broken act work again after the rollback, which restores every condition as at the act before the break: a grant issued, revoked or ended by an act of the stretch is as it was before the break (rule 73).

A grantee's act is of the stretch where:

- neither the broken act's history nor that of an act of the collective's own key before it holds it; and
- it cites neither the rollback nor any later decision of the collective (rule 58).

**After the rollback, a grantee's act counts only once it cites the rollback or a later decision of the collective** (BQ1). An act citing nothing newer than the time before the break counts for nothing, and is signed again; this includes one made just before the break that no act of the collective took in.

*Client conformance:* a grantee's client cites the latest decision of the collective it holds in every act it signs. *MOR has no clock of its own, so an unanchored act proves where it stands only by what it cites; an anchor can place an act, and the rule asks for the citation all the same, as the safer path.*

**What others relied on during the stretch (a debt, a sale, a receipt) counts for nothing, for good, and is signed anew after the rollback**, never adopted by citing it (RB2; rule 76 does not reach it).

**What the collective keeps.**

**The broken collective keeps exactly what its rules before the break allowed** (BQ2). The test: would it have been valid under the collective's rules as they stood just before the break? If yes, the collective keeps it; if not, it does not.

A payment is judged by the offer it names, not by when it was paid (BQ3):

- A sale under an offer made before the break is kept, even received during the stretch. What the collective owes for it (a key delivery) is done once the rollback lets it act.
- A debt paid to the collective under an agreement made before the break is kept, and stands as paid.
- A payment naming nothing, a tip, is kept.
- **A payment under an offer made during the stretch is no purchase and is owed back to its payer** (Money rule 10a), unless the sale is signed anew after the rollback, by a receipt of the collective for the same payment. Until settled it stands as a visible open obligation.

A payment shown only by its payer's claim is judged the same way, by the offer it names. The rail's own time (a block, an invoice) is supporting evidence where it exists, never the rule.

*An offer made during the stretch is a version of the collective's agreement descending from the one in force just before the broken act and not from the rollback's clone (reading, to confirm); a deal the collective signed during the stretch never came into force, its signature counting for nothing. "We need to assume that the collective has an internal problem that freezes changes to external interactions" (Nobody, allegedly): the collective's own acts of the stretch still count for nothing; what it keeps is only what others did within rules that already held.*

**Money owed back, and how it is repaid.**

**Money owed back from the stretch is repaid like any debt** (QF5, F190), with no new act:

- by a payment naming what it repays: the receipt's field 5 or the claim's field 4 naming a receipt or claim of the payment owed back (wording settled by the project lead under the delegation of technical wording);
- made to its payer;
- proven by either side's record, the payer's receipt or the collective's claim (double entry, Money rule 10).

Repaid in full, it is no longer owed.

**It follows the debt rule exactly** (QG2; Money rule 14). The repayment goes:

- to the refund rail the payer's own claim on the payment names (key 7), where it names one, counting up to the payer's vault limit for its unit and, above it, only once it reaches the payer's vault (new in draft 11, F255; rule 11; built);
- otherwise, to the pointer Money rule 14 selects from the payer's own acts on that payment, or the payer's pointer in force where they hold none.

A payment to the payer's vault always counts, and the payer's own receipt counts wherever the money went.

*Why: a repayment counted at any pointer the payer ever published would let a collective repay to an old pointer a thief controls and be cleared while the payer lost the money; Money rules 14 and 15 exist to stop exactly that for every other debt. Stated cost: the collective looks up the payer's current pointer, as any debtor does.*

**A refund owed to a bare key** (Money rule 10a) **is repaid once the collective's claim carries the rail's proof that the money reached where the claim signed with that key said** (QG1): the same double entry, a claim with a rail proof showing the money arrived.

**Money owed back to nobody stays open and visible, but does not block a closing** (stated here, not in Money: F242, M7). This is a payment that committed no key, whose refund Money rule 10a can pay to nobody. The closing act names every such obligation it leaves open (QG1; the closing's field 4), as a settling version names every tip it drops.

*Why: one anonymous tip with no key, received in a broken stretch, kept the collective from ever closing, a protection trapping every member with nobody gaining from it. Stated cost: a collective may close while visibly owing an anonymous payer who could never have been paid.*

**Money owed back to a payer who gave no address** (F197): a payer with no payee pointer, vault, or refund rail on a claim of theirs. Before closing, the collective sends the payer **a notice sealed to their identity, with a deadline on a time reference** (type 24), asking where the money should go.

- If the payer names an address in time, they are paid.
- If not, the collective may close, the debt named in the closing act, visible and unpaid.

A collective may also set the amount aside with a named holder that outlives the closing and pays the payer when they turn up (a keeper or custodian role, for a cMIP to define). The core allows it, shows it beside the debt in the closing act, and requires neither the holder nor any one notice period.

*Why: a stranger who pays a penny from a fresh identity under an offer of the stretch, and publishes nothing, would otherwise freeze the collective's ending for good. After a closing the collective's keys count for nothing, so nothing could be paid later: the notice comes first. "The notice demonstrates good faith. The degree of that good faith depends on the conditions of the notice" (Nobody, allegedly).*

*A payer who committed only a one-time key cannot be reached by a notice (F197; QH5, "Open in this draft").*

*The network records and shows the debt and the payer's claim; it cannot force a payment back, as with every obligation in MOR.*

**No fork or closing while broken.**

**A broken collective cannot fork or close before it is fixed** (RB6). A fork or closing made during the broken stretch counts for nothing, then and after the rollback (rule 91). And while a collective is broken, no fork or closing counts, whatever line it names, the last good link before the broken act included (F189, 7).

Money owed back from the broken stretch is a debt for a closing's "owes nothing": the collective cannot close until those payers are settled. Two kinds are the exception, and the closing names them as left open (QG1, F197):

- money owed back to nobody;
- money owed back to a payer who gave no address and let a notice's deadline pass.

*"It's an emergency situation with emergency rules. All that happened under emergency has to be settled to the respect of the rules before the emergency is lifted" (Nobody, allegedly). A closing on an old line was a quiet way out of the refunds owed for the stretch.*

**The rollback.**

**The way back is a rollback.** A later rotation of the collective declares a rollback: a clone

- whose parent (field 11) is **the agreement in force just before the broken act**, the one in force at that rotation (rule 72); and
- which names the broken act, which it voids and which stays shown ("Collectives in the identity chain").

Nothing is erased. The identity chain carries on: the rollback rotates from the keys the broken stretch holds, as any rotation does (Identity). The agreement chain forks from before the broken act, and the broken clone stays on its own branch, never in force.

*"The chain needs to fork from one act before the corrupted one, all while showing the corrupted one. I'm not saying mechanically that is what happens, but that is what the collective faces, a rollback" (Nobody, allegedly).*

The power it needs. **A rollback needs the constitutional change rule of the agreement in force just before the broken act** (rule 64), counted among the voices that remain at the rollback (rule 65). By default that rule is every party whose voice remains. So a party no longer blocks it:

- whose departure (a resignation, rule 70, or a declaration with outcome 0, rule 89) the collective registered on a line before the broken act; or
- whose resignation, stepping down from an area, or declaration of absence the rollback itself registers.

*"Whatever was in vigor just before the broken act needs approval of all involved before the broken act. The broken act is simply void … and always referenced," then: "the constitutional powers need to be met."*

Where every voice that remains for that rule has resigned, nothing meets it (rule 65): no rollback is possible, and the collective stays broken (rule 70, "The last voice").

The rollback's mark (field 4) names the constitutional change rule, `[0]`, with its signers. Where the clone also changes the judicial tier, it names the judicial tier's rule, `[4]`, too, every party whose voice remains (rules 64.1, 67). It names nothing else, whatever its changes: a rollback may change nothing, or may redo correctly the change the broken act failed. *Its mark is checked against these powers, not those of rule 64.2, so a rollback copying its parent unchanged is no false mark (rule 46). Rule 69's "MUST change the constitutional tier" (B5) does not apply to it: a rollback is a rotation by its nature, since only a rotation fences off the broken stretch's keys.*

What it registers. **The rollback may register a declaration of absence made during the broken stretch** under the clause of the agreement in force just before the broken act, exactly as a record does outside the stretch (RB3), so that a member who dies or vanishes during the stretch does not block the way back. *A faction that is the absence authority could already declare a member absent falsely with no break: the break adds steps, not power; the new risk was attention, which rule 88's client conformance closes.*

**The rollback is a line**, by its kept tips, as every rotation is ("Made before, made after"): its clone is in force for the collective's acts after it, and the resignations it registers take effect there.

In everything else a rollback is a clone like any other:

- complete only with the signature acts its declaration names, counted at the rollback (rules 45, 46, 48);
- every party it adds signing it (Q11);
- every constitutional voice covered (rule 61);
- its key grammar valid (rule 59).

What it may never do. **The rollback's clone is never the clone the broken act declared**, which stays on its own branch, never in force (F187, 5): a clone of the same rules, signed afresh, may redo the change.

A rotation in the broken stretch that names another act as the broken act, or declares any other agreement, or a rollback that is not complete, is part of the broken stretch, and a later rollback still names the first broken act.

After a rollback, a later rotation that fails rule 69 breaks the collective again, and the next rollback names that rotation.

**A rollback voids only an act Agreements read as broken, never a valid one** (F185). A rollback naming a rotation that met rule 69, or declared where the collective is not broken, puts nothing in force. Being a rotation declaring an agreement that fails rule 69, it is itself a broken act: the attempt breaks the collective, visibly, and is repaired by a rollback to just before it.

*Repair, not undo: a complete ending is final (IT1), and a signature placed counts. A break made on purpose reaches back no further than the agreement in force just before it, which holds every valid decision made until then; a person has no rollback.*

*Client conformance:* **a client MUST NOT propose a rollback unless Agreements read the collective as broken** (F185). *A client that does not comply makes its user's attempt fail as above, visibly, at the attempters' cost.*

*Stated cost: the hold-out (F185). A member whose signature the rollback needs can ask a price for it; it is the veto it has over any constitutional change, worth more on a broken collective.*

*Stated cost: break and hold (F185). Holders of enough of the chain key can break the collective on purpose and refuse the repair, freezing a decision they dislike while staying in; they could already leave it unable to act before F185, and the break stays shown.*

**Technical breaks, and a first agreement that fails.**

**A rotation that removes the collective's Agreements declaration, or carries one in neither form, is a broken act too**, a technical one, with the same way back (RB4).

**A collective whose very first agreement fails has nothing to roll back to: it is simply refounded**, as a new collective, with no rollback. *Stated cost: small; a new collective has no history, works, money or followers yet.*

### Made before, made after

*In short: MOR has no clock, so "before" and "after" are always read on one chain. For anything a collective does, that chain is the collective's own: never a member's personal sequences.*

*MOR has no clock, and every identity, a collective as much as a member, may keep several sequences at once, one per device (Envelopes, "Sequences"). A member's own sequences cannot settle what happened first: members keep as many as they like, forget some, and nobody else can see them all. Envelopes' own principle settles it instead: every "before" in the core is judged inside one named chain, by reference to a specific act (Envelopes, "Chains", rule 3). For anything a collective does, that chain is the collective's own (F109).*

*A collective's sequences form two chains (F127). Its decisions (genesis, rotations, records, fork, closing) are its lines, each citing the action head it saw by the tips it names. Its actions (every other act in its name) are the acts of its devices' sequences, each citing its previous act there. Each action also cites, in `objects`, the decision it acts under, and the heads of the other devices it knows, so that the next act joins the heads where several devices signed at once (rule 58). A sequence is one device's strand of the actions chain; a join ties strands together between lines. "The chain's role is to make acts unmissable, not to confirm them."*

**For anything done in a collective's name, "before" and "after" are judged only on the collective's own sequences, never on a member's personal sequences** (F109).

This holds for every event that changes who may act for the collective:

- a member's resignation or stepping down (rules 70, 71);
- a declaration removing a voice (rule 89);
- a member's own rotation (Identity);
- a clone put in force (rules 69, 72); and
- the freeze of an area (rule 71).

Each takes effect for the collective's acts at a line. A line is a record (type 17) or a rotation of the collective. It names the latest act of every sequence the collective keeps other than its own (record field 1; a rotation's kept tips, Identity).

1. **The collective's own acts.** An act of the collective counts as made before a line when the line's history holds it (F127). That is, when it:

   - precedes the line in the line's own sequence;
   - lies in the ancestry of a tip the line names, proved by position and running summary as for a rotation's kept ancestry (Identity); or
   - is reached from there through what those acts cite on the collective's chain, the tips of a record so reached included.

   For a rotation, its kept tips alone count, by Identity's rule.

   Every other act of the collective counts as made after it, whenever it was really made. That includes an act on a branch the line left out, by mistake or on purpose; an offline device's act its drafter had not heard of; and a fork made later from an act before the line.
