```cddl
  0 => hash,                ; the agreement of the collective in which the member gives up their voice
  ? 1 => uint,              ; stepping down: the area given up, by its id in that agreement (Q32); absent: the whole voice
  ? 2 => [+ hash]           ; the drafts its signer had signed and leaves behind, ascending, none twice (F207)
}
```

Signed by a party to that agreement, with its own key. Like a signature, its inside names the agreement as both chain and predecessor, `[[agreement, agreement]]`, and it is never a fork.

Only a party to an agreement founding a collective may resign or step down. A deal is left only as its terms provide.

What it gives up:

- Without field 1, it gives up the signer's voice in that agreement and in every agreement descending from it by clones (rule 70).
- With field 1, it is a stepping down. It gives up only the signer's place among that area's holders, in that agreement and its descendants, until a constitutional clone refits the area (rule 71). The signer must hold that area there.

It takes effect for the collective's acts at the collective's line registering it (record field 3). It never takes effect at its place in its signer's own sequences, which are not read ("Made before, made after", F109).

**A resignation, or a stepping down, names the drafts it leaves behind** (F207). A version it names never brings its signer back, nor, for a stepping down, gives the signer the area back, whichever line puts it in force. What she signed while present still speaks where C2 counts it.

*Client conformance:* her client names the drafts when she resigns or steps down. *Stated cost (F195):* a draft it fails to name brings her back.

*This replaces QH4's reading (the version the registering line puts in force); the order lives inside one act, read on the collective's own chain (F109).*

**A resignation or a stepping down is spent once its signer comes back** (F189, 1; QF6; F195). Its signer comes back by any later version they signed that names them again, as a member, or as holder of the area, whether or not the resignation was ever registered.

- "Named again" is by a constitutional clone (B10: never an ordinary clone that only copies the list), signed by a signature act of theirs that is valid.
- "Later" is descending from the agreement the resignation names: the only order MOR can see between the two acts.

Once spent, it is still on the record and visible, but no line registers it, by a record or by a rollback. So it can never take a voice or an area its signer kept or came back to.

*A member who means to leave signs a new resignation after the last version they signed.*

*Reading, to confirm (QH4): the version a line registering a departure puts in force, whose signature the departing member placed before that line (C2; F187, 6), never brings them back: it is the version of their leaving, not of a return.*

### Record (type 17)

*In short: a record is the collective's everyday line. It writes a new clone of the collective's agreement on the record, or registers members' departures and rotations, or both.*

```cddl
record-payload = {
  ? 0 => hash,              ; the complete clone of the collective's agreement it writes on the record
  ? 2 => [+ hash],          ; the signature acts that complete that clone (A2); present exactly when field 0 is
  1 => [* kept-tip],        ; the latest act of every other sequence the collective keeps
  ? 3 => [+ hash]           ; what it registers (A1, C3, C5): resignations and steppings down by parties, abandonment
                            ;   declarations removing a party's voice, and parties' own rotations, each by its act's id
}                           ; at least one of fields 0 and 3

kept-tip = [ act: hash, position: uint, summary: hash ]   ; as in Identity: the act's id, its position, and the running summary including it
```

Signed by the collective, with its signing key, as an everyday act in its own sequence. Its inside names, in `objects`, the clone as both chain and predecessor, `[[clone, clone]]`, as a signature does. A record naming no clone names the agreement in force for it the same way, `[[agreement, agreement]]`.

**A record is the collective's everyday line** ("Made before, made after"): field 1 names the collective's other sequences.

A record is a decision (F127). Its own sequence and the tips field 1 names are the action heads it saw, the previous decision among their history. It carries no other citation.

**A record must be done** (W3, F127). A record that is neither sealed to every member of the agreement in force for it, nor public, is no line: it puts nothing in force, registers nothing, and places no signature (rule 57). *One rule for every act in the collective's name, no exception by type. Example: a key holder draws a record registering a member's resignation, sealed to nobody: it is no line, and the member's voice remains until a record that is done registers it.*

**A record puts its clone in force with the signature acts it names, and only those** (A2).

- Each act field 2 names must be a valid signature act naming the clone (rule 6).
- The record places each at its own position.
- The clone counts as complete at the record only if those signatures meet the powers its mark names, counted as rule 65 says at the record.

A signature act naming the clone that field 2 does not name counts nothing toward it at this record, whenever it was made. *Naming the signatures keeps a record from being completed late, by a signature arriving after it, and then knocking a later record out of force; and it keeps a member who had already left from signing a clone and being counted (F109).*

A record puts its clone in force only if both hold:

- that clone is complete, judged with the signature acts it names; and
- its parent is the agreement in force for the record act itself (rule 72), or, where records on concurrent lines leave a fork for the record (rule 72), the latest clone of one of its branches, the record coming after both lines (rule 90, B11).

Records follow the agreement chain one clone at a time, never skipping it.

A record of a clone that changes the constitutional tier puts nothing in force: only a rotation declares such a clone (rule 69).

**A record's two parts count apart** (Q36). A record whose clone it does not put in force still registers what its field 3 names, and is still a line.

No area reaches a record, as none reaches a rotation. It is judged by the clone it names alone, whose own mark already carries the consent it needed. So the holders of an Agreements lane cannot hold up another area's change. *The key grammar's holders produce the collective's signature on it, as on every act of the collective; they could already withhold that signature from any area's acts, so the record gives them no new power.*

**Field 3 registers departures and rotations** (A1, C3, C5). Each act it names must be one of these:

- a resignation or stepping down (type 16) by a party of the agreement in force for the record;
- an abandonment declaration removing such a party's voice (rule 89);
- a rotation (Identity) of such a party's own identity.

A record naming any other act there counts for nothing.

Each takes effect for the collective's acts at this line ("Made before, made after"), whether or not the record puts its clone in force (Q36). A resignation spent by its signer's return registers nothing (type 16; F189, 1).

The record is not before itself. A clone the same record writes is judged with the departures it registers already in effect. So a signature it places counts for its departed signer only where an act of the collective placed it before (C2).

*Client conformance:* the collective's client registers a departure, and a member's rotation it learns of, in its next act, as it records a complete clone at once (rule 72). The rotation that follows a departure (rule 69) still fences off the departed member's key share (F100). *A departure needs nobody's permission; the line is the collective's, drawn with the signing key, and a collective that delays it only delays itself (F109).*

*The rare case, in detail:* when a declaration removes a voice. A declaration removes a voice when:

- it counts (type 13);
- its outcomes include 0;
- its party is a party of the agreement in force for the record; and
- the agreement it names is that agreement or one it descends from by clones (B12).

Where its authority is a threshold of the other parties, that number is:

- counted at this record, among the other parties whose voice remains there, with the resignations and steppings down this same record registers in effect, other declarations it registers not (rule 85, Q37; reading in "Open in this draft"); and
- met by its signer with the signers of the signature acts naming it that this record or an earlier act of the collective acknowledges (B15).

### Abandonment declaration (type 13)

*In short: an abandonment declaration declares a party absent, under the abandonment clause of an agreement the party signed, with the outcomes that clause allows.*

```cddl
declaration-payload = {
  0 => hash,                ; the agreement in which the party is declared absent
  1 => hash,                ; the clause's version: the version of that agreement whose abandonment clause (field 9) it applies,
                            ;   the last one, back from field 0 through its parents, that the party signed (rule 87); as the judicial
                            ;   tier changes only with every member whose voice remains (rule 67, F121), it carries the clause in force for every such party
  2 => hash,                ; the party declared absent: a party to the agreement of field 0
  3 => [+ uint]             ; the outcomes (rule 89), ascending, none twice, each one the clause allows
}
```

Signed by the authority the clause names (rule 85), with its own key (B12). Like a resignation, its inside names the agreement as both chain and predecessor, `[[agreement, agreement]]`, and it is never a fork.

Where it takes effect:

- In a collective, at the collective's line registering it (record field 3). Or, where the declared party is the one who would draw that line, at the recovery rotation: the rotation that removes the declared party (C7, B16).
- In a deal, at its own place, as the deal's keepers record it (Q28; "Made before, made after").

**A declaration counts only if it is public, or addressed to the member it names** (F189, 8). Public means its content key is on its outside. Addressed means sealed to that member among its recipients (Envelopes, `to`), with others if its signer wishes. One that is neither counts for nothing, so no line or rollback can register it.

*Why it needs no agreement on relays: a declaration has effect only where an act registers it, and that act names it; whoever checks that act, the member named included, must obtain the declaration to accept it, so it reaches the member through the very act that uses it, or that act does not count for them.*

*Client conformance, SHOULD: the declaration is published where the collective's clients look, and delivered to the member's inbox, so that they see it early; this is not the safeguard.*

A declaration counts only when it passes rule 87's checks:

- it is valid under Identity;
- the party is a party to the agreement field 0 names;
- field 1 is the last version of that agreement, back from it, that the party signed by a valid signature act, and carries an abandonment clause;
- every outcome it states is one the clause in force allows, the abandonment clause of the agreement field 0 names; and
- it is signed by that clause's authority (reading 7, corrected: the clause in force, for every party, one whose voice was removed before a judicial change included). That is the identity the clause names, or, where the authority is a threshold of the other parties, one of them, its number counted where the declaration takes effect (rule 85, Q37).

A declaration that fails any check, judged at the point where the record names it, is no declaration there: a record naming it in field 3 counts for nothing (record, field 3). A later act of the party never makes a record fail that passed where it was drawn (F172).

*The core does not check the period of absence (clause key 2): it is judged on the time reference, outside the core, and a party wrongly declared absent contests it (rule 88). Field 1 names a version rather than repeating the clause, so a verifier checks it against the version the party signed, and a declaration can never pick a friendlier clause. That version carries the one clause in force for every member whose voice remains (rule 67, F121); the field and its check stay as they are.*

*Where a party's voice was removed before a judicial change, the version it signed last may carry an older clause; the clause in force applies to it, as to every departed holder (reading 7, corrected).*

*The rare cases, in detail:* an authority that is a threshold of the other parties.

**Where the authority is a threshold of the other parties, one of them signs the declaration and the others add signature acts naming it** (type 1, field 0), as for terms. It counts once the required number have signed (B15): its signer and the signers of those signature acts, each among the other parties whose voice remains where it takes effect, meet the number counted there.

In a collective, a signature act on a declaration counts as the collective's own sequence places it ("Made before, made after", 2). Toward a line, it counts only where the collective placed it at that line or before:

- by an act of the collective acknowledging it before the line; or
- by the record registering the declaration acknowledging it (Envelopes, `acks`), as a record names the signature acts that complete its clone (A2).

A signature the collective places only after that line completes nothing there; a later record registering the declaration counts it. *Fixing the signatures at the line keeps a declaration from being completed late, by a signature arriving after its line, and changing what an earlier line did (A2's reason).* *Client conformance: the collective's client acknowledges the others' signatures in the record registering the declaration, or before.*

Where the declaration takes effect at a recovery rotation (C7), the collective cannot act without the declared member before it. So **the recovery rotation places the others' signatures: its Agreements declaration names them**, as it names its clone's (Flaw M), and they count at that rotation (Flaw B18, "Collectives in the identity chain"). A signature act on the declaration that the rotation does not name, and that no act of the collective placed before it, completes nothing there. *Client conformance:* the members who make the recovery rotation name in it the signature acts on the declaration that they hold.

In a deal the authority is one identity, never a threshold of the other parties (B19), so a deal's declaration is complete with its own signer.

### Collectives in the identity chain

*In short: a collective's own identity chain says which agreement it lives under. Its genesis declares the founding agreement; a rotation declares a clone that changes the constitutional tier; every other clone is written by a record.*

Each declaration sits in the declarations slot (Identity). Kind 0 is **the agreement the collective lives under**.

- A collective's genesis declares its founding agreement: `[AGREEMENTS, 0, agreement]`, the value being its id.
- A rotation declares, with the same kind, each complete clone that changes the constitutional tier, membership included (rule 69). Its value names the clone and the signature acts that complete it: `[AGREEMENTS, 0, [clone, [+ hash]]]` (Flaw M). Only such clones need the collective's keys to rotate (Q8).
- The recovery rotation of C7, where a declaration taking effect there needs other signatures than its signer's, adds a third element naming those signature acts: `[AGREEMENTS, 0, [clone, [+ hash], [+ hash]]]` (Flaw B18).
- A rollback (rule 93, F185) declares `[AGREEMENTS, 0, [clone, [+ hash], broken, [* hash]]]`. These are: the clone; the signature acts that complete it; the broken act (the rotation's id, as an act of the collective's identity chain); and the acts it registers, each by its act's id, possibly none. Those acts are resignations and steppings down (type 16), declarations of absence (type 13, RB3), and the signature acts on those declarations that their authority needs (type 1). Its third element, a hash and not a list, tells it from the recovery rotation's form.

Every other complete clone is written by a record act in the collective's own sequence (rule 72, F106).

**A rotation puts its clone in force with the signature acts its declaration names, and only those** (Flaw M), as a record does (A2):

- each must be a valid signature act naming the clone (rule 6);
- the rotation places each at its own position; and
- the clone is complete at the rotation only if it is complete (rule 45) judged with those signature acts alone, the powers its mark names counted as rule 65 says at the rotation.

A signature act naming the clone that the declaration does not name counts nothing toward it there, whenever it was made. *So no signature arriving after the chain key's ceremony completes the clone late, and the records made since are never judged anew. Identity is unchanged: a declaration's value is the higher MIR's to define (Identity rule 8b). The genesis declares founding terms, which exist only when every founder has signed them (Q11), and names no signatures.*

For an act the collective's key signs, the agreement in force is the agreement named by the latest declaration of kind 0 at the chain act its key is bound to. Where a valid record made since that declaration counts for it, it is instead the clone named by the latest such record (rule 72):

- for a record, a record before it;
- for an action, a record its history holds, or one before such a record (F127, rule 58).

Its areas are the ones that act is judged by.

**A rotation that declares no new agreement changes keys, not rules** (Flaw B1). Its declarations slot carries the Agreements declaration on unchanged, and the agreement in force carries forward, recorded clones included. The records made since the last declaration, under that rotation's binding and the earlier ones, keep counting. Only a rotation declaring a new value of kind 0 supersedes the records before it. *Otherwise a collective that adopted a payment cMIP by a record, then rotated to fence off a departed member's share before the refit was signed, would silently undo the Money lane's adoption for every act under the new key.*

*The rare cases, in detail:* a recovery rotation and a rollback.

**A recovery rotation places the signature acts on a declaration that its third element names, and only those** (Flaw B18), as it places its clone's (Flaw M).

- Each act the third element names must be a valid signature act (rule 6) naming an abandonment declaration (type 13) against a party that the declared clone takes out of the parties and that the collective's signing key cannot be produced without under the agreement in force at the rotation: the declaration that takes effect at this rotation (C7, B16).
- A rotation naming any other act there puts nothing in force, as one naming an act that is not a signature on its clone (Flaw M).
- The rotation places each at its own position. The declaration's number is counted there, among the other parties whose voice remains at the rotation, met by its signer with the signers of the signature acts placed at or before it (rule 85, Q37, B15).

*The declared member is the one who would draw the collective's line, so no act of the collective can place the others' signatures before the rotation; the rotation itself does, at the chain key's ceremony, so no signature arriving later completes the declaration there (A2's reason).*

**A rollback puts its clone in force as a rotation does, and registers departures as a record does** (F185, rule 93). It counts only in the broken stretch, naming as broken the broken act itself. Further:

- its clone's parent is the agreement in force just before the broken act;
- its mark names the powers rule 93 gives it; and
- the clone must be complete at the rollback with the signature acts its second element names, and only those. The powers are counted as rule 65 says at the rollback, with the departures registered on lines before the broken act in effect and none registered in the broken stretch.

Each act its fourth element names must be one of these:

- a valid resignation or stepping down (type 16) by a party of the agreement in force just before the broken act, naming that agreement or one it descends from by clones, a stepping down naming an area its signer holds there;
- a declaration of absence (type 13) with outcome 0 against such a party, under the clause of that agreement or one it descends from (RB3). Its authority is counted at the rollback, among the other parties whose voice remains there, the resignations and steppings down it registers in effect and other declarations not, as a record counts it. It is met by its signer with the signers of the signature acts naming it that the fourth element also names, which the rollback places at its own position, as a recovery rotation places those its third element names (Flaw B18);
- or such a signature act.

A rollback naming any other act there puts nothing in force. A resignation spent by its signer's return (type 16, F189 1) is valid there and registers nothing. *No act of the broken stretch places a signature (rule 93), so the rollback itself places the declaration's other signatures, as the recovery rotation does.*

Each takes effect at the rollback, its line, for the collective's acts after it. The clone the rollback declares is judged with them already in effect, as a record judges the clone it writes (record field 3). So a member whose departure the rollback registers is not counted for it. The exception: a signature of theirs on the clone that an act of the collective placed before the rollback counts there, for that clone alone, its signer still counted among the voices that remain, as at every other line (C2; F187, 6). *The resignations and declarations of the broken stretch cannot be registered by any record of the stretch, so the rollback registers them; otherwise one member who resigned, or vanished, and stayed silent would block the way back for good under "every party".*

### Notice (type 24)

*In short: a notice asks a payer whom a collective owes money back where the money should go, with a deadline.*

```cddl
notice-payload = {
  0 => hash,                ; the payment owed back: one receipt or claim of it (rule 93)
  1 => [ hash, any ]        ; the deadline: a time reference, and the point on it
}
```

*Most of the time a collective never sends one.*

A collective owing money back from a broken stretch to a payer who gave no address (no payee pointer, vault, or refund rail on a claim of theirs) sends that payer, before it closes, **a notice sealed to their identity** (or public). The notice asks where the money should go, with a deadline on a time reference (F197).

Signed by the collective, with its own key. Its inside names the payment as chain and predecessor, `[[payment, payment]]`.

If the payer names an address in time, they are paid. If not, the collective may close, the debt named in the closing act, visible and unpaid (rule 93; the closing's field 4).

Its conditions (the deadline, how it was sent) are visible, so its good faith can be judged: *"the notice demonstrates good faith. The degree of that good faith depends on the conditions of the notice"* (Nobody, allegedly).

Whether the deadline has passed, with no address given within it, is the time reference's answer, which a verifier states as it states every time reference's answer: the core reads no time reference. The core requires no one notice period; it shows the one a collective chose.

*The rare case, in detail:* a notice to a one-time key (F208). F197 applies to a payer who committed only a one-time key. A bare signing key cannot be sealed to, so the notice is public, naming the payment (mechanic, the build's), and its holder finds it by looking. If the key's holder names where to pay in time, it is paid. If not, the closing names the debt, visible and unpaid, and does not wait.

*Stated cost:* the payer's privacy: the key is linked to this collective.

*A payer who committed only a one-time key cannot be reached by a notice (F197).*

### Fork (type 19)

*In short: a fork divides a collective among its members into new collectives, one per side. Every member it lists signs it on their own identity chain. It must hand every debt binding the collective in the history it cites to a side, or to several sides jointly, and the members must meet the constitutional change rule. Once complete, it is final.*

```cddl
fork-payload = {
  0 => hash,                ; the original's agreement in force at the fork
  1 => hash,                ; the original collective
  2 => hash,                ; the original's identity-chain act (genesis or a rotation) its latest acts are bound to
  3 => [* kept-tip],        ; the latest act of every sequence the original keeps under that chain act: the fork's line (N2),
                            ;   the action head it cites (F127)
  4 => [2* side],           ; the sides
  ? 5 => [+ fork-share],    ; the original's stakes, where not divided by default
  ? 6 => [+ [ obligation: hash, sides: [+ uint] ]]  ; every obligation in the history it cites: the side, or the sides jointly, owing it (N13, F127)
}
side       = [ successor: hash, members: [+ hash] ]   ; the collective the side founded first, by its identity (N4), and its members
fork-share = [ agreement: hash, index: uint, [+ share: uint] ]   ; a stake the original holds, and each side's share, in millionths
```

Field 4 names each successor once, never the original. Field 5's shares sum to 1,000,000, one per side, one entry per stake. Field 6 names each obligation once, its sides ascending, each a side the act lists. A fork act breaking any of these is no fork.

**How it is signed.** A fork is signed by one member of the original collective, with their own identity, never the collective's key. Its inside names, in `objects`, the original agreement as chain and predecessor, `[[agreement, agreement]]`, followed by `[agreement, ending]` for each earlier fork or closing of the same collective it names, the act it follows on that chain, each once (F131, IT1). Its chain citations, if any, come after.

**Every member it lists, its own signer included, signs it by a chain signature** (Identity type 16): an act on that member's identity chain, signed with their chain key, naming the fork (F132, U1). A signature act (type 1), or the fork act itself, is no member's signature on it. Each successor a debt is assigned to adds a signature act naming it (type 1), as for terms (N13). *Reading, to confirm (F132): the fork act, an everyday act, is the proposal; a successor's signature stays a signature act, since two endings sharing a successor share that side's members, whose chains order them.*

**A fork is complete when:**

- it is valid under Identity;
- its chain act counts in the original's identity chain, and every kept tip it names holds there (as a rotation's kept ancestry, Identity);
- the agreement it names is the one in force at its line, the original's records before the line counted (rule 72);
- every member it lists is a member whose voice remains at the line (rule 65's voices, the departures registered before it counted), is on one side, and signed it by a chain signature that counts in their identity chain and is not void (U4, below);
- **those members meet the constitutional change rule, counted as rule 65 says** (N1);
- each side's successor is held, and its founding terms, as its genesis declares them, have exactly that side's members as parties, and keep, in its stake in itself (field 7) and its departed members entry (field 22), every departed holder of the original and every member whose voice remains on no side, each at exactly their share of all the original's income (N1, N4; how the members divide the rest is theirs);
- each successor a debt is assigned to signed it, its signature counting by its own rules;
- it hands out everything in the history it cites (F127, F126 item 2), as the next paragraph says; otherwise the fork does not take effect;
- the fork is done (W5, F128): sealed to every member of the agreement in force at its line, or public, as it cites the collective's head by its line; otherwise it does not take effect either.

*"Information runs free": an ending is no exception.* *Client conformance: the members' clients also make a fork public, since creditors and buyers depend on it.*

**What it must hand out.** Field 6 names:

- every obligation of the original in that history, published or not, paid or not, save one that does not bind the collective. An obligation is handed out only if it binds the collective, that is, done (sealed to every member or public, and on its chain, rules 57 and 58) and within its signer's powers, or adopted (rules 76 and 78) (F144); one signed with a grant key is included (F128);
- every obligation an earlier fork handed to the original, where the original's own signature act on that fork lies in that history (F125 reading 4; F126).

**Otherwise the fork does not take effect.** A verifier that does not hold every act that history names cannot tell what it must hand out: for it, the fork is not complete.

**What lies outside that history is void** (the tie rule, "Made before, made after", point 7): an obligation of the original the fork's history does not hold binds no one, and no successor owes it. The one exception is a payee pointer left outside it, which still counts for payments already made to it, as the chain stood for each payment (F181), and for nothing after the fork (F198). *Why: as a stated cost, members could erase, by a fork on an old line, sales they had visibly taken on, against IT2a; failing the fork instead would let one payment block an ending, the trap QG1 and F197 closed for closings.*

**Whether a fork took effect is judged only by what its own history holds** (F199). The departures registered before its line count for its check of the obligations it must hand out; a departure registered after it never changes it. *A fork that left out a debt binding in its history stays incomplete, whatever is registered after it; the members sign a new one that hands the debt out.*

*F127: "a fork cites the action head; everything in that history must be handed out, or the fork does not take effect." A successor owes only what the fork handed it, by its own signature. Publishing has no place in the line, and needs none, since what the line cites is handed out whether published or not; a debt published late was either in the history, and handed out, or outside it, and void (W1). Reading (F199): "everything" is every obligation, published or not, and paid or not, so that whether a fork took effect never changes with what happens after it.* *Reading, to confirm: while the history is not all held, the fork is not complete for that verifier.*

With no stakes written, a member who signs no side keeps each member's equal share, leftovers to the first (rule 9; F125 reading 3).

**The line.** An act of the original counts as made before the fork when it is bound to an earlier link of the original's identity chain than the fork's chain act, or, bound to that chain act, lies in the history the fork cites: a kept tip it names, their ancestry, and whatever acts there cite on the original's chain (F127), a grantee's act so joined included. Every other act of the original counts as made after it.

*"Forks is a constitutional decision" (Nobody, allegedly). Signing with their own identities keeps the key grammar out of it: a key holder refusing to rotate could otherwise block the fork and keep the identity (Q3). "If they are in a position to fork, how they fork is up to them": each side founds its collective first, and the act names it, so no successor needs to claim descent.*

Field 23 of each successor names the original collective, not its agreement, since the agreement may still change before the fork.

**Finality.** **A complete ending is final** (F131, IT1): a later fork or closing of the same collective, one that names it (directly, or through the endings it names), counts for nothing.

An ending names another in its own act's `objects`, or through a signer's own chain (F132, U1): where one member's chain signature on it lies, in that member's identity chain, after the same member's chain signature on the other. *Each identity chain is one line that never branches, so any two ending signatures of one member are ordered, whatever devices the member uses: a later ending sharing a signer with a complete one names it.*

Of the complete endings, the one that counts is the one every other names or is named by, and that names no other such one. Where there is none, the collective is not ended (two endings each naming the other through two signers' chains included).

**Ties among endings.** Only two complete endings sharing no signer and neither naming the other are concurrent, made without knowing each other, and **neither counts**, as any race between equals (F125 reading 4; F131, F132), until a third ending naming both settles it (U2, F132). That third ending is ordered against both, and counts, as a clone of both branches resolves concurrent records (B11); a later one naming it counts for nothing. *A tie no ending could settle would trap the collective for good, against the right of exit.*

**A signature on an old proposal, finished late, counts for nothing** (F132, U4). A member's chain signature on an ending counts for nothing where it lies, in their identity chain, after their own chain signature on another ending of the same collective that names it, in `objects` or through a signer's chain. Once a member signed the ending that replaced a proposal, they can no longer finish it.

**A drafter names the endings they signed** (F132, U4b). A fork or closing is no ending where its signer signed, earlier in their own identity chain than their chain signature on it, another fork or closing of the same collective that it does not name in its `objects` (directly, or through the endings it names there). It is never complete, and its signatures count for nothing, for naming and for U4 alike. *Checkable on the drafter's own chain, unlike "every ending a client holds": without it, a drafter leaving out a proposal they had signed, then the proposal finished late, leaves two endings each naming the other through two signers' chains, which no reading of the chains can tell from a true race.*

*Reading, to confirm: "names" is judged here in two steps, so that no signature both judges and is judged: first by `objects`; then, among the signatures left, through signers' chains too; what an ending names through signers' chains is read from the signatures left after both steps.*

*Stated cost (U4, U4b): where the drafter of the ending replacing an old proposal never signed that proposal and leaves it unnamed, the proposal can still be finished late, by members who signed no ending naming it, with others' earlier signatures: it then counts as the earlier ending, and the one replacing it for nothing, or the two name each other through two signers' chains, and neither counts until a third ending names both (U2). This needs the drafter not to have held the proposal (a true race) or to bypass their client, which names every ending it holds; under a threshold it needs no more than members who signed neither. The late signatures lie on their signers' chains, visible.*

*Client conformance (U4): a member's client signs no ending that another ending it holds names, nor any ending of a collective once it holds a complete one.* *Stated cost (F132): every member signing an ending goes through the chain-key ceremony, so forks and closings are slow and heavy, as rotations are.*

*A complete fork is never undone: a debt published after it was either in the history it cites, and so handed out, or outside it, and void (F127; F125 D1); and a later ending, naming it, counts for nothing (F131).* *Client conformance: a new ending names every ending of the collective its client holds, complete or not.*

### Closing (type 20)

*In short: a closing ends a collective for good. Its members sign it as they would a fork. It can complete only once the collective holds nothing and owes nothing, with some money owed back left open as the rules allow. An absentee's share is left where the collective's own rules sent it.*

```cddl
closing-payload = {
  0 => hash,                ; the collective's agreement in force at the closing
  1 => hash,                ; the collective
  2 => hash,                ; its identity-chain act its latest acts are bound to
  3 => [* kept-tip],        ; the latest act of every sequence it keeps under that chain act: the closing's line
  ? 4 => [+ left-open]      ; money owed back it leaves open (rule 93; QG1, F191; F197):
                            ;   ascending by payment, none twice
}
left-open = [ payment: hash, notice: hash / null, ? holder: hash ]
                            ; the payment owed back, by one receipt or claim of it; the notice sent to its payer (type 24), or
                            ;   null where it is owed to nobody; a holder that outlives the closing, where the collective chose one
```

**How it is signed.** A closing is signed by one member of the collective, with their own identity. Its inside names the agreement as chain and predecessor, `[[agreement, agreement]]`, followed by `[agreement, ending]` for each earlier fork or closing of the collective it names, as a fork does (F131, IT1). Its signer and the other members sign it by chain signatures on their identity chains, as for a fork (F124, N9; F132, U1).

**A closing is complete when:**

- it is valid under Identity;
- its line holds, and the agreement it names is the one in force there, as for a fork;
- **it is done** (W5, F128): sealed to every member of that agreement, or public (client conformance: made public);
- its signer and the members whose chain signatures on it count (F132) are members whose voice remains at the line, its signer among them, and **meet the constitutional change rule**, counted as rule 65 says;
- **the collective holds nothing**: no stake of a latest version of an agreement a verifier holds names it as a holder, by its identity or as null in its own terms, other than in its own income, unless the work's release (rule 55) counted with the collective's consent placed before the line;
- **the collective owes nothing** (F125, D5): every obligation that binds it and lies before its line, that is, in the history it cites (F127; one outside it is void, the tie rule), and every obligation it owes as a fork's successor (rule 91), is fulfilled in full by receipts a verifier holds, or ended by its creditor's release (Money type 4, rule 92). An obligation it owes as a successor is placed by the original's line, not its own (F126 reading 5). **An absentee's money share, left where the collective's own rules sent it, does not block the closing** (new in draft 11, F262, F265; below);
- **no money is owed back from a broken stretch**, whatever the closing's line (rule 93; F189, 7), save what field 4 leaves open as the rules allow (below);
- **the collective is not broken** (F189, 7).

**Money owed back that field 4 may leave open** is of two kinds:

- money owed back to nobody (QG1, F191);
- money owed back to a payer who gave no address, named with a notice of the collective sealed to that payer, or public, whose deadline has passed with no address given (F197).

Every such payment it leaves open, it names. *A holder that outlives the closing and pays the payer when they turn up may be named beside such a payment (F197: a keeper or custodian role, for a cMIP to define; Nobody, allegedly's reference: the Swiss 2nd pillar, where benefits an employee names no destination for go to a holding institution that then seeks the employee). The core allows it and shows it, and requires neither the holder nor any one notice period.* *Stated cost (QG1): a collective may close while visibly owing an anonymous payer who could never have been paid; (F197) and while visibly owing a payer who gave no address and let the notice's deadline pass.*

**An absentee's money share (new in draft 11, F262).** A collective is liquidated by ordinary sales and transfers before its closing (rule 15): its stakes sold or moved by stake transfers, or released, so that it holds nothing. The money share of someone it has nowhere to pay, an absentee, does not block the closing. **New in draft 11 (F265):** where that share goes, and how the absentee is reached or waited for, follows the collective's own rules: its split plan, its absence clause, its terms, written before anyone left. The core checks two things only:

- the closing counts only when the collective holds nothing (above);
- nothing owed vanishes silently: what the collective's rules could not deliver stays visible as an open obligation wherever those rules sent it (its split service, for instance, rule 24), until a valid claim appears: never lost, never taken.

The closing needs no entry for it: field 4 stays for money owed back. No new field; the closing names no stake and moves none. *Batching the sales, transfers and closing together is a client's or a cMIP's convenience, never a second way in the core.* *"Is this core business? Or has core defined how the choice is made by participants?" (Nobody, allegedly, F265).*

A refund past the terms the buyer accepted by paying is ended, and is not a debt that binds the collective: it never blocks a closing (F219).

**After its line**, the collective's keys count for nothing in Agreements, as after a fork, and its open offers are withdrawn, as by a fork (F125 reading 6). *"Let's close… work is either sold or made public domain. Clean" (Nobody, allegedly).* *A collective that cannot pay cannot close: it stays open, abandoned, its debts visible (D5). "Holds nothing" is judged on the latest versions of the agreements a verifier holds (F125 reading 6); a stake sold by a transfer (type 4, its format still open) is not seen, so a collective that sold one is shown as still holding it.*

**Finality and ties.** A complete closing counts only as a fork does (F131, IT1; F132, U1, U2, U4, U4b):

- it is final, and a later ending naming it (in `objects` or through a signer's chain) counts for nothing;
- of two concurrent ones sharing no signer, neither counts until a third names both;
- a signature on it placed after its signer's signature on an ending naming it counts for nothing;
- one whose signer signed earlier an ending it does not name is no closing.

**Judged on its own history** (F143). Whether a fork or closing is complete is judged on its own line and history, as if it were the ending that counts: its own debts, its own departures, its own signatures; never with another ending already in force. A complete ending that names a final one (directly, or through the endings it names), and so counts for nothing, is shown as such ("complete, counts for nothing: an ending it names is final"; F161), and an incomplete one as incomplete. *The answer does not depend on which ending a verifier judged first. A closing's own conditions (receipts that fulfil its debts, "holds nothing") may still change as a verifier comes to hold more acts.*

### Creditor's release (retired type 21)

*Agreements type 21 is retired and never reused. The creditor's release is Money type 4, "Creditor's release" (F126): forgiving a sum only gives up money, "a simple money decision" (Nobody, allegedly), so a collective creditor signs it by its Money lane alone (E2). A release given against future terms is also a deal, an Agreements agreement of its own (rule 92).*

### Decided in text only, not yet in code

These are decided in text only, and not written in code by build step 12b, the step that wrote the open formats (standing offer, work claim, stake transfer, request to a judge, liveness):

- the general contest (OF25 a: a contest names any act, standing as rule 54);
- the keeper record (OF21 a, d: one act per record, public, `{ 0 => [act, signer] }`; the library still takes keepers' logs as stated);
- a rail Module's kind as a Development field (OF26 a, values `0` request rail, `1` push rail binding the commitment, `2` push rail binding nothing; the library still takes push and unbound rails as stated);
- the unit with nothing machine-read (OF27 a);
- the leftover count a buyer of a whole holding inherits (OF19 d).

## Part five: tasks, reasoning and freeze scenarios

### Tasks

- **Split.** A cMIP accepts an amount received and a split plan. It produces payouts that sum exactly, with its declared remainder rule and the evidence for each role share. **It also accepts the agreement and the attempts made for a payment, and produces the receiver for this payment** (F250, rule 17). The receiver is one of the services the signed terms name: field 14's first, or a backup of field 29 only once every attempt before it failed as the cMIP defines failure. It is never an address the owners did not sign. Its fields (field 29's key 1) say what counts as a failed attempt.
- **Condition evaluation.** A cMIP accepts a condition and the acts it refers to, and produces true, false, pending or unknown.
- **Time reference.** A cMIP accepts a point on its reference (for example a block height) and an act, and produces whether the act is before, after, or undetermined.
- **Absence proof** (F172). This is a judicial task (F178): it joins condition evaluation, time reference and anchoring; a judge never handles what it judges, and the chain of judgment applies. A cMIP accepts an abandonment declaration, the clause's parameters and the acts the history of the record or clone using it holds (acts of the party the collective acknowledged or a keeper recorded before the line; F178). It answers whether the party's absence is shown: accepted, refused, or unknown. It may use anchors, liveness acts and acknowledgements as it defines. A declaration it refuses or cannot judge does not count. It is task 14, and a chain of judgment names it as any judge (F182). A client MUST NOT sign terms naming an absence-proof cMIP it does not implement, as for an unknown extension (F182). *Proving absence from time needs a clock the core does not have (F136 to F166 each found a new way around a time check), so the core keeps the members' chosen judgment and leaves proof to modules that compete on it.*
- **Anchoring** is an Envelopes task (F173). Agreements use it for deadlines, time references and claim priority, an agreement naming the anchoring cMIP and the time reference it relies on. *Identity's validity does not depend on it (F63).*
- **Grant limits.** A cMIP defines how a grant's limits are expressed and checked.
- **Work claims.** A cMIP MAY define pre-publication commitments carried by a claim.

### Reasoning

- **Terms plus signatures.** *One signer per act (F17) makes every consent a separate, visible act. An agreement is complete exactly when its rule is met, a party refuses simply by not signing, and nobody owes anything they did not sign for.*
- **Identities, not keys.** *Keys change; promises should not. Naming identities means a rotation never breaks a deal.*
- **Stakes and seats can go to different people.** *A stake is property: passing it changes only who is paid. A seat is trust: the other members chose to share control with that person. A plan can give the stake to an heir and the seat to someone trusted for the job, paid through a position.*
- **Collectives are identities.** *A collective that receives money must sign receipts and rotate like anyone else. Giving it its own keys, held as its members choose, keeps one Identity MIR for everyone. The price is that a threshold signature looks like one signature; where individual consent matters, an area can require visible member signatures. And every grammar leaves a way through that survives the loss of any one key holder. So a dead member never freezes the key, not even a sole holder, whose successor holds an escrowed share: the rule is about the exit, not the number (F96). A collective's member signatures are judged under the rules its own chain declared when the key signed. So leaving is final: a former member's signature never again counts on what the collective signs after its line registering the departure (F100, F109), though it can still complete what the collective signed before it (C1).*
- **Keepers stamp sealed envelopes, and only real ones.** *Proving an act existed before a rotation needs its id and signer, not its content. Keepers that never read keep private deals private; judging content is left to arbitrators and verifiers the parties choose and deliver keys to. A record counts only alongside the act it names, so a bought record of nothing changes nothing.*
- **Protected clauses, one version for everyone** (F121). *A stake cannot be reduced without its holder's signature. But the clause that decides absence, the keeper who judges it and the split service could move the stake in two steps; and an arbitrator, a condition cMIP or an anchoring cMIP chosen after the fact could decide who wins. So in a collective they change only with every member's signature. Keeping, for each party, the version that party signed (F71) would leave two members with no judge they both signed. One version, signed by all, gives every dispute one judge; a holdout makes a standoff, which talking or a fork of the collective settles.*
- **A chain of judgment** (F121). *Unanimity could trap a collective whose judge disappears. Naming in advance who takes over, signed by everyone, replaces a failing judge with no new signature.*
- **A departed holder is paid, never consulted** (F121). *A stake is a share of income, not a say. Whoever keeps a stake after leaving accepts the collective's decisions, and is protected by what company law protects: the stake never shrinks without them, and no term treats it worse than a member's. Keeping their old judges (F71) would leave a split service keeping two sets of terms for one payment.*
- **A fork closes the original, under the constitutional rule, each side founding its successor first** (F121, F124 N1, N4). *When a collective splits, no side keeps its identity, so a lone holdout cannot keep the name, history and pointers while everyone else leaves; every side starts anew, and what was made together still pays everyone who made it. A fork is a constitutional decision: where the founders allowed less than everyone, a member who signs no side keeps their money, as a departed holder of each successor, and loses only the seat. The members sign with their own identities, so no key holder can block the fork by refusing to rotate; the fork act names the successors, so none has to claim descent.*
- **Every debt goes somewhere** (F124, N13; F125; F126; F127). *A fork cites the collective's history and hands out every obligation in it, each successor signing for its own, or it does not take effect; what lies outside that history is void, the ending winning (D1's joint liability withdrawn). A collective's acts are done only once sealed to every member and on its chain, so hiding a debt gains nothing. A collective that owes cannot close, and a debt ends only by payment or its creditor's release, a Money act.*
- **Two chains** (F127). *Hidden debt, late publishing, the missing device, the stray payment and W2 had one cause: MOR has no shared clock, acts are ordered only where one cites another, and a collective's history may branch, so an act could live on a branch nobody builds on. A collective's decisions and its actions are kept on two chains that cite each other. An act within its signer's powers counts once done, citing the head as its signer knew it, and becomes the new head; a decision cites the head it saw. The chain makes acts unmissable; it does not confirm them. Where two acts signed at the same moment never saw each other, only a decision ending powers against an action using them matters, and the ending wins. "A collective has two chains. One is the collective public actions, the other is the decisions by all members within the collective" (Nobody, allegedly).*
- **One thing is to act, one thing is to read** (F126). *Powers can be segmented among members; information runs free among them. What a collective plans can stay with some members for a while; once done, it is a fact touching everyone in it, sealed to every member and on its chain. Negotiation is talk, and may stay secret; the deal it leads to is done like any act (W6).*
- **An act is valid where it is held** (F128). *Naming relays would make an act's validity depend on infrastructure: an operator vanishing or turning hostile could freeze a collective, even its decision to change relays; readers checking one relay could be shown less; an offline verifier could decide nothing. The two chains and the tie rule already answer "before"; a relay is transport.*
- **A grant is a key** (F128). *A grantee who signed with a personal key pointing to a grant would act outside the collective's chains, where no ending could place its acts (G1, G2). A grant key makes the grantee's acts a strand of the collective's own actions chain, so a revocation, an emptied area, a fork or a closing places them like every other act, and the tie rule settles the rest. "Maybe what is missing is a mechanic in the grant" (Nobody, allegedly).*
- **The burden is where the action takes place** (F128, W4). *A buyer can only guess the current version; the seller knows it. On a request rail the seller's request fixes the claim; on a push rail each holder's own record settles it, the only clock MOR has, belonging to the one answering for it. Old versions stay readable forever; they must not be actionable.*
- **Buying is not pure money** (F126). *A work's claim and offer are Agreements, so a purchase names the claim it pays under, and a payment that names none is no purchase: the money goes back to whoever paid. A stray payment exists only because a rail moves money without reading Agreements.*
- **Payment follows the claim** (F124, N14). *The work is the point of payment; the fork changes only who owns it, so the money follows to the new owners' own pointers, and nothing keeps the old service alive.*
- **A judge and the constitution together need both rules** (F122). *Otherwise two members of three could change a judge by adding a one-word change to the constitution.*
- **The split service vouches for the owners' pointer** (F123). *A pointer names rails, never an identity, so the check is that the service's own signed pointer carries every address: a bypass needs the service to sign, publicly, for an address not its own.*
- **Splits are seen by everyone they pay** (F121, Q9). *The core cannot know who owns a service; it can make sure every holder sees every fee taken before their share, and who received it.*
- **A release ends a claim, never a history** (F121, F124 N7). *A work given to everyone still says who made it and who owned it. Every direct owner signs, since a majority releasing would give away the minority's value. Where the owner is a collective, the work is its asset, and releasing it is a business decision taken by its own rules, which every holder of its income feels alike.*
- **Payouts follow stakes, exactly** (F124, N10). *Equal treatment is not a separate test: a split that pays every stake exactly, every fee alike, treats every holder alike; any deviation, either way, is visible.*
- **Deals are everyone's; majorities belong to collectives** (F107). *A deal is as simple as possible while remaining rich: nobody's terms change without their signature, so a party never has to read a rule to know whether they can be outvoted. The price is that one silent party blocks every change, as in everyday deal making. Where many parties or a long relationship need majorities, areas and tiers, they found a collective.*
- **The constitution never freezes** (F105). *The constitution can repair every other tier, so it alone must survive the loss of anyone whose signature it needs. Every such member is covered by an abandonment clause they signed, which can remove their voice, never their stake.*
- **Leaving needs no permission; removal never takes the stake** (Q6). *Anyone can walk out of a collective alone, keeping what they own. A constitution may allow removing a member from the board; it can never force them to give up their shares: "Kicked from the board does not mean being forced to give up shares."*
- **Lanes are computed from the collective's own list, and govern it** (F106). *What the core sees of a cMIP is the result of a task, and the task belongs to a layer. Reading the lane from which task the collective adopted the cMIP for, never from the cMIP's own label, lets every verifier agree; and letting the lane's holders adopt it lets a new rail join the Money lane without a constitutional change. Extensions belong to no task, so they sit in Development; one that reaches into another layer needs that layer's holders too, and binds nothing they did not approve.*
- **A number never freezes a rule** (F105, flaw C). *When fewer voices remain than a rule asks for, all of them together meet it: the same as "every party" already does, and the founders' number wherever enough remain.*
- **Before and after are the collective's** (F109). *With no clock, "before" means before a named act in one named chain. A member may write from any number of devices, forget some, and nobody else can see them all; every patch that asked which of a member's lines an act named opened another gap (Flaws E to L). The collective's own sequence is one every member signed up to and every verifier can follow, so what was done in the collective's name is before or after an event by where it sits there. A departure takes effect at the line the collective draws; a member's signature sits at the act it signs, or where the collective acknowledged it; a grantee's act, signed with its grant key, sits on the collective's own chain (F128).* **What others relied on stands** (Flaw F). *A completed clone stays complete, a paid publication stays paid, a buyer the collective acknowledged or paid is protected, a year of receipts a member signed for the collective survives a declaration of absence. A line drawn without a branch costs the collective that drew it, never a member's forgotten phone.* **Only the collective's acts place** (Flaw G, A2). *An acknowledgement by a friend carries no place in the collective's sequence; a record names exactly the signatures it puts in force, and so does the rotation declaring a constitutional clone (Flaw M), so nothing completes either late and nobody who already left is counted. A reinstatement is the collective's own act, made after the new holders took the area (Flaw N).* **A declaration takes effect at the same line** (Q28, C7). *A voice removed by someone else ends at the collective's line: what a record or rotation already put in force stays, whichever version of the clause the declaration names, and what the absent member signed for the collective before it keeps its signature.*
- **Absence in a deal is judged by one identity** (B19). *A deal has no sequence of its own, so nothing could place several signatures against its line. Parties who must decide together are already a group with a say of its own: they form a collective, which has a sequence, and name it.*
- **A signature speaks for the moment it was made** (Q23). *A member who signed and then left was still acting when they signed; their signature stands, and the rule asks for exactly what it asked before they left, neither less nor more.*
- **Nobody is left holding an area they no longer have, and no hire outlives an emptied area** (Q21, Q22, G2). *A clone that removes a member takes them off their areas, and an area may wait, empty and frozen, for the collective to refit it, keeping the founders' number for the refit (Q26). An empty area is an emergency: its grants end at the line that emptied it, which places every act of their keys like any act of the collective. The new holders clone, one by one and visibly, the hires they want back, by the area's own rule (Q27), and nothing is left undetermined (F128, withdrawing C8).*
- **A judge is never chosen by one lane** (Q15). *The time reference, the condition cMIP and the anchoring cMIP can decide who wins a dispute; they stay judicial, as every member signed them, whoever runs Agreements' other tasks. And a judge never handles what it judges (Q20): a specification chosen as a judge serves no other task and is no extension, however it is named and in a deal as in a collective (Q24, Q25). This removes a conflict of interest and keeps every lane out of the choice of judges.*
- **Nothing passes around the lanes** (Q16, R4). *A specification the collective never listed would let whoever holds its key act around every lane, so in a collective with areas it counts for nothing until a lane adopts it. One that serves two layers is named for the second only with the power over both layers' tasks, and each of its acts counts only when the holders of each lane reaching it have signed it, as their lane's rule asks (rules 60, 63).*
- **Every change is on the record at once** (F106, flaw D, F109). *With no clock, the collective's own sequence is what tells which version governed an act, and who still had a say. A rotation is the chain key's ceremony, kept for changes to who decides; every other change, and every departure, is written with the signing key, at once.*
- **Nobody is added, and nobody is held** (Q11, Q13). *A collective exists only when every founder signed it, and nobody joins it or takes an area without signing. A holder can step down at once; the area waits, frozen, for the collective to refit it, rather than running on without anyone answerable for it.*
- **A split service holds authority, not discretion.** *It is named by grant, bound to the plan, and every incoming receipt or payer's claim must be matched by a split. It can still steal, but never invisibly, and never by naming its own sybil as the referrer: role-share evidence is signed by someone on the other side.*
- **Shares follow stakes, holders follow transfers.** *A plan written in holder names would go stale at the first sale. Written in stakes, it pays whoever the agreement's transfer chain names, and two services compute the same answer.*
- **Leftovers by largest remainder, ties taking turns** (F150, F165). *Every client must compute the same result, so the rule is deterministic. It must also move no money when holders are reordered, since a reorder changes no share and needs no holder's signature: giving leftovers to the first listed holder would let a clone that only reordered holders send every small payment to one of them. Largest remainder keeps every payout within one smallest unit of its exact share. Ties cannot be broken by a hash of an act the divider signs, since the signer chooses its salt and can grind the hash. Taking turns, counted along the service's own receipts, lets history decide, and keeps equal holders within one unit of each other over any run of payments (F165).*
- **Keepers are chosen up front.** *Testimony is only trusted if nobody could pick a friendly witness after the fact.*
- **Many ways to split, one vocabulary.** *A paying client may pay each owner directly; a service may receive and forward at once; or it may pool and let owners withdraw, with every unwithdrawn share an open obligation rather than a number in a private database. Each is a competing split cMIP on the same vocabulary.*
- **Splits belong to owners.** *Money delivers money to a pointer; the owners' agreement decides who gets what. Each payout is a simple payment with its own receipt, so a missing one is as visible as a broken route.*
- **Exact sums, declared remainders, bounded fees.** *Rounding is where money quietly disappears; an unbounded fee line is where it disappears loudly. The plan states the maximum, and the payer's claim fixes what arrived.*
- **Role shares pay for help that is only known at payment time.** *A reposter, a relay or a module earns when its part in a sale is evidenced by someone other than the party paying it, so rewards attach to settlements, never to attention, and never to the service's say-so. That someone is the payer the payment commits to (F193, F194): help known in advance takes a named share.*
- **A stake pays through whoever sells.** *The core cannot tie a sale to a work's stakes without judging which claim is the claim, and a rule that tried would be evaded by re-encoding the file. What it can do is make a sale outside the claiming agreement visible, and give builders every piece needed to refuse or rate such sales. The core is as little intrusive as possible, and carries the bits and bolts for sound solutions on top.*
- **Clone, never modify.** *Nothing in MOR is updated. A clone that meets its rule is the only way terms change, and the parent's history stays intact.*
- **Nobody loses their say by default.** *Protecting the abandonment clause guards one route to removing a party's voice; rewriting the clone rule is another, which would be unguarded (F103). So the rules that decide who decides form their own tier, changed by their own rule, which is everyone unless the founders, all of them, agreed otherwise. What judges the members changes only with all of them (F121). Everything else is operational, and can be handed to the members who run it, as areas: they decide there without asking the rest, and can hire, but never give more than they hold.*
- **The tier follows from the bytes.** *Which power a clone needs is read from which fields it changes, never from what it says about itself, so two verifiers cannot disagree, and a clone cannot slip a constitutional change past as an operational one.*
- **Every version says who brought it into force** (F104). *A field every agreement carries should never be free text that looks binding and is not. A clone's field 4 is a claim anyone can check: this power, these people. A false claim sinks the clone.*
- **A grantor cannot unsay a deal it was paid on.** *Voiding a deal the collective acknowledged or collected on would leave the counterparty with a contest and nothing else. So a revocation never cuts off what the collective itself adopted, wherever its act sits (C6). Deals the collective never touched are the counterparty's risk, which is why a counterparty waits until a later act of the collective cites its deal before performing.*
- **Default made visible.** *The core cannot force anyone to pay or deliver. It can make sure nobody can hide that they did not.*

### Open technical parameters

- Exact formats for every act type other than terms, signatures, grants (fields 5 to 9, and field 2's null, F129), revocations (F128), resignations, records, abandonment declarations, negotiation messages, splits, releases, forks and closings. Also exact formats for terms fields 8, 10 and 17 (split plan, concurrency rule, refund terms). The closing (type 20) is exact (F124).
- Encoding of conditions and grant limits.
- **The nesting bound** (new in draft 11, F254): an agreement may sit at most 8 layers deep among agreements that are parties of agreements (rule 15). A freeze parameter.

### Freeze scenarios

The scenario numbers after each colon are those of the freeze test suite.

#### Making an agreement

- Agreements carry cMIP hashes, one per task: all.
- A party bound only by its own signature; founding terms naming a non-signer's debt stay a draft, the debt shown unsigned (Q11): 3.
- Terms with a bidirectional override shown visibly before signing: 1.
- Negotiation record with acknowledgements, proven complete by negotiation messages, Agreements acts; a text act in the thread is no part of it and carries no acknowledgement (F118); disclosure by publishing keys: 5.

#### Works and stakes

- Stakes, stake rule, transfer naming both chains; the split service pays the buyer after a sale: 1, 3.
- Binding a work; conflicting claims; a co-owner's lone sale visible as outside the claiming agreement: 2.
- A deal made on a vow: the stake in it held by the vow's signer, the platform's fee off the top, the rest hers, the platform raising its fee only with her signature; terms writing the stake held by anyone else invalid; an offer under a deal writing no stake in the vow it sells not counting (reading, to confirm) (new in draft 13, F284): 1.

#### Selling

- Standing offers; key delivery or confirmation as the binding moment; an anonymous refund claimable only by a signature with the key the payment committed to, never by presenting the rail proof (F113): 2, 5.
- One push payment whose holders' receipts name different claims: the receipt naming another claim than the payment's commitment is a wrong receipt, counting for nothing; until the rail answers, the payment is unrecorded (F131, IT3): 1.
- On a push rail, two holders of a song clone their deal: receipts each holder records before its own signature on the new version make a purchase; after it, every holder refunds; until every holder signs its receipt, the payment is unrecorded; on a request rail, the claim the seller's request committed to is bought (F128, W4): 3.

#### Splits

- A deal's version raising a fee, signed by every party, stays a draft until a holder who is no party, paid less by it, signs; a collective's clone lowering a stake's part of the plan, met by its area, stays a draft until each departed holder of that stake signs; a member outside the area is not asked (new in draft 11, F270): 1, 3.
- A plan putting a role share, a named receiver or a metric share in the rest is invalid; the same off the top is valid, and what is left goes to the stakes alone, by their parts and within each by ownership (new in draft 12, F280): 1, 2, 3.
- A declaration of absence passes a holder's stake to its stake successor, by a clone the holder does not sign; one stating outcome 1 or 2 against a holder naming a stake successor is refused; with none named, outcome 1 shares the stake out; no declaration lets a version pay less a holder whose stake stays (new in draft 12, F279): 1, 3.
- Conservation and remainder rule; module fees and an omitted fee visible; maximum fee per payout, and a plan stating none flagged before signing (new in draft 11, F271); split by an attention metric evidenced by a record the identity running the service signs, never by a Module (F116): 1, 2, 7.
- A role share filled by a reposter, with the payer's signed referral; a referral named by the service alone earning nothing; an unfilled role share following the chosen option: 2. A relay's delivery record counting for its role share only when the payer's claim acknowledges it, and only for the object paid for; a claim the relay signs itself confirming nothing (F184): 2.
- A rail Module's role share evidenced by the split service's own receipt naming a rail Module the owners' pointer names; a receipt naming a rail Module the owners' pointer and vault never named evidences nothing (F119): 2.
- A relay's delivery record and a service's use record: each counts only acknowledged by the payer the payment's commitment names, never the payer a receipt names; a verifier not told the relay transport cMIP gives the same answer (F193, F194, QG3): 3.
- A split service that receives a payment and fails to publish a split, or under-reports against the payer's claim, shown as an open obligation; the owners switch services: 2, 7.
- The owners' payee pointer carrying an address the split service's own pointer does not, or a vault entry its own vault does not, is shown as bypassing it by an Agreements client before paying; one whose addresses and vault entries are all in the service's own, or in those of the service its chain names, is ordinary; a Money-only wallet pays it as it stands (F121, F123, F124 P2, rule 16); with no split service, a wallet reading Agreements pays each holder by the stakes, through a collective holder to its own holders (P2): 3.
- A deal's payees grant its split service in its terms: each payee's grant names this agreement by null, the deal lists them in field 14, one per payee; before every party signed, none counts; then the service's receipts in each payee's name for purchases under the deal's claim are backed; one whose payer is the service, one naming a split or carrying a batch, one for another agreement, one another payee received, and any act but a receipt are not; a payee revokes its own grant, its later receipts void, the other payee's standing; a deal listing grants to two services carries none (F129, H4, H5): 3.
- A deal's backup split services are the split cMIP's fields (field 29): for each backup, one grant per payee, grouped by service, as many as field 14 lists, every grant once; a single hash there, a short group, or a group in a collective is invalid; a chain of judgment following the split service (`[ 2 ]`) is invalid; the backup's grant keys count once the deal exists, under the same limit, receipts whose payer is either service refused; a payee's pointer leading to the backup passes the pointer check; a group signed twice by one payee, or naming field 14's own service, leaves the deal carrying none of its grants (F130, H6; F249, F250): 3.
- The split task (F249, F250): a layer pays the designated pointer; a payout to a backup counts only for a payment whose earlier attempts failed as the split cMIP defines failure; the next payment starts again from the first; a payout to an address the owners did not sign is a mismatch whatever is stated; each participant's cut is checked at its flow address or vault: 3.
- One rule for every split service: a collective's split service, named by field 14, receipts with its grant key a fan's purchase under the collective's own claim and a payment following its terms; never a receipt whose payer is the service, a payout the collective is owed by another work's service, nor money under another agreement's claim, even citing an older head than the version naming the service; a second grant to the same identity, named by no field 14, reaches what its kinds name (F130, H7): 3.

#### Time, conditions and judges

- Named time reference: 1, 7.
- Recurring obligation; lapse shown as past its terms: 7.
- A chain of judgment: the condition cMIP answers "unknown", the next in the chain decides, with no new signature; a fallback named elsewhere in the terms is invalid; a silent arbitrator is followed after its period on the time reference, a chain following an identity without a time reference is invalid (F121, Q7, rule 35): 3.
- Terms naming one specification for a judicial task and for another task are invalid (Q20): 3.
- A deal naming one specification for anchoring and payments is invalid; a judge named as an extension, or in field 6 and for a payment task, is invalid (Q24, Q25): 1, 3.
- Terms whose field 6 and field 2's task 10 name different specifications are invalid; naming the same one is valid (Q31): 1, 3.

#### Keepers

- Keepers named by the agreement; keepers check homes; recorded acts survive rotation as disputes; a record of an act nobody holds confers nothing: 1.
- Several keepers with a threshold; a keeper lost, its records surviving in copies: 1.

#### Changing an agreement

- Clone drafts; a deal's clone signed by two of three stays a draft (F107); the concurrency rule withdrawn (step 12b); status quo default; the judicial tier changed only by every member, one version for everyone (F121): 1, 3.
- A clone changing the arbitrator, signed by two of three, stays a draft; signed by all three, it judges everyone (Q3, F121): 3.

#### Public domain

- Public domain: a release every direct owner signs, ending the claim, naming the history and publishing the content key; one with an owner missing incomplete; a release rule allowing fewer, changed by a clone every owner signs (N8); a claim it does not name shown beside it (N12); a collective owner releasing by its own rules across its lanes (N7); a timed release ending the claim at its point, none without a time reference (N11) (F121, F124): 2, 3.

#### A collective

- Collective born from its founding agreement, with a grammar that leaves a way to rotate; a grammar with a single holder and no successor rejected (F96); a dead member's share released by the recovery path: 3.
- A collective founded on a threshold of its founders does not exist; a member added without signing is not added (Q11): 3.
- Founding terms carrying the members' stakes in the collective and its own grant, written null; a deal writing null, or a clone writing the collective's own hash, invalid (F124 S1): 3.
- Acknowledgement recorded by the collective: 3.
- A collective keeps two chains: its actions cite the decision they act under and the heads they join; an act citing nothing on the chain, or an act off it, counts for nothing; a joined act is before a line drawn after the join, an unjoined one after it; an act signed before a record it never cites is judged under the decision it cites (F127, rule 58): 3.
- A record not sealed to every member, nor public, is no line: it puts nothing in force and registers nothing (F127, W3): 3.
- A collective's debt sealed to its creditor alone binds no one; sealed to every member, it binds, wherever it is held; a grantee's act sealed to its counterparty alone is not backed; terms carrying field 25 are invalid (F126, F128, rule 57): 3.
- A collective's negotiation message is talk: it binds nothing, and need not be sealed to every member (F128, W6): 3.

#### Tiers and clones

- A majority clone that rewrites the clone rule without the minority is incomplete under the default constitutional change rule; one whose mark claims the clone rule for it is invalid (F103, F104): 3.
- An area's power: its holder changes the area's fields alone, and grants within it; a grant beyond its issuer's area is invalid; an act beyond a grant's reach is not backed (F103): 3.
- A clone whose mark names a rule it did not meet, or another rule than the one its changes require, is invalid (F104); a clone changing two areas, each area's power met and named, comes into force at once (Q7); an operational clone needs no rotation (Q8): 3.
- A clone changing an area and a judicial clause needs both powers (Q12): 3.
- A version changing the constitution and a judge needs the constitutional change rule and every member for the judge, its mark naming both (F122): 3.
- A mark whose signers are not ascending by hash is invalid (B8): 3.
- The collective's receipts, Money acts, fall in its Money lane, and count only on a rail Module its pointer or vault names, implementing the payment cMIP its terms name (F115); an act of a cMIP its terms name nowhere falls in no lane and, the collective having areas, counts for nothing, whoever signs it, until the lane adopts it (Q16); an act of an adopted extension falls in the Development lane (F106): 3.
- The Money lane's holder adopts a second payment cMIP alone; the clone is recorded at once, without a rotation, and governs the collective's acts from the record on (F106, flaw D); a new rail needs no clone, only a payee pointer the holder signs (F115): 3.
